A.20. Executing Entity information

The project activities will be implemented through the IP and other responsible parties, namely the Department of Forests and Soil Management Authority (NDFM), the Ministry of Energy and Environment and its sub-organizations, the Department of Forestry and Wildlife Conservation (DPC) and the Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC).

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 1. Nepal is a Level Divided Country

Nepal is a country with diverse landscapes defined by variable topography and climate, ranging from subtropical conditions in the lowland plains to sub-cold climates in the steep Himalayan mountain ranges. The high-altitude glaciers of the Himalayas are located at altitudes above 3000 meters.

### 2. The Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 and 8.5 climate scenarios project that temperatures and monsoon rainfall will continue to rise in the future, both increasing lake water volumes and the probability of glacial outburst floods (GLOFs). Areas most at risk are Thulagi, Lower Barun, Luming Taho and Hongj 2 Lakes.

### 3. Since the 1970s, 36 GLOF events have been recorded across 4 potentially dangerous lakes, resulting in catastrophic consequences that have ranked Nepal, alongside Bhutan, as the most economically impacted country globally due to hydropower plants and other infrastructure damage. The economic losses are estimated at $25 million per year for private assets, and disrupt transportation networks, agricultural production, power supplies from hydropower plants and electricity generation. In addition, GLOFs have caused loss of life and property, as well as the spread of diseases spreading widely after flood events. Damages from GLOFs are often irreplaceable for decades, with considerable economic costs. The 1975 Thulagi Lake GLOF is estimated to have cost $20 million in damages.

### 4. The high-risk glacial lakes will result in an economic loss of up to US$109 million annually; although this conservative figure excludes the potential for catastrophic consequences, such as those experienced by Hongj 2 Lake in 2015, which cut off water supply. Because of the Thulagi, Lower Barun, Luming Taho and Hongj 2 Lakes increasingly high risk of breaching, there is a need to take action now to prevent future GLOFs. Without intervention, these glacial lakes could breach at any given moment, adversely impacting vulnerable Nepalese communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

A

## While the Government of Nepal (GoN) acknowledges the need to prioritize investment into proactive GLOF risk reduction strategies, it is also aware that these interventions are limited in scope and scale compared with other climate change adaptation interventions with substantial GLOF risk reduction and adaptation benefits. These limitations are further constrained by the fact that the GoN has not been able to secure adequate funding for these interventions since 2018-2019, with projections to increase in the future. By 2022, central government debt has reached 41% of GDP and is projected to continue increasing. The GoN’s current approach to GLOF risk reduction is largely project-based, which are ineffective and insufficient to build the long-term resilience of Nepal's population. This context necessitates a shift in focus from project-based interventions towards more integrated, system-level approaches.

Without external support, the GoN will remain unable to invest in the necessary GLOF risk-reduction strategies required for building long-term resilience across communities in the country or support major economic sectors facing GLOFs and other extreme events.

### Approximately US$36 million of GCF funds are being requested alongside USD14 million in co-financing to reduce the risk of glacial lake outburst floods (GLOFs) through a combination of proactive measures for GLOF risk reduction. The priority glacial lakes were identified based on extensive consultations and modeling by CGIAR-SCAL, with support from the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM), and the Hindu Kush Himalayan region using remote sensing). The proposed project presents an integrated approach to GLOF risk reduction that includes: 1) a multi-year monitoring program; 2) a community-based early warning system; 3) green and grey infrastructure approaches, hazard monitoring and early warning. This integrated approach will utilize the existing capacity of the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM), as well as the Luhding Tashi Lakes by 3 in order to reduce their risk of breaching. To implement Eco-Disaster Risk Reduction (Eco-DRR) measures, the project proposes to: 1) establish a multi-year monitoring program; 2) develop and implement green infrastructure approaches to mitigate the residual flood impacts from GLOFs; 3) establish and implement early warning systems (EWS) to alert communities of potential GLOF events. The proposed project will also build on existing DHM capacity, as well as the capacity of Nepal's Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) in plan for and respond to GLOFs and floods.

These measures are expected to reduce the risk of GLOFs by 50% over a period of five years. The project will employ approaches engendered through strengthened capacity among institutions and communities. This package of interventions is designed to be implemented within existing institutional frameworks, including the extreme flooding on vulnerable communities in Nepal, ensuring that livelihoods, agriculture and infrastructure are safeguarded from GLOF events. These measures include: 1) a multi-year monitoring program; 2) development of a robust operations and maintenance (O&M) plan, which carried across all physical interventions of the project; 3) establishment of an early warning system (EWS); 4) implementation of green infrastructure conservation (GDCF) – having formally committed financial and human resources to this end - several other systems-level approaches are also being implemented. The proposed project is designed with a strong commitment to the success of interventions and form an integral part of long-term sustainability.

### The combination of interventions described above will reduce flood risks associated with glacial lakes across over 11,000 km², delivering considerable adaptation benefits to the communities located in the Gobind Sagar and

The project is expected to reduce food insecurity for 10 million vulnerable people in Nepal respectively, by reducing the adverse impacts of GLOFs on human life and enabling the population to adapt to climate change. Furthermore, the project will contribute towards addressing the unequal vulnerabilities of different groups of society, including women and indigenous groups. Partnerships will be developed to support the scaling, operation and maintenance of project interventions beyond the lifespan of the project. The project is aligned with Nepal’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2021-2050) and Nepal's Third National Communication (TNC) (to the UNFCCC).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words; approximately two pages)

**Context:**

* **Nepal:** Located in Southern Asia, with an area of about 147,000 km² and a population of around 29 million people.
    * The country is characterized by diverse topography, including the Himalayan mountain range (up to 8,849 m) along its northern border, lowland Terai Plains (less than 70 m) along the southern border with India, the steep Himalayan foothills, and the subtropical plains of the Terai region.
    * Nepal experiences a wide range of climatic conditions at higher altitudes, intermediate cold climates south of the highest peaks, transitioning into tropical regions along its southern borders. The country has a monsoon climate characterized by significant seasonal variation, with hot, wet summers and cool, dry winters. Summer rainfall is strongly influenced by the monsoon, which brings heavy precipitation to most parts of Nepal.
    * **Nepal is one of the Least Developed Countries (LDC), ranked as the 26th poorest nation globally and the second poorest in South Asia with approximately a quarter of the population living below the poverty line. Socio-economic status and exposure of local communities are key factors influencing vulnerability to climate change impacts, particularly in mountainous regions where most people live.**

* **Baseline scenario – Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs):**
    * Glacial lakes form when a glacier erodes rock debris into the lake basin, and subsequently melts, filling the depression with fresh water.
    * These lakes are extremely dangerous as their dam walls are unstable and can be easily breached by earthquakes or heavy rainfall, resulting in devastating Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs).
    * **In Nepal, there are 3,902 glaciers and 2,050 glacial lakes.** Of these, approximately 17% of the lakes are potentially dangerous based on:
        1) characteristics of the lake itself,
        2) the activity of the source glacier, and
        3) the morphology of the surrounding terrain.
    * An example of one of these potentially dangerous glacial lakes is presented in Figure 1.

**Figure 1:** Basic glacial lake structure and the sequence of triggers that cause a GLOF. (Source: Bajracharya et al., 2020; Research Report, ICIMOD and UNDP)

**Page 10**

11. A GLOF occurs when there is a breach of a glacial lake.* In the simplest case, an avalanche entering a lake, causing it to overtop and end the terminal moraine dam, results in a flooding flood. Other triggers include: i) displacement of a terminal moraine (TMR) due to dam settlement or erosion; ii) collapse of a debris-covered moraine; iii) seismic activity; or iv) rapid melting of ice from GLOF rainfall events or from a GLOF upstream lake, which can cause multiple GLOFs and the interdependence of their impacts. It is difficult to quantify the threats they pose because of the catastrophic consequences in Nepal through direct loss of life and secondary impacts such as landslides, erosion, and sedimentation in river valleys. Damages caused by GLOFs in Nepal include destruction of human lives; destruction of houses and whole villages and loss of critical infrastructure such as roads, bridges and hydropower plants. Often, damages are immense and the recovery process is long and difficult for downstream communities. A total of 26 GLOF events have been recorded in Nepal since 1977 and the country has been ranked amongst the top five countries most vulnerable to GLOFs globally, excluding deaths. Figure 4 below is a screenshot from a video showing the severe damage to infrastructure from a GLOF experienced among downstream communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

12. In addition to GLOF, Nepal is susceptible to floods resulting from extreme rainfall events, which have similar but less intense impacts on the population in comparison to GLOFs. During heavy monsoon cloudbursts, flash floods often occur in the valleys and gorges of the Himalayan region. These are particularly hazardous for settlements located at lower altitudes. Furthermore, given the steepness of slopes and the swift flow of water bodies, heavy rains and flash floods often trigger landslides, which can cause significant damage to infrastructure and property.

13. The vulnerability to floods in Nepal was demonstrated by the tragic floods experienced across several districts around September 2015. These floods were triggered by a combination of heavy rainfall and melting snow from the Himalayas. In addition, a further 158 flood events with the Nepal Red Cross reporting damages to at least 3,500 households (approximating approximately 26,000 people) in 2015. These floods caused damage to infrastructure such as roads, bridges, water supply systems, communication, transportation and electricity services, particularly in rural areas where such services take time to recover.

## Climate rationale

### Climate change impacts on glaciers and GLOFs

14. As glaciers retreat, the surface area and volume of a glacial lake at their base increases, creating a heightened risk of GLOF. The RCP 8.5 scenario projects an increase in glacier volume between 2006 and 2035 by approximately 7% to 21%, respectively, with more extreme increases under the RCP 8.5 scenario, reaching 1.0°C/yr by 2045, 2.8°C/yr by 2095, and 4.6°C/yr by 2100 compared to pre-industrial levels (IPCC AR5). The volume of glacial lakes in Nepal has increased between 40% and 65% since 1970, with the largest increases expected for glaciers located at lower altitudes.

15. In addition to accelerating glacier retreat, other climate change related factors contribute to the increased risk of GLOFs including: more intense and frequent rainfall and rainfall-induced floods, which increase the risk of rainfall-triggered landslides; increasing temperatures which accelerate glacial melt and snowmelt, increasing the volume of water in glacial lakes; increasing precipitation which increases the likelihood of flood and landslides triggering a GLOF event; increased melting rates of glacial lakes lowers the likelihood of a breach as a result of the elevated pressure on moraine or ice dams; reduced thickness of the ice dam due to increased meltwater volume at the top of the dam with the increased lake volume, thereby increasing the likelihood of overflow.

16. In Nepal, glacier areas have decreased by 24% in the 33 years between 1977 and 2010. Much of the resulting meltwater has settled in glacial lakes. Indeed, evidence from several glacial lakes in Nepal indicates that they have been expanding since the late 1980s. The area of these lakes has increased by an average of 35% between 1976 and 2004 (from a surface area of 0.7 to 2.94 km² since 1990, while Lower Barun Glacier Lake in Sankhuwasabha district has increased in area from 0.8 to 3.5 km²). The volume of these lakes has also increased by an average of 60% between 1976 and 2004 (from a volume of 0.2 to 1.9 km³ from 1990 to 2002) (See Section 4.4, Annex 2 for more details on the impacts on these four high priority glacial lakes). These increases combined with other characteristics of the lake...

# Impacts of GLOFs and potential for risk reduction

17. Given the magnitude of GLOF events, their impacts on settlements, roads, bridges, hydropower projects and other infrastructure are likely to be significant, especially in the most frequent low- and medium-level floods. Flood models (e.g., RCP2.6) indicate that these estimates do not include potential losses to hydropower plants and the subsequent economic consequences of reduced electricity generation capacity. The magnitude of GLOFs is expected to increase with climate change. However, these risks can be reduced significantly through the implementation of appropriate adaptation measures. These measures are grouped into three categories: (i) reducing flood risk by reducing their total volume and subsequently reducing the GLOF magnitude; (ii) constructing check dams and other structures that reduce the flow rate of water in the channel; and (iii) implementing early warning systems (EWSs) such as brush lighting and reforestation, to reduce residual flood impacts; and (iv) establishing and implementing land-use planning measures to reduce damages and protect lives. (See Section 1.3 of Annex I for two more details on option assessed)

18. It is estimated that, by manually reversing the Thulug, Lower Bannu and Lohar Talsi Yaks III case, and the Hongri-2 landslide, the total volume of water released from the GLOF events in these three cases (and associated with a GLOF can be reduced by US$ 45.3–86.9 billion) based on four different probability models of a GLOF event occurring at any given time. The potential economic benefits and avoided costs associated with the scenarios (Scenario I – which used historical records from 1982 to 2017 to calculate the percentage chance of a given event occurring; Scenario II – which accounted for the magnitude of a GLOF as well as the return-period for an event of a given magnitude, and Scenario III – which accounted for the magnitude of a GLOF as well as the return-period for an event of a given magnitude) are shown in Table 1. The economic benefits associated with these interventions include infrastructure and livelihood losses in the potential flood areas, as well as the potential loss for oil. The details of the costs associated with these interventions include the cost of construction of check dams, other structures to reduce flood risk, and land-use planning measures. The economic benefits are calculated by subtracting total losses and intervention costs with project benefits (including discount factors), the Net Present Value (NPV) of the discounted cash flow for the Return Period Based Probability Scenario I is US$ 135 billion; NPV of the discounted cash flow for the Return Period Based Probability Scenario II is US$ 2.4 trillion, and NPV of the discounted cash flow for the Return Period Based Probability Scenario III is US$ 809 billion. Under both scenarios, the interventions save 1,352,400 lives (Scenario I), 67,000 lives (Scenario II) and 1,352,400 lives (Scenario III). The return on investment ratios are 4.9 and 8.0 for Scenarios I and III respectively. Thus, the implementation of these measures will clearly substantially reduce flood risk in the GLOF-prone areas and contribute to economic security especially in rural communities.

### Complementary with existing projects, programmes and initiatives

19. In recent years, numerous projects, programmes and baseline investments have been identified and being funded by various agencies. These include the following: (i) The Learning lessons have been used to inform these initiatives have been incorporated into the proposed project's design to replicate successful adaptation techniques; (ii) A number of existing projects, programmes and baseline investments are complementary with the initiatives identified during project development. An analysis of identified projects was last conducted to prevent the duplication of efforts in the proposed project. The following table lists some of these projects, programmes and baseline investments specifically filling gaps and areas not yet covered by baseline investments. Achieving complementarity between the proposed project and existing projects, programmes and baseline investments will ensure that monitoring global glacial lakes is funded beyond the project's lifespan.

* **Hydraulic modeling used in 5-step approach:** (i) Branch-Average Approach; (ii) Branch-Hydraulic Modeling; (iii) Floods-Forecast Branch Flow; iv) Branch-Flow Forecasting; v) Branch-Flow Forecasting with Snowmelt
* **Cameras were assessed based on GLOF development modeling, flood velocity and depth as the primary parameters. Damage risks are estimated using a combination of hazard and vulnerability assessments. The assessment was conducted for existing infrastructure in gorges (i.e., roads, bridges, hydropower plants). Damage estimates were based on the calculation of the impact of multiple scenarios.**
* **The NPV value of losses can be attributed to the exposure of hydropower installations to the GLOF impacts and the associated loss of revenue from electricity generation.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL 3.0 | PAGE 11

## Initiatives (inspired by the parameters discussed above) are presented below and include a description of how the proposed project will complement existing initiatives.

### The Nepal Climate Change Support Programme II (NCCSP II) (2019–2024, UK Aid; US$1.3 million)
NCCSP is the third phase of the GCF-funded NCCSP I (2015-2018). It aims to support 26 local governments in 14 districts in Nepal, with support from UNDP. In the first phase (NCCSP-I), UNDP provided technical assistance and capacity building for the government to implement national adaptation plans (NAPs) and national strategies on climate change. The second phase of NCCSP II will expand this initiative by implementing local adaptation programs of action (LAPA) and 20% on strengthening institutional capacity and national strategies on climate change. The third phase of NCCSP II will be implemented in the second phase of GCF-II project (the proposed project). The Phase I and Phase II of the project are bridged by the GEF-funded Nepal Climate Change Resilience Project (NCCRP) (2015-2024, GEF; US$3.8 million). NCCSP II will support 26 local governments in 14 districts to implement multiple Climate Resilient Development Projects (CRDPs). The NCCSP ITE supports the implementation of CRDPs and provides technical assistance for the development of LAPAs. The GEF project will expand on this network. The proposed project will complement this initiative by addressing community resilience through the establishment of a climate-resilient livelihoods program in the Saptakoshi River Basin, including wetlands and settlements (Output 3). Expanding these initiatives from districts located in neighboring provinces to those located in the target area will further expand the proposed project to fill gaps in country coverage and complement the activities implemented under NCCSP I.

### Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal (2015–2024, GEF; US$3.8 million)
This GCF project aims to improve the resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin by establishing a climate-resilient livelihoods program through integrated watershed management practices and adaptation measures. There is strong complementarity with the proposed project that this project has the same target area as the proposed project, and it will be implemented under the GEF project. The project will address the risk of areas needing further adaptive action to complement the adaptation measures supported by the ongoing project. Additional climate-resilient livelihoods activities will be introduced in the upper Saptakoshi River Basin, including Dhudhkeshi, Mungo, Likhu and Sukashati Canyon within the lower Saptakoshi basin. An additional focus of this project is to integrate climate change adaptation into existing government policies and plans. The GCF project will incorporate ecosystem-based climate change adaptation approaches into government policies and plans. And then both projects will work together in a coordinated manner, with the GEF project providing technical capacity building and knowledge management-based interventions of this GCF project to enhance the impact of the proposed project.

### Developing Climate-Resilient Livelihoods in the Vulnerable Watersheds in Nepal (2020–2024, GEF; LDCF, US$2.9 million)
The objective of this project is to develop climate-resilient community livelihoods through integrated watershed management practices by establishing the necessary policies and institutional frameworks. The project will introduce climate-resilient livelihoods activities in the upper Saptakoshi River Basin, including Dhudhkeshi, Mungo, Likhu and Sukashati Canyon within the lower Saptakoshi basin. An additional focus of this project is to integrate climate change adaptation into existing government policies and plans. The GCF project will incorporate ecosystem-based climate change adaptation approaches into government policies and plans. The LDCF and GEF projects will work together in a coordinated manner, with the GEF project providing technical capacity building and knowledge management-based interventions of this GCF project to enhance the impact of the proposed project.

### Saptakoshi High Dam Project (ongoing; Government of Nepal; US$28.3 million)
The Saptakoshi High Dam is a multipurpose project on the Saptakoshi River of Nepal. The project's primary focus is flood control and irrigation, with secondary benefits including power generation, water supply, and floodplain management. Catastrophic flooding resulting from the erosion of the river's embankments in 2008 catalyzed this project's efforts, including the development of a comprehensive flood risk management strategy for the Saptakoshi River Basin. The improved production of electricity and irrigation potential. The improved control of downstream flooding under this project will complement the current flood risk mitigation measures implemented as part of the proposed project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## B (a.) Theme of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

### Problem statement

To date, 26 GLOF events have been recorded in Nepal since the 1970s. The accelerated melting of glaciers caused by climate change has led to an increase in the frequency and magnitude of these events. These events are particularly severe for women under future climate scenarios. The impacts of GLOFs on the largely indigenous communities living downstream from the glacial lakes are significant, as they cause loss of life, property damage, displacement, and livelihood losses and wildfires, as well as causing significant damage to infrastructure and private assets, while also disrupting agricultural activities in the region. These GLOF events also have considerable economic impacts, where a single GLOF has the potential to destroy entire villages and communities, leading to loss of income from agriculture and other livelihood activities in the country.

### Where implemented

Efforts to implement the impacts of GLOFs have been reactive and project-based, limiting the capacity to build resilience. In addition, the current level of the synthetic hydrologic system is insufficient to meet the requirements for early warning systems (EWS) and risk reduction measures. The Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) has recently updated climate risk monitoring and national early warning system capacity under the World Bank-funded Climate Resilience Project (CRP). However, these efforts have not been fully integrated into the GLOF risk in Nepal effectively. These limitations have persisted as the project did not include monitoring systems at glacial lakes, which are critical for early detection of GLOFs and to provide timely information on potential GLOF events.

### Preferred adaptation solution

There is an urgent need in Nepal to shift away from a reactive, project-based response to GLOFs towards building sustainable resilience. This can be achieved by integrating the reduction of risk from GLOFs with other climate change mitigation measures both up- and downstream. This integrated approach to the reduction of GLOF risk and the implementation of adaptation strategies is expected to provide multiple benefits for Nepal:

1. **Upstream:** (i) Improved glacier monitoring; (ii) Early warning systems at glacial lakes; (iii) Downstream, watershed-level interventions such as reforestation and forest cover management as well as flood protection measures.
2. **Downstream:** (i) Improved early warning systems for GLOFs; (ii) Watershed-level interventions (like lowering and reducing the release impacts of a GLOF event on sustainable livelihoods); (iii) Community-based disaster risk reduction strategies, including evacuation plans and community-based response strategies.

This integrated approach to climate change adaptation is expected to provide multiple benefits in Nepal. It will help to reduce the vulnerability and exposure of communities living downstream from glacial lakes, the consequent trigger risk and subsequent magnitude of GLOFs are expected to continue to rise as a result of continued glacier retreat.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Barriers to the preferred solution

Several barriers including financial and technical barriers to the preferred adaptation solution have been identified for Nepal in order for the preferred solution above to be successfully implemented.

### Barrier 2 — Limited technical knowledge and institutional capacity, including watershed-level approaches in GLOF risk reduction. The GoN has: (i) insufficient data and expertise related to GLOF risk reduction and management; (ii) limited experience with watershed-level approaches in GLOF risk reduction; (iii) inadequate institutional arrangements for GLOF risk reduction; and (iv) inadequate mechanisms to ensure the coordination of efforts across different agencies. These barriers are exacerbated by limited collaboration and coordination between different GoN agencies, as well as a lack of inter-agency communication and information sharing.

### Barrier 3 — Limited access to current, reliable and timely climate risk information and early warning technology. There is a general lack of hydro-meteorological monitoring networks in Nepal, which constrain the implementation of integrated watershed-level approaches for GLOF risk reduction. This is particularly evident in the lack of vulnerability maps, technical field surveys, hazard and risk information systems, and other data that are critical to understanding the risks associated with GLOFs.

### Barrier 4 — Limited community awareness and skills for GLOF disaster reduction actions. Knowledge of basic climate change adaptation measures at the community level is limited – especially among the different groups living in areas exposed to GLOFs and other extreme climate events throughout Nepal. This is linked to the limited adaptive capacity of communities, which is exacerbated by inadequate investment in DRR interventions in the exposed areas, increasing the risk of damage from GLOFs and other extreme weather events.

### Barrier 5 — Limited access to financing for GLOF risk reduction measures. The GoN has limited financial mechanisms to sustain risk reduction efforts over time, particularly for downstream flood management. Moreover, investment in risk reduction measures is hindered by limited opportunities for private sector involvement due to the lack of a clear regulatory framework and policy environment.

### Barrier 6 — Limited awareness among communities about the importance of investing in GLOF risk reduction. This constrains the GoN’s ability to attract and secure the necessary investments for GLOF risk reduction, particularly from the private sector. The GoN also faces challenges related to the lack of a clear regulatory framework and policy environment for programme financial requirements for risk reduction measures.

### Barrier 7 — Limited awareness among communities about the importance of investing in GLOF risk reduction. This constrains the GoN’s ability to attract and secure the necessary investments for GLOF risk reduction, particularly from the private sector. The GoN also faces challenges related to the lack of a clear regulatory framework and policy environment for programme financial requirements for risk reduction measures.

### Barrier 8 — Limited awareness among communities about the importance of investing in GLOF risk reduction. # Project Approach

## Goal Statement

34 IF the levels of the Thuring, Lower Basin, Luming Taro and Hengzi 2 global lakes are reduced and nature-based solutions are sensitive and early warning systems are installed in downstream catchments, then the resilience, disaster preparedness and adaptive capacity of downstream communities will be enhanced. BECAUSE the likelihood of GLOFs (Glacial Lake Outburst Floods) will be reduced and communities will be better prepared to avoid and recover from residual flood risks.

## Theory of Change

35 The proposed project seeks to enhance Nepal’s resilience to climate-induced hazards, specifically glacial lake outburst floods (GLOFs) and landslides. This will be achieved by strengthening institutional capacity to integrate climate risk into planning and development, implementing disaster risk reduction measures, and improving early warning systems. By reducing the likelihood of GLOF occurrence through improved monitoring and management of exposure to these extreme events, the project aims to safeguard lives, livelihoods, and critical infrastructure while promoting long-term resilience.

36 A key outcome of the project is ensuring that local and national-level institutions adopt an integrated approach to planning and development that incorporates climate risk and hazard information. Strengthening institutions and technical capacity will be achieved through training programs, policy development, and the integration of climate risk into planning and development processes (Output 1). This will also include developing a framework for disaster risk reduction and investment plans, ensuring proactive and informed decision-making (Output 1). The project will facilitate collaboration between local communities and national-level institutions to improve early warning systems and risk management, creating a foundation for long-term resilience.

37 At the community level, the second project outcome will be the reduced exposure of vulnerable communities to GLOFs through improved monitoring and management of residual flood risks. This will be achieved by improving hazard monitoring, the generation and dissemination of early warnings (Output 1), and the implementation of risk reduction measures such as drainage systems, check dams, and other mitigation strategies. An early warning system that provides timely and actionable information allows for effective preparedness and response. Furthermore, the project will support community-based disaster risk reduction initiatives through the use of climate disaster risk reduction (CdRiR) strategies, mitigating the probability and intensity of GLOFs, reducing downstream flood risks.

38 To achieve these outcomes, the project will undertake a range of activities. Institutional capacity will be strengthened through training programs, policy development, and the integration of climate risk considerations into planning processes. This will include developing a framework for disaster risk reduction and investment plans, improving data collection and analysis, and developing user-friendly climate information products. Early warning systems will be improved by enhancing monitoring capabilities, generating early warnings, and implementing community-based preparedness initiatives, such as drills, awareness campaigns, and response planning. Risk reduction measures will be implemented through the use of climate disaster risk reduction strategies that address solutions to manage residual flood risks, ensuring a comprehensive approach to disaster risk management.

39 The project also addresses critical barriers that have hindered Nepal’s ability to implement effective adaptation measures in the face of climate change. These include limited institutional capacity and resources, weak coordination among relevant institutions, the lack of tangible risk reduction measures to be addressed by communities, and insufficient policy frameworks for GLOF risk reduction. By addressing these barriers, the project will catalyse the development of policy and financial mechanisms for GLOF risk reduction, engaging private sector actors in disaster risk management activities, and promoting community-based solutions. The process of ownership will be followed through participatory approaches, ensuring that local populations are actively involved in planning and implementation.

40 The success of the project is contingent on several assumptions. It assumes that government agencies and stakeholders at all levels will provide adequate resources for the project’s implementation and effective collaboration. The project is also based on the assumption that lowering global lakes will reduce the likelihood and intensity of GLOFs, thereby reducing downstream flood risks. Additionally, it assumes communities will understand and adhere to the warnings and response measures designed for their specific locations. Potential risks include financial or institutional constraints that could

### Overcoming these barriers and reinforcing key assumptions, the project will contribute to a transformative shift in how Nepal manages climate risks.

The integration of climate risk considerations into institutional decision-making, the strengthening of disaster risk management systems, and the development of early warning systems will reduce disaster impacts and build long-term resilience. Through these interventions, Nepal will move from a reactive disaster response model towards an integrated approach that protects vulnerable communities and key economic sectors from the growing risks posed by GLOFs and extreme flooding events.

# B3. Project/programme description (max: 250 words, approximately 5 pages)

## Project objective

46. The overall aim of the proposed project is to safeguard the lives and livelihoods of people and their assets by reducing the risk of Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and related hazards in Nepal. This shall be achieved by implementing a climate-resilient integrated approach to GLOF risk reduction, including early warning systems for floods and landslides. Specifically, direct investments from the GCF combined with co-financing from DFID, SDFC, MoES, and other donors will support: i) strengthening the technical and institutional capacity of the GoN and local communities; ii) reducing impacts on vulnerable communities; iii) promoting the adoption of GLOF early warning systems (EWS) measures in high-risk areas; iv) improving the resilience of hydropower infrastructure to GLOFs; v) enhancing the technical and institutional capacity of the GoN and local communities to implement GLOF risk reduction and flood management measures. The implementation of this project will result in the adoption and implementation of a climate-resilient, integrated approach to GLOF risk reduction and flood management across Nepal.

47. This project is expected to contribute significantly towards achieving the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): i) SDG 1: No Poverty; ii) SDG 2: Zero Hunger; iii) SDG 3: Good Health and Well-being; iv) SDG 6: Clean Water and Sanitation; v) SDG 7: Affordable and Clean Energy; vi) SDG 9: Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure; vii) SDG 10: Reduced Inequalities; viii) SDG 13: Climate Action. The project will also contribute to the GCF’s portfolio shift objective of increased climate-resilient sustainable development.

48. The project activities that will be implemented under Output 1 are described below, with further details on these activities (including the associated sub-activities and deliverables) presented in Section E.6 of this Funding Proposal.

## B

### Activity 4. Build and support the implementation of this Output through the DRM Field Office, which will assist in the implementation of project activities (Sub-activities: 1.1.1, 1.1.2 and 1.2.2).

#### 40. The budget for Output 1 is USD 3,251,876, including a combination of USD 3,206,993 GCF finance and USD 44,883 from the DRM Field Office.

#### 41. This activity will support the implementation of this Output by building on existing capacity within DRM to strengthen its capacity for hazard mapping and prioritizing/designing GLOF risk reduction strategies. This funding will complement other sources of financing (e.g., GCF) that are already in place, ensuring that all planned interventions are mainstreamed into the day-to-day processes of DRM. Moreover, GCF funds will be used to engage the private sector and civil society organizations (CSOs) with the aim that it can catalyze sustainable finance for the maintenance of GLOF and flood risk information services.

#### 42. Activity 1.1. Strengthen the knowledge base and technical capacities for improved climate research and risk reduction strategies in Nepal.

##### 43. There is an urgent need within DRM for the development of an in-house monitoring database for glaciers and global lakes, as well as a GIS database to support the implementation of GLOF risk reduction strategies. The provision of training and capacity building are required to catalyze the development and management of this address. To this end, Activity 1.1 will focus on strengthening the knowledge base and technical capacities for improved climate research and risk reduction strategies in Nepal.

##### 44. First methods and SOPs for the inventory and development of a spatial digital database (GIS) of glaciers and global lakes will be developed and institutionalized within DRM. The development of this GIS database is the first and most crucial step towards building an effective monitoring system for risks through data storage and remote sensing analysis.

##### 45. The SOPs for the GIS database should focus on:
- data collection, management and dissemination;
- database management;
- methodology and procedures for analysis and quality control, incorporating hydrodynamic models that account for the cascading hazards (including not only climate hazards, but other natural hazards such as landslides and earthquakes) affecting GLOF risk;
- reporting and publication formats; and
- online GIS data sharing and dissemination of reports.

##### 46. The establishment of the GIS database will be supported by geophysical investigation, as well as topographic and bathymetric surveys. SOPs for the investigation and surveys should focus on:
- pre-investigation and survey equipment check and survey logistics;
- human resources management;
- expedition planning and field work;
- Monitoring and Evaluation protocols; and
- report generation and publication formats for bathymetric, DGPS, ERT and other surveys.

##### 47. The status of the glaciers and global lakes will be updated by DRM every five years to account for changes in climate and geophysical conditions. The current status is not sufficient as it was last updated in 2013. A recent study by ICIMOD reported that updating the status every five years is sufficient. Several other studies have also recommended this frequency of updating. However, there are several challenges associated with updating the status, including:
- lack of resources for fieldwork;
- lack of trained personnel to carry out the fieldwork; and
- lack of funding.

##### 48. The SOPs will be updated by DRM every five years to account for changes in climate and geophysical conditions. The current status is not sufficient as it was last updated in 2013. A recent study by ICIMOD reported that updating the status every five years is sufficient. Several other studies have also recommended this frequency of updating.

##### 49. Technical staff from DRM will be trained on regular/periodic monitoring for risk prioritization, as well as how to use the GIS database and its associated tools. Established methods and tools will be robust and replicable on a national scale for operational monitoring and DRM is determined to continue providing training to be used in the Remote Sensing and GIS and Shore Survey and

## B

### Sub-activities:

#### 1. Develop methods and SOPs for inventory and development of spatial digital databases (GIFs) of glaciers and large lakes including geophysical investigation, as well as topographic and bathymetric surveys.

#### 2. Train staff in DMI's Remote Sensing and GIS and Snow Survey and Glacier Lake Depressions on remote sensing and monitoring techniques to assess the impacts of climate change on extreme floods, landslides, mudflow and soil erosion hazards and risks, using a combination of remote-sensing and satellite imagery.

#### 3. Introduce methodology and standard operating procedures (SOPs) for integrated GLOF risk reduction planning and management in Nepal.

#### 4. Train technical staff in DMI's Remote Sensing and GIS and Snow Survey and Glacier Lake Depressions on remote sensing and monitoring techniques to assess the impacts of climate change on extreme floods, landslides, mudflow and soil erosion hazards and risks, using a combination of remote-sensing and satellite imagery.

### Activity 12. Develop public-private partnerships for sustainable investment in GLOF and flood risk information services.

#### 54. This project activity will contribute to improving GLOF and flood risk reduction in Nepal by (i) improving DMI's production capacity, (ii) increasing the dissemination of climate change information to hydropower and tourism sectors through public-private partnerships, and (iii) enhancing private-public sector partnerships to encourage future investment into GLOF risk reduction projects. The project will achieve this objective by:

- Expanding the dissemination of climate change information to hydropower and tourism sectors via the public through the DMI website, phone messaging, radio, television and tailored bulletins.
- Using social media platforms such as Facebook, Twitter, and YouTube to disseminate climate change information in a more interactive manner. These platforms are also used for targeted communication with specific focus areas and disseminated only when an external potential extreme event has been identified. While DMI produces climate change information on a regular basis, there is a need to expand the provision of tailored climate risk information to the hydropower and tourism sectors which will be facilitated through public-private partnerships.
- Expanding the dissemination of climate change information via the generation, recording, and maintenance of hydroclimatological databases to disseminate the most updated climate change information in a more interactive manner. This will also include the expansion of the DMI website for hydropower and tourism which are particularly vulnerable to GLOFs. The use of the information by the targeted sectors will be encouraged through public-private partnerships.
- Expanding the dissemination of climate change information via the generation, recording, and maintenance of hydroclimatological databases to disseminate the most updated climate change information in a more interactive manner.

#### 55. The sustainability of GLOF risk reduction strategies in Nepal will be supported by the establishment of public-private sector partnerships (PPPs) to formalize and institutionalize long-term agreements focused on the sustainable uptake of GLOF risk reduction measures, as well as the implementation of EWS systems. Private sector actors in the hydropower, tourism and other sectors are encouraged to participate in PPPs with DMI. The private sector will be invited to provide their consultations their commitment as partners, including Independent Power Producers' Association, Nepal (IPPA), Tourism Industry Association of Nepal (TIAN) and others. These partnerships will also include the financial contributions outlined in Section C. The initial co-finance investments from IPPA and NTB will include the provision of the necessary technical expertise to support DMI's efforts on GLOF risk reduction strategies, including climate change adaptation and hazard specific advice for hydrotourism, travel agencies, porters, hotels and local businesses. Ongoing engagement with private sector actors is also encouraged through PPPs which will be established in response to interventions – including the physical lake lowering and Eco-DRR measures, as well as the improved climatic information services provided by DMI.

#### 56. The project will support the implementation of GLOF risk reduction measures upstream of hydropower dams, which serve to not only reduce flood risk, but also provide co-benefits such as water quality improvement and reducing a win-win situation where the more severe risk from a catastrophic GLOF is reduced through lake lowering.

communities to the increased risk of being impacted by a GLOF and the resultant flooding. To address these constraints, a study was recently commissioned by UNDP for the "Identification of required optimum number of Hydrological and Meteorological Stations in Nepal" (see Annexure 1). The proposed project will establish a network – in terms of numbers of stations required – to detect and monitor hydrological and meteorological changes that could lead to GLOF events. This will enable early warning systems, thereby providing the information of early warnings to downstream communities to enable them to respond timely to impending GLOFs.

50. Output 2 of the project will fill these gaps in two ways. First, hydrological and meteorological stations will be installed across high-risk GLOF watersheds (see Annexure 1). Second, a network of early warning systems will be established which will be complemented by training workshops targeting DHM staff to strengthen their capacity to: (i) collect, process and interpret data from the new hydrological and meteorological stations; (ii) disseminate early warnings through information products, including easily interpretable infographics for local communities. This will enable DHM to relay timely and accurate early warning messages to downstream communities in a timely manner. The network of existing hydrological and meteorological stations. The data from these stations will be housed on DHM's existing server infrastructure. In addition, the capacity of DHI staff at the project sites (e.g., Chitwan, Kaski, Manang, Sindhupalchowk and Gorkha) to GLOFs will be enhanced through a combination of capacity-building and information sharing activities. The proposed network of early warning systems is illustrated in Annexure 2.

51. Further details on these activities, including the associated sub-activities and deliverables are presented below. These activities will be implemented under Sub-Activity 2.1 (Observation) and Sub-Activity 2.2 (Early Warning). The project will ensure their alignment with key government priorities laid out in the GFM's Fifth Phase Plan (2019/2020 – 2025/2026).

Support for the implementation of this Output will be provided by: (i) DTM through the PUI and DHM Field Office, assisting in the implementation of the project activities (Sub-activity 2.1.1 and supporting Operation and Maintenance of the early warning systems); (ii) UNDP through the PUI and DHI Field Office, providing support to the provision of communication, data processing and EWS software to strengthen the technical capacity of DHM staff; (iii) NORMRA by assisting with the development of the early warning system (Sub-activity 2.1.3); (iv) UNDP through activity Sub-activity 2.2.1); and v) NORRMA by assisting with disaster preparedness and response training (Sub-activity 2.2.4) and information sharing.

The budget for Output 2 is USD 10,589,961 including a contribution of USD 5,532,609 GCF finance and USD 1,854,300 co-finance from DHRM, NTB, IPUUN and DPH. GCF finance will be used to cover the cost of establishing hydrological and meteorological stations in high-risk GLOF watersheds (Sub-activity 2.1.1) and to strengthen the technical capacity of DHM staff to produce and disseminate early warnings, as well as supporting the operation and maintenance of the early warning systems (Sub-activity 2.1.3). The co-finance from DHRM, NTB, IPUUN and DPH will cover staff costs and provide the necessary processing equipment for the EWS. The NORMRA will provide technical assistance to develop the early warning system software and the UNDP will provide data for effective disaster preparedness. Finally, private sector co-financing from IPUN and NTB will cover the cost of land acquisition and construction of the early warning systems.

Activity 2.1: Improve observation network density in GLOF watersheds to strengthen the technical capacity of DHM for the monitoring of climate hazard risk and risk

Use existing hydrological and meteorological stations will be installed across high-risk GLOF watersheds to fill gaps in the current observational network. These gaps include imperative monitoring stations, poor coverage in certain areas, and a lack of data on key parameters such as temperature, atmospheric pressure, humidity, horizontal wind and snow depth data.

6 base stations at Pangchogho, Jayagram Ghil, Mahal Hill, Tumlingtar (Chitwan), Chataras and Muguin (Kaski) will be installed to provide a more comprehensive network of monitoring stations. This will improve monitoring that will produce high quality hydrological, sediment and water quality data.

24 additional monitoring stations will be established in the Dhauliganga River Basin (5 in the Dhauliganga Basin, 5 in the Manangyati Basin, 5 in the DudhKoshi Sub-basin and 10 in the Arun Sub-basin) to collect temperature, atmospheric pressure, humidity, horizontal wind and snow depth data.

### Activity 2.3: Develop and implement early warning systems planning to strengthen adaptive capacity and response to GLOFs and other climate hazards in vulnerable sectors and communities

#### Key activities:
- i) Critical national and local entities involved in disaster response (including the National Disaster Management Authority, Ministry of Home Affairs, National Emergency Operation Centre (NEOC), national stakeholders, district and local authorities, Local Emergency Operation Centres (LEOCs), and community-based organizations) will be engaged to develop and implement early warning systems for GLOFs and other climate hazards in vulnerable sectors and communities;
- ii) Key actors in the private sector networks; and
- iii) Vulnerable communities in the target districts and through their representatives will be involved in the development of early warning systems.

#### Objectives:
- i) To generate and disseminate early warnings to improve community response to GLOFs.
- ii) To ensure that the observation network is sustainable, with a focus on the existing and newly installed hydrological and meteorological stations in the target basins. In so doing, it will have the capacity to provide real-time data for decision-making.

65. To ensure sufficient understanding among target communities of how to access and respond to early warnings, comprehensive response plans will be developed for each of the 10 districts in the target watersheds, namely Bhojpur, Chakradhara, Khotang, Okhaldhunga, Sankhuwasabha, Manang, Lamjung, Sinhuli, Solukhumbu and Sunsari. These plans will be developed with the participation of local government leaders and women leaders in the target districts and provide step-by-step guidance on how to access early warnings and what actions should be taken when a warning is issued. The response plans will be gender-sensitive and will be conducted in a gender-responsive manner, in line with actions identified in the Gender Action Plan (Annex B). Training workshops for local government leaders and women leaders will be organized by NDRMIP to develop these response plans. The training will include discussions on how to access early warnings, what actions contained in the response plans and response drills will be organized by these entities to practice what actions should be taken when a warning is issued. These drills will also be gender-sensitive and will include training under this activity, assist with logistics arrangements and the mobilization of youth networks and volunteer corps, ensuring that the capacity building interventions reach indigenous People groups and are gender-sensitive.

66. GLOF EWS will be fully institutionalized by developing multi-hazard-based risk assessments – which integrate comprehensive early warning systems (EWS) for all hazards including landslides, floods, earthquakes, and disaster risk management (DRM), first aid and search and rescue. This process will start with a comprehensive stocktaking of existing DRM activities in the target districts, followed by the identification of gaps and corresponding specific measures at the local level. NDRMIP will lead this institutionalization process, supported by both government and non-government partners. The EWS will be integrated into the National Early Warning System (NEWS), updates to the observational network, safety, operation and maintenance will also be conducted to ensure the establishment of a fully functional GLOF EWS in the target districts.

67. Effective collaboration between Local Emergency Operation Centres and DRM will be ensured through engagement workshops and meetings that will strengthen vertical and horizontal linkages between these entities, strengthening existing early warning systems (EWS) for all hazards including landslides, floods, earthquakes, and disaster risk management (DRM), first aid and search and rescue. The integration of DRM’s dissemination mechanism into the LEOC with specialists contracted to import household data in the target districts will be conducted by NDRMIP. This will ensure that the EWS is fully functional and will also allow for tracking and rolling out of the digital DIMS/DRM Portal, supported by awareness raising that targets local government leaders and women leaders.

68. To ensure that target districts are able to access and respond to early warnings effectively, knowledge and awareness will be improved with training workshops and materials for vulnerable communities in the four target global watersheds. These workshops will also include discussions on how to access early warnings, what actions should be taken when a warning is issued, and how to mobilize volunteers, increasing community involvement in the early warning processes – including through the creation of volunteer corps.

69. Climate-responsive Municipal Disaster Preparedness and Response Plans will be developed with effective collaboration at the municipal level through engagement workshops for the preparation and completion of these plans. The workshops will include discussions on how to access early warnings, what actions should be taken when a warning is issued, and how to mobilize volunteers, increasing community involvement in the early warning processes – including through the creation of volunteer corps.

70. This project will establish the appropriate structures to ensure the Plans are implemented and followed beyond project completion. The plans will be developed with local government leaders and women leaders, ensuring that Plans in close collaboration with local municipalities and raising awareness on early warning response and access.

Output 3: Reduced probability of GLOF events and flash floods through disaster risk reduction measures implemented in the Gandaki Basin and DudhKoshi and Arun Sub-basins.

70 Output 3 will reduce the risk of GLOFs in the Gandaki Basin, and DudhKoshi and Arun Sub-basins. A joint assessment was carried out by the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) and the International Centre for Integrated Mountain Development (ICIMOD) to identify the critical lakes that pose a serious risk to people living near these lakes and the morphology of the surroundings. The critical lakes were classed under Rank 1, and these warrant the immediate implementation of urgent intervention measures. These include the construction of embankments, spillways, and monitoring. Several Rank 1 glacial lakes were identified within Nepal, and while all pose a serious risk, 4 of the lakes at the headwaters of the Gandaki Basin are considered to be the most critical due to their size and the risks they pose to the population of Nepal. These are the Thulagi, Lower Barun, Lumbung Tsho and Hongu 2 Lakes (Figure 1). More details on the selection of these lakes for urgent intervention are provided in Section 4 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

## B

**Figure 5. Locations of each of the target glacial lakes, relative to one another and their respective districts.**

71: Following the selection of the target lakes, an options analysis based on a priori screening, as well as international literature and expert opinion was undertaken (described in Section 2). A sensitivity analysis was undertaken to determine the specific interventions required to sufficiently reduce the risk of GLOF impacting nearby vulnerable communities. The most cost-effective intervention that would provide the greatest benefit for the least cost was selected for the proposed project. To reduce the primary risk of a GLOF, the water levels will be lowered in the lakes by constructing a dam and/or implementing other interventions to lower the water level of the lakes will reduce the potential of triggering a GLOF, as well as reducing the magnitude of a GLOF when one is triggered. This intervention would also provide benefits for nearby communities that are located within the potential flood-affected area. Furthermore, Eco-Disaster Risk Reduction (EDRR) interventions were selected to be implemented in the downstream areas of the lakes and in the communities downstream of the lakes to reduce the residual impacts of a GLOF after lake breaching. These downstream interventions include the construction of check dams, the implementation of community-based early warning systems, and nature-based eco-engineering solutions for sediment stabilization and flood mitigation. Implementing these downstream interventions would provide benefits for nearby communities that are located within the potential flood-affected area.

72: The proposed project was developed in close collaboration with the Government of Nepal (GoN) and is based on the recommendations provided by the UNDP commissioned Residual Risk and Vulnerability assessment (Annex 23), which included modeling for four different scenarios. These scenarios were designed to assess the residual risk of a GLOF occurring, as well as account for the GLOF impacts on the entire river basin downstream of each Glacial Lake, with the Lower Barun Glacial Lake being the most vulnerable. The four scenarios are: (1) Scenario 1: No intervention in any sublets with the Hingol-2 Glacial Lake affecting the Hingol-Dudh-Kosi River Basin subset and the Limiding Tino Glacial Lake; (2) Scenario 2: No intervention in any sublets with the Hingol-2 Glacial Lake affecting the Hingol-1 Glacial Lake without any interventions with a complete break; (3) Scenarios 3 & 4: The proposed project will be implemented and the residual risk of GLOF at the Glacial Lake without any interventions with a complete break. Scenarios 3 & 4: The proposed project will be implemented and the residual risk of GLOF at the Glacial Lake without any interventions with a complete break.

At the global level with a partial breach (a) after lowering by the prescribed amount of 3 m for Lower Bawen and Lamingo Tano and 1 m in Hongyo 2, and (b) Scenarios S4 (a–b)—GLOF at the Glaciers Lake with a partial breach(b) after lowering by the prescribed amount of 3 m for Lower Bawen and Lamingo Tano and 1 m in Hongyo 2. The impacts on agricultural land and infrastructure were calculated for all four scenarios and are summarised in Table 2. While there is a reduction in the number of people affected, the total economic damage (including both direct and indirect) after lowering by the prescribed amount to reduce damages and losses—a should be noted that the primary benefit of lowering is the reduction in the number of people affected—will increase as compared with the baseline scenario. The main reason for this is the benefit of this is demonstrated by the significant decrease observed for S3 and S4. Moreover, further reductions in the level of the lakes will result in a greater economic damage. However, the impact on the environment (e.g., biodiversity) will often offset the impacts of a partial GLOF at any of the three Glaciers Lakes. These results show that EcoDRR is the most cost-effective option for reducing the number of people affected and the total economic damage (including direct and gray infrastructure interventions downstream). The project activities that will be implemented under Output 3 are described in Section E.6 of this Funding Proposal.

The budget for Output 3 is USD 37,936,823 including a combination of USD 20,697,118 GCF finance and USD 9,039,754 co-finance from DFID, DfID-SC, DFATD and UNDP. For take lowering interventions, GCF finance will be used to cover the cost of technical assistance for the development and implementation of integrated climate–gender-responsive watershed management plans, as well as the implementation of EcoDRR measures to reduce the potential impact of residual GLOF flood waters, such as EcoDRR measures (e.g., check dams, terraces, etc.) and other interventions. The budget will also be used for the development and implementation of integrated climate–gender-responsive watershed and national park management plans, as well as technical assistance for the development and implementation of integrated climate–gender-responsive watershed management plans. The budget will cover the cost of technical assistance for the implementation of downstream measures, including technical advisors, as well as GEES; M&E and safeguards experts.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

## Under Item 7, the levels of the four high-priority lakes will be physically lowered to reduce the volume of the lakes. This will involve several steps. First, detailed site-specific engineering designs will be undertaken for the four lakes. These designs will be based on the results of the field surveys and studies conducted by the team of ground-on-the-ground assessments and validated by engineers and the AE before construction starts. The required environmental assessments include:

- **Biophysical surveys** of the site and the moraine dam to inform detailed analysis and precise design;
- **Bulk sediment sampling** to determine the most recent lake volume, depth, and topography, extension, and end moraines change;
- **Hydrological investigations** using a GPR survey; and
- **Geophysical investigations** using a GPR survey, and electric resistivity tomography assessments of the site to estimate the elevation of the moraine dam and the extent of the groundwater environment.

## Once the design is complete, the following steps will be undertaken:

1. **Temporary lowering of the Thulagi, Lower Barun and Lumbung Taho by 3 m and the Hongju Lake by 1 m**: The optimal level of take lowering for each lake will be determined based on a combination of factors including: (a) the volume reduction potential; (b) the ecological aspects of the lakes and the cost-benefit ratio of the risk reduction potential (Annex 26: Technical Lake Lowering Design). The design will include detailed information about the engineering required to lower each lake by the optimal level. The technical concept designs of the lake lowering interventions, and further details on the engineering required to lower the four lakes are presented in Section 13 of Annex 26: Technical Lake Lowering Design.
2. **Implementation of this activity** will determine whether to mobilize its own expert resources or outsource construction. If outsourcing is chosen, a detailed tender process will be undertaken and the selected contractor will be embedded within and will develop the capacity of DRM. DRM will also address the ongoing in-house Operations and Maintenance (O&M) for the Thulagi Lake Lowering activity (details below). For further details regarding O&M, see Annex 21: Operations and Maintenance Manual.

## The lowering of the Thulagi, Lumbung Taho, and Lower Barun Lakes will be undertaken through a 4-step process:

First, the diversion channel will be constructed to divert the water flow while the construction for the permanent lake lowering is undertaken. The effluent from this diversion channel will be directed into an outlet channel which will be built at the base of the moraine dam and will discharge downstream of the proposed structure. Thirdly, once the flow in diverted a sluice gate will be constructed at an optimal location on the main channel to control water levels during the lowering process. Fourth, once the main channel is complete, the outlet channel where the temporary collection is in place. Fourth, once the channel and sluice gates are in place, the collection of water from the lake will begin. The sluice gate will be closed when the water level drops below the top of the dam to protect it against bank erosion when the water is released after removing the confederate. After the completion of the main channel, the outlet channel will be constructed and connected with the main channel. The outlet channel (which is at higher elevation from the main channel) will be left as it is and water will flow from the main channel to the outlet channel through a sluice gate which will be installed in the main channel. An overview of this four-takes are presented in Section 12 of Annex 26: Feasibility Study and Annex 26: Technical Lake Lowering Design.

## For Hongju 2, only the main channel is proposed. Since there is no outlet discharge of lake, the end moraine itself will act as a sluice gate and no diversion channels are required. Once the main channels are built, the end moraine will be lowered to reduce the volume of the lake.

## Considering lessons learned from previous lake lowering exercises, all interventions targeting the 4 high-glacial lakes will take special consideration of cultural connections between local people and the lakes. This will include respect for local communities’ traditional practices and values in relation to the lakes, as well as their role in contributing commercial work. All waste generated during the lake lowering activities will also be carried back to Kathmandu Valley. The project team will ensure that all necessary safety measures are taken to protect workers – including evacuation procedures in case of serious injury – and overnight stay facilities in temporary housing made on onsite for this purpose.

### 3.2.2 Construct 67 conservation ponds (38 in the Arun Sub-basin and 29 in the Duthikot Sub-basin).

#### Activity 3.3 Implement Eco-dam/Risk Reduction solutions to reduce the impact of GLOFs and flash floods.

The project will implement interventions at 105 sites in the mountain ecosystems of Dhurkot and Arun Basin sub-basins to reduce the impacts of residual flow from GLOFs and flash floods on vulnerable communities. The project will also identify and mitigate risks associated with degraded ecosystems in floodplain areas. The interventions that will be implemented under this project activity were identified through a participatory risk assessment process involving local communities, government officials, and other stakeholders.

Specifically, the project will implement interventions at 105 sites in the mountain ecosystems of Dhurkot and Arun Basin sub-basins to reduce the impacts of residual flow from GLOFs and flash floods on vulnerable communities. The project will also identify and mitigate risks associated with degraded ecosystems in floodplain areas.

Specific interventions were then identified for each site (Arun River Basin: 108 sites, Duthikot River Basin: 124 sites). These included the construction of check dams, vegetation checks, terraces, bunds, and other structures to protect waterways and floodwaters and systems of river waters. The project will construct 7,744 m³ of vegetative gulleys, 5,960 m³ of vegetative gullies (v.g.), installing 25,885 m² of vegetative gully check dams and 15,013 m² of check dams. The project will also plant 47,000 trees, construct 1,000 terraces, build 69 bunds, and implement 298 hectares of brush layering; and planting 29.8 ha of grass seedlings.

This activity will be implemented by DoSFC which will also partner with the local communities to ensure that they are involved in the implementation process through community forest user groups (FUGs). To this end, the project will engage organizations such as the Federation of Community Forest User Groups (FCFUG) and the Nepal Forestry Department to provide technical support and use rights. This engagement will promote community ownership of ecological infrastructure and support the long-term sustainability of these interventions.

The specific species chosen for tree planting and vegetative hill infrastructure will follow local guidelines and best practices, with a focus on indigenous species that are well adapted to the local environment. These protocols are based on best practices through past projects and are currently being used under extensive government programs in Nepal. The project will also implement erosion control measures such as check dams and other operating mechanisms under DoSFC while specifically targeting flood vulnerable areas.

The lessons learned from this project activity can be applied to other similar projects, including the climate-smart agricultural program (CSAP) and the climate-resilient agriculture program (CRAP). The involvement of communities in these programs will ensure that they are involved at all stages that the community develops their own plans for the implementation of the interventions.

52. It is anticipated that the direct involvement of local governments will leverage additional investment in local adaptation measures downstream of the project, extending their contribution beyond the initial commitments made by them to the project. This will be achieved through a number of mechanisms, including capacity building and development of local institutions, contributes to the upscaling potential and sustainability of project interventions. Furthermore, the involvement of local governments is expected to contribute towards the achievement of the goals outlined in the Nepal Tourism Board (NTB) and the Independent Power Producers Association of Nepal (IPPN), providing a strong foundation for the long-term success of the project.

53. The Government of Nepal has already expressed its support for the project through co-financing commitments. While the values of these commitments are not yet known, they will be considered in the overall financial assessment and as an indication and a recognition of the potential value-benefits of the project. The potential for leveraging additional finance is particularly strong given that the Government has already committed to supporting the project with its own sustainable power production and risk reduction – bolstered by the information services provided through Output 1.

### Physical adaptation interventions under this activity will be supported by developing Integrated Waterhed Management Plans (IWMPs) in the Arun Sub-basin, Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin and the national level. These plans will cover land management and climate change adaptation. Training and capacity building interventions targeting community members, local government officials, and other stakeholders will ensure that these plans are effectively implemented by all officials who will ensure the effective implementation of these plans. The capacity development of these groups will be developed through training in the use of tools and techniques used in the MRHA and integrated watershed management (IWM) planning process. Training will also include the development of a national government on climate-induced hazards and disasters, and adaptation measures; i) curriculum for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (CCADRR); ii) curriculum for gender equality and women’s empowerment; iii) the development of training for CSOs, including mothers groups, women-leaders cooperatives, Indigenous Peoples organizations, and other community-based organizations; iv) curriculum for the implementation of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Programme (SGP); v) curriculum for the Gender and Social Inclusion (GESI) Action Plan (Annex 8), including Indigenous Peoples, Dalits, and women representatives.

### Training modules will be designed to empower government officials in the implementation of Multi-Hazard Risk Reduction Measures (MHRMs). The training will enhance their capacity with tools and techniques used in the MRHA and integrated watershed management planning process. These include the use of Remote Sensing and Geographic Information Systems (RS-GIS), Climate Resilient Development Index (CRDI), and the use of Agriculture Management Information Systems (AMIS). Capacity development of the relevant government officials will be developed through training in the use of tools and techniques used in MRHA and integrated, climate- and gender-responsive watershed and national park management plans in the Arun and Dhudhkoshi Sub-basins, as well as on the effective integration of these measures into federal and municipal level budgets.

#### Sub-activities

1. Install 533.6 ha of tree cover vulnerable riverbanks, sub-watershed areas and flood plains of 4 rivers (8.16 km²) in the Arun Sub-basin and 77.16 ha in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin.
2. Install 90.5 m³ of check dams (653.996 m³ in the Arun Sub-basin and 23,040 m³ in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
3. Install 18,746 m³ of live check dams (653.996 m³ in the Arun Sub-basin and 23,040 m³ in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
4. Install 15,298 ha of vegetative gabion retention structures (3,447.3 m² in the Arun Sub-basin and 16,151.44 m² in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
5. Install 10,000 m³ of vegetative gabion check dams (980.962 m³ in the Arun Sub-basin and 7,735 m³ in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin) and a total of 15,000.8 ha of vegetative dry stone check dams (9,371.25 m² in the Arun Sub-basin and 5,629.55 m² in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
6. Install a total of 1,194 m² of vegetated stone check dams (10-15.52 m² in the Arun Sub-basin and 1,611.5 m² in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
7. Install 38,000 m³ of rock check dams (15,819 m³ in the Arun Sub-basin and 22,181 m³ in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin) and a total of 46,000 m³ of dry stone check dams (37.650 m³ in the Arun Sub-basin and 8,438 m³ in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
8. Plant a total of 298,162 ha of grass seedlings (155,419 m² in the Arun Sub-basin and 142,743 m² in the Dhudhkoshi Sub-basin).
