## The project is a requirement of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) under Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MEWRiW) of Nepal.

### Executing Entity (IP per UNDP terminology)

The executing entity for this project is the Government of Nepal, represented by two entities:
- **The Department of Hydrology and Meteorology** (DHM) under Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MEWRiW), which will be responsible for implementation and serve as UNDP’s Implementing Partner
- The MEWRiW Finance Unit, which will play an important role in distributing the funds to the IP and Responsible Parties through the appropriate channels.

### The MEWRiW DHM will be accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of its implementation. The MEWRiW Finance Unit will also be responsible for disbursing funds.

The MEWRiW DHM will designate a National Project Coordinator, responsible for the overall direction, strategic guidance and timely execution of the project in Nepal through UNDP Country Office in Nepal, the Himalayan Region Hub and Headquarters in New York.

### In addition, the Government of Nepal has requested UNDP to provide direct project services for this project. UNDP, in collaboration with the MEWRiW DHM, will enter into a Letter of Agreement (LOA) signed between MEWRiW/DHM (on behalf of Govt.) and UNDP. Such support will be predominantly administrative in nature and will include:
- Project Management Function: The MEWRiW DHM and UNDP agree that these services are not mandatory, and will be provided only upon Government request.
- Project Monitoring and Evaluation Function: UNDP will provide this service as a stand-alone service to the project. All other project services follow UNDP policies on the recovery of direct project costs relating to GCF funded projects.

### Notwithstanding the provision of these administrative support services by UNDP and as stated above, it is noted that the MEWRiW DHM retains full responsibility for all aspects of the project implementation and management of the project board. UNDP will not exercise any discretion or decision-making with respect to day-to-day management and execution of the project.

## Accredited Entity

UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional level, as well as independent external support through the Project Steering Committee providing out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management and implementation by ensuring that the project is managed according to the agreed Project Management function; the Project Steering Committee cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to UNDP staff or other parties.

The UNDP Country Office in Nepal will provide support services for this project through a Letter of Agreement (LOA) with the MEWRiW DHM. The UNDP Country Office will be responsible for providing an Accredited Entity to the GCF. UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific overall quality and assurance services including: (1) day-to-day oversight supervision; (2) oversight of the project completion, (3) oversight of report preparation.

## Justification

The justification to engage UNDP support services relates to the capacity constraints identified based on partner capacity assessment. The MEWRiW DHM has a limited number of staff with expertise in hydrology and meteorology (DHM). UNDP will be the best entity to engage various partners from both public and private sector, either following competitive selection or through existing relationships. The terms and conditions for the provision of UNDP support services shall be detailed in a Letter of Agreement (LOA) for UNDP support services. As the service supports will be provided by UNDP, all GCF implementation rules and policies apply.

## Project Steering Committee

The project will be governed by the Project Board (PB) which will be set up by the MoEWR/DHI, the project Executing Entity, and chaired by the Secretary of MoEWR responsible for Water Resources and Irrigation. The PB will consist of senior representatives from relevant federal and provincial ministries including National Planning Commission, UNDP, representatives from the executing entity, and other stakeholders such as local government bodies, civil society, and private sector as necessary.

The PB will be chaired by the Secretary of MoEWR responsible for Water Resources and Irrigation. The PB will be responsible for ensuring that the project complies with the strategies, principles, and overall approach outlined in this proposal. The PB will be responsible for making decisions by consensus. Its responsibilities including recommending to the Project Board (PB) on major issues such as project scope, budget, and schedule.

The PB will review progress reports from the Project Board (PB) and provide feedback on the project progress report. In order to ensure UNDP’s ultimate accountability, PB decisions should be made in accordance with the principles of transparency, integrity, and efficiency. The PB will meet at least once a year or as necessary when raised by the Project Coordination Officer (PCO) or one of the committee members. The PB may also meet more frequently if required.

The PB will have the following functions:
- All decisions on project implementation will be made by the implementing entity/Implementing Partner that holds the project ownership and chairs the PB.
  - The Executive will be the National Project Director, DMIET, which will play the overall coordination function for the project.
  - The Development Partner represents the interests of the parties concerned that provide funding and/or technical assistance to the project.
- Beneficiary Representatives from MoEWR, MoFE, MoH, as well as provincial local governments, CSOs and private sector partners who ensure the realization of project benefits from the perspective of project beneficiaries.

## Implementation and Operational Arrangements

### 104. Technical Advisory Group (TAG)

The PB will set up a Technical Advisory Group (TAG) chaired by a senior government official, who is not involved in project management and oversight. The TAG of the project selected by the PB will be composed of representatives from relevant government agencies, including the members of the TAG. Other members of TAG will include technical experts working in other government agencies, research institutions, or international organizations with expertise on climate change adaptation and mitigation in various thematic areas of the project, as decided by UNDP in consultation with the Chair of the PB. The main role of the TAG is to provide advice and guidance to the PB on the implementation of the project based on international best practice, reports to thematic assessments and technical studies conducted by the project team, and other relevant information. Meetings of the TAG will be organized at least twice a year or as required.

### 105. Project Management Unit (PMU)

The Executing Entity will establish and manage a Project Management Unit (PMU) that will operate under the overall supervision of the PB. The PMU will be responsible for the day-to-day management, including recruitment staff for processing of human resources, procurement and logistic aspects of the implementation of the project may be assigned to the PMU.

### 106. Responsibilities

The PMU is also responsible for overall planning and budgeting, collaborating with other ministries, sectors, and government entities to implement the project performance management system; bidding for the provision of project consultancy services, including technical assistance, procurement of goods and services, monitoring and evaluation, disbursement and implementation of project audits, operation of accounts, monitoring and preparation of overall periodic reports to the PB.

### 107. Project Coordination Officer (PCO)

The PMU will be headed by the Project Coordination Officer (PCO), who will have day-to-day management and supervisory responsibilities of the project team and will be supported by specialist theames as well as operational assistants. The PCO is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with quality standards and within the specified constraints of time and cost. The multi-year work plan and annual work plan are prepared, monitored, and updated on a regular basis to ensure alignment with the approved budget set by the PB. The PCO is also responsible for maintaining and updating the procurement plan, project risk log, and stakeholder engagement plan. The PCO will be supported by the Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Officer, and the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) Expert. The PCO will provide regular updates to the PB on progress of the project implementation through periodic reports to the PB. General gender will be mainstreamed in each output and activity and oversight for gender mainstreaming will be provided by the GESI Expert.

### 108. Project Assurance

The "project assurance" function of UNDP is to support the PB by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management milestones are managed and monitored, including the preparation and submission of periodic reports to the PB. The project assurance responsibilities will be assumed by UNDP Deputy Resident Representative (DPR) in consultation with the Chief of the Programme for Environment and Resilience, Programme Analyst – RBM and Programme Associate on behalf of UNDP.

## Fund Flow

Upon approval of the project by the GCF Board, a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) will be signed between the GCF and UNDP. Following signing, UNDP and Government of Nepal will sign project document, which is then submitted to the Ministry of Finance for Accession. Upon receipt of the FAA from the GCF, the signed FAA will be transferred to UNDP. UNDP will transfer the GCF funds to the Ministry of Finance for Accession. The Ministry of Finance will then transfer the funds to UNDP. UNDP will retain necessary funds in line with the approved budget in the funding proposal to cover project implementation costs and other operational expenses. The disbursement of project funds following decision of the PB, the funds will be transferred on quarterly basis from MoF to the Executing Entity (DEMT) and the RPs (DoFS, DoPWP, NDRRMA) through respective line ministries.

### B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

107. Nepal is one of the top 20 most-disaster-prone countries in the world, with multiple climate change and non-climate change hazards. The main climate-related hazards include: floods; landslides; windstorms; wildfires; forest fires; Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and associated debris flows; and droughts. Nepal is ranked among the top 10 countries most affected by climate change on the long-term global Climate Risk Index from 2000 to 2014, with a score of 358. The country experienced extreme rainfall in August 2017 which caused widespread flooding and landslides. In 2017, Nepal experienced its worst monsoon season since 1968. A total of 1,100 people were killed, over 400,000 houses were destroyed or partially damaged, and over 3 million people were displaced by the floods. These events also resulted in shortages of food and water, as well as other humanitarian impacts in the affected areas.

108. The capacity of the Government of Nepal (GoN) to respond to climate hazards is additionally constrained by the country’s limited financial resources. For example, the GoN was unable to fully implement its National Disaster Risk Management Strategy following the two earthquakes in April and May 2015 that caused extensive damage to property and physical infrastructure and resulted in over 9,000 deaths. The GoN is currently implementing a Post-Disaster Recovery Strategy and a Post-Earthquake Reconstruction Strategy to expedite the reconstruction of damaged infrastructure and to shape the course of the country’s long-term development through the promotion of sustainable livelihoods[16]. The 2017 extreme rainfall events


---
**Page 40**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

B

resulted in widespread flooding which affected 1.7 million people and damaged 126,000 ha of rice paddy**. The combined impact of these major events on Nepal resulted in US$8 billion in economic losses and required the expenditure of US$354 million for recovery and reconstruction efforts. These efforts were funded by the GCF through a now 95% complete, which translates to the reconstruction of 753,000 private houses out of 811,000. Moreover, the reconstruction of public infrastructure such as roads, bridges, schools, health facilities and other government buildings are all still ongoing**. The current state of the reconstruction efforts emphasizes: (i) the extended amount of time required to complete these efforts; (ii) the significant challenges in coordinating recovery and rebuilding with these extreme events; and (iii) how vulnerable Nepalese communities are to extreme events. The recovery efforts, as well as the ongoing reconstruction efforts, have a direct impact on Nepal’s capacity to adapt.

109. In the 2022/23 UNDP Human Development Report, Nepal is ranked 144 of 191 countries on the Human Development Index (HDI), with an HDI value of 0.602 and a GNI per capita of US$758. Its gross domestic product (GDP) accounts for $29.1 billion in 2019, which is equivalent to 3% of the global GDP. The share of Nepal’s GDP has decreased from 26% of GDP in 2016–2017 to 50% in 2018–2019 — and is projected to further increase into the future**. This data further highlights that Nepal’s economy is highly vulnerable to external shocks, such as natural disasters. The funding required to support recovery efforts and thereby reducing economic growth and the ability of the GNI to repay itself.

110. Imports of goods and services accounted for 45% of Nepal's total GDP at US$29 billion in 2018. These imports were significantly disrupted in September 2015, for a period of four months, during a period where Nepal was experiencing the worst economic crisis since its independence from British rule in 1950. The earthquake exacerbated already-existing unequal social and economic opportunities, which are prevalent in the hierarchical society of Nepal**. The combination of the 2015 earthquakes and their impacts on trade resulted in a decrease in Nepal’s GDP growth rate to below zero for the first time since independence from British rule in 1950. The recurrence of these extreme events will have significant impacts on Nepal’s population — 80% of whom lived in rural areas at the time of the earthquake**. These events have also had a direct impact on Nepal’s economy, with the loss of revenue and increased costs for reconstruction efforts. The economic losses on Nepal's economy have resulted in the GNI having to prioritize national rebuilding efforts, with little funding available for climate change adaptation.

111. Nepal’s economic growth model is aimed at achieving middle-income country status by 2030, by shifting from consumption to investments — with a focus on the development of physical and social infrastructure. The country's current economic model relies heavily on foreign aid, which accounts for approximately 45% of its GDP. The South Asian Council countries, India and Malaysia — which accounted for 25% of the GDP in 2018–19**. This overseas employment has been a major source of income for Nepal’s economy; however, it is also having a significant substantive impact on the GNI's ability to invest in climate-resilience building activities and projects. The Nepali economic model relies heavily on foreign aid, which accounts for approximately 45% of its GDP**. This has had a significant cash deficit for various financial and economic activities as the liquidity crunch persists. GNI support, in the form of foreign aid, is critical to Nepal’s ability to invest in climate-resilience building activities and projects following extreme climate events by improving the resilience and preparedness of Nepal and lay the foundation for the private sector engagement and investment in climate risk reduction in the future.

#### B

**112. To reduce the risk of floods, particularly GLOFs**, Nepal should adopt a comprehensive and integrated climate risk management approach centred around the reduction and prevention of, as well as the preparations for these risks. This will be achieved by adopting an integrated approach to flood risk management in the context of the target glacial lakes, a practice that has been effectively implemented in the country previously. For example, **Lake Tsho Rolpa** is one such lake where the **Integrated Flood and Glacial Lake Outburst Flood Risk Reduction Project was established in 2015 to lower its water level by 3-4 meters through the construction of a spillway and other flood risk reduction measures**, with an estimated cost of US$6.2 million. The lowering of the four target lakes is US$2.6 million each, **with further investment required to bring about the paradigm shift in Nepal’s approach towards integrated flood risk management**.

The project will also provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms – including both private and public sector finance – as a priorities fund for the abovementioned reduction efforts. The Gulf Fund has already provided US$10 million to support the establishment of such a fund, which is being developed in Nepal, to adapt to the impacts of GLOFs and other climate-driven extreme events. Co-finance of US$131.785 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), World Bank, Asian Development Bank (ADB), UNDP, NDRF, UNDP, ITPS and IPPAN [please see Section 6.1 for a breakdown of co-finance contributions].

**113. Without GCF support and with the financing impact of the recent Covid-19 pandemic, the public and private sector will be unable to implement the flood risk reduction measures introduced under Output 2.5**, which are critical in reducing disaster risks, limiting national and local decision-makers’ capacity to plan for and respond to the increasing impacts of climate change. The lack of financing will also result in a significant increase in the vulnerability of communities living near glacial lakes, particularly those in remote areas. **As such, GLOFs, the vulnerability of communities in Nepal will continue to rise – with ongoing rebuilding efforts being insufficient to address the underlying causes and risks associated with these events**.

The project will therefore provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms – including both private and public sector finance – as priorities funds for the abovementioned reduction efforts. ### B.8 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Sustainable disaster risk reduction in Nepal

**The principles of sustainability underpin all three project outputs**, with the highest direct adaptation impact potential being achieved through Output 2. The measures introduced under Output 2 will ensure that vulnerable communities are timely informed of GLOFs and other extreme events, while the interventions under Output 3 will provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms – including both private and public sector finance – as priorities funds for the abovementioned reduction efforts.

The combination of long-term monitoring systems coupled with improved dissemination systems and methods under Output 2 will ensure that communities are informed in good time, allowing them to take appropriate action and adequately in the future. The integration of the physical risk reduction measures introduced under Output 3, with improved early warning systems and other interventions under Output 2, will enable vulnerable communities to respond to extreme events such as GLOFs – more effectively. Furthermore, the physical risk reduction measures introduced under Output 3 will also provide additional protection for the local population and infrastructure in the target areas.

The combination of grey and green infrastructure will ensure the long-term sustainability of these systems, with the former providing immediate benefits while the latter provides longer term benefits to ecosystems in the target areas – these ecosystems will provide additional protective services once restored – including flood risk reduction. The integration of grey and green infrastructure will also provide a more resilient infrastructure which will take longer to provide the expected benefits. Involvement and training of community members, particularly women, in the implementation of the project interventions under Output 3 will ensure that they are within the communities, allowing them to take responsibility for the implementation and management of similar systems in future years.

The project will therefore provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms – including both private and public sector finance – as priorities funds for the abovementioned reduction efforts. The Gulf Fund has already provided US$10 million to support the establishment of such a fund, which is being developed in Nepal, to adapt to the impacts of GLOFs and other climate-driven extreme events. Co-finance of US$131.785 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), World Bank, Asian Development Bank (ADB), UNDP, NDRF, UNDP, ITPS and IPPAN [please see Section 6.1 for a breakdown of co-finance contributions].

### Public-private partnerships to promote GLOF risk reduction

**Investment in long-term capacity building under Output 3 will be complemented by the establishment of public-private partnerships (PPPs) with private sector entities focused on the sustainable update of GLOF risk reduction in Nepal to complement the investment in long-term capacity building under Output 3. The PPPs will provide a platform for the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms – including both private and public sector finance – as priorities funds for the abovementioned reduction efforts**.

The Gulf Fund has already provided US$10 million to support the establishment of such a fund, which is being developed in Nepal, to adapt to the impacts of GLOFs and other climate-driven extreme events. Co-finance of US$131.785 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), World Bank, Asian Development Bank (ADB), UNDP, NDRF, UNDP, ITPS and IPPAN [please see Section 6.1 for a breakdown of co-finance contributions].

B

For private sector investment in future disaster risk reduction initiatives: Country-wide uptake of GLOF risk reduction interventions will be promoted by the establishment of public-private partnerships, and introducing the model to leverage private sector investments from IPNAN and TANR. The combination of private sector investments from IPNAN and TANR demonstrates the willingness of the private sector to engage with the project and a principal-agent relationship is established between the private sector and the project. The establishment of public-private partnerships, combined with ground-truthed agreements and risk and agreements facilitated by the project, combined on-broth-ground interventions, will have potential to leverage investments from the private sector in future disaster risk reduction initiatives, particularly for eco-DNR interventions that align with watershed management investments of the hydropower producers.

The mechanisms for establishing and expanding these public-private partnerships will be developed under Activity 1.2, which will involve the development of a knowledge base for GLOF risk reduction interventions, including customized and targeted risk information for pre-project adaptation planning and early warning opportunities to engage with private sector stakeholders in the hydropower sector, particularly those involved in the production of vector-borne diseases after flood events. The production of customized and targeted risk information will incentivize disaster risk reduction investments from the private sector, which will be further supported by a sense of ownership in the approach, creating a platform for continued, sustainable investment in the long term.

Capacity development and knowledge sharing for climate-responsive planning

1.0 Capacity development and knowledge sharing for climate-responsive planning will be developed under Activity 1.1 to effectively manage the hazard monitoring and the generation and dissemination of early warning introduced through GLOF risk reduction interventions. The project will develop a technical capacity of DHI under Output 1.1, as well as the capacity of TANR and other sectoral stakeholders in the hydropower sector to support required hazard mapping and design and implement GLOF risk reduction interventions in-house, as opposed to being dependent on external partners. This will be achieved through a combination of training workshops, additional to the establishment of public-private partnerships with IPNAN and other tourism-sector entities, the sharing of knowledge and experience gained from the project, and the development of a digital database that supports the long-term sustainability of project interventions by providing a knowledge base for GLOF risk reduction in vulnerable watersheds. This will be achieved through the establishment of a technical capacity for hazard mapping and development of a spatial digital database (GIS) of glaciers and glacial lakes along with methods, tools and operating procedures to support the generation and dissemination of early warning information for GLOF risk reduction in vulnerable watersheds.

1.9 The project will promote knowledge sharing (disseminated broadly) through collaboration between TANR, other sectoral stakeholders, and IPNAN to disseminate knowledge about GLOF monitoring and knowledge management. The dissemination of sector-specific weather- and climate-change-related risk information by DHI will be expanded to include the hydropower sector, as well as other tourism-sector entities. This will be achieved through the use of radio, television and tailored bulletins. In addition, evacuation plans, adaptation planning and knowledge database development will be disseminated to TANR and other sectoral stakeholders in the hydropower sector. The collaboration will be fostered under Activity 1.1, which will involve the strengthening of knowledge bases, and Activity 1.2, which will involve the establishment of public-private partnerships. This will be achieved through training workshops and not-for-informed planning. Moreover, the design of the proposed project accounts for mutual learning and knowledge sharing among stakeholders to ensure that there is a strong sense of ownership amongst all stakeholders involved in the project. The project will also facilitate knowledge sharing among stakeholders to ensure that there is a strong sense of country ownership around GLOF risk reduction interventions, which will be achieved through the establishment of public-private partnerships as well as the scalability and replicability of project interventions.

The proposed project aligns with the priorities laid out in the GoN’s 5th Five-Year Development Plan (2019-2023) [Section 8.2], which prioritizes sustainable use of natural resources and disaster risk reduction. The alignment between the proposed project and the GoN’s 5th Five-Year Development Plan is presented under Section D.5. This alignment will ensure that the strategies introduced in this project are effectively integrated into national development plans, thereby ensuring that the knowledge and lessons learned from the resulting knowledge and decision-making processes fully embedded within national systems. ## Operation and maintenance beyond the project lifetime

### 12.1 Maintenance of the lake lowering interventions

- **DGM**: The operation and maintenance of the lake lowering interventions, EWS installations and downstream EcoDRR measures will be undertaken during and after the project to guarantee the sustainability and optimal functioning of these interventions.
- **Hydrological and meteorological stations**: These stations are expected to continue operating for 15 years beyond the project. The targets learned in Year 10 will – and must continue for 15 years, DGM will decide whether to mobilize its own expert resources or to hire a consultant from outside the country.
- **Maintenance of the interventions**: Maintenance activities will be recruited and retained for a period of time by the DGM or a professional consultant, after which they will perform their functions. These maintenance activities include: regular inspection of the gate operation, timely inspection of hydromechanical and civil structures and reporting of any faults to DGM.
- **Maintenance of the stations**: Maintenance of the stations will be performed in accordance with the terms of reference for contractors to fulfill contracts to perform O&M depending on the scope of work necessary. Minor civil works and maintenance will be carried out as a regular operation according to specific structures, which are expected every 8–10 years.

### 12.2 Maintenance of the new stations

- **DGM**: DGM will also be responsible for O&M of the new hydrological monitoring stations installed as part of the lake lowering interventions and maintenance of the meteorological and hydrological stations installed under Output 2 of the project.
- **Maintenance frequency**: The different types of maintenance required for these interventions will include: (i) routine annual maintenance; (ii) maintenance following extreme events. Proper and regular maintenance will be especially important until the planted species have established themselves, as they are expected to take up to five years before being able to be adequately regulated.

### 12.3 Long-term sustainability of the restoration measures

- **Restoration targets**: The Government of Nepal has given high priority to plantation programmes, which are reflected in several strategic plans. This includes targeting a good tree planting at least 50% of the time over the course of ten years for the country’s degraded forests.
- **Forest land degradation**: Forest land degradation is one of the major causes of soil erosion and loss of biodiversity. The Government has set targets to plant 10,000 hectares per year of terrestrial restoration areas, sustainably manage forests, combat desertification, and halt and reverse land degradation by 2030.
- **Plantation targets**: Plantation area targets range from 45,000 ha to 60,000 ha by the end of the project period, as well as to produce at least 23.2 million seedlings per year for the next five years. The Government has set a target of 100,000 hectares of forest land restoration per year and public-land plantations per 20,000 ha.
- **National priorities**: National priorities for forest restoration are also reflected in the country’s Forest Act (2073), which sets out to increase the area under forest cover by 15% over five years. The project will work with government agencies and local communities to plant trees on degraded land, restore forests, and maintain forest carbon storage for combating climate change impacts by planting trees, while maintaining 45% of the total area of the country under forest cover by 2030.

### Technology transfer
- The technical and institutional capacity of decision-makers at all the national and local levels will be strengthened through:
  - Developing methods and SOPs for inventorying and developing spatial digital databases (GIS) of glaciers and glacial lakes, including their vulnerability assessment.
  - Introducing methods and tools for GLOF hazard prioritization, mapping and other extrinsic flood, landslide, rainfall-induced landslides, and GLOF, using a combination of remote-sensing and satellite data, as well as ground-truthing and socio-economic vulnerability assessments.
  - Developing climate-induced hazard event recording and management database systems.

### Capacity building
- The proposed project will facilitate technology transfer by implementing innovative climate change adaptation interventions to reduce the risk and impacts of GLOFs in Nepal. These interventions include:
  - Strengthening the technical capacity of decision-makers at all levels for monitoring, early warning, and response infrastructure for the diversification of GLOF and flash flood flow.
  - Implementing Eco-Disaster Risk Reduction solutions (EDRRS) to reduce the risk and impacts of GLOFs in Nepal. These EDRRS will be implemented through:
    a) Strengthening the technical capacity of DHM for the monitoring of climate risk and hazard.
    b) Developing and implementing an integrated GLOF risk management system that includes both donor-funded and nationally-driven initiatives (described in Section B.1). However, creating conditions and capacities to enable the use of these systems will require:
      i) Strengthening the technical capacity of DHM for monitoring climate risk and hazard.
      ii) Developing and implementing an integrated GLOF risk management system that includes both donor-funded and nationally-driven initiatives (described in Section B.1).
    c) Building the capacities of decision-makers at all levels to enable them to:
      i) Implement a national GLOF early warning system, including the development of standard operating procedures across the relevant national institutions; and
      ii) Building the required capacities to enable the use of these systems.
  - Strengthening the technical capacity of DHM for monitoring climate risk and hazard.

129. Under Output 2 of the project, capacity-building activities will focus on increasing the capacity of DRIM to lead climate change adaptation efforts at the local level. This includes training and mentoring of DRIM staff to deliver climate information products to the most vulnerable communities in the Gandaki Basin, and Dhaulagiri and Arun Sub-basins. The training will be conducted by the Climate Change Division of the Department of Water Supply and Drift staff will be delivered to strengthen their capacity to: i) collect, process and manage climate data from the river and glacier monitoring stations; ii) develop and disseminate climate information products in a format that is easily interpretable infographics for local communities. The adaptive capacity and response among vulnerable communities in the upstream areas of the Gandaki Basin and Dhaulagiri and Arun Sub-basins to GLOF will be enhanced through a combination of training and awareness-raising interventions.

#### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximated on page)

**Fund-level Impacts**

- **3.16**: The proposed project will enhance climate resilience at Nepal’s Gandaki Basin, and Dutkhuli and Anan Basins by contributing to GCF’s First Adaptation Readiness Area (ARA) – increased resilient and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions, and ARA4 – Ecosystems and ecosystem services.

- **3.17**: To reduce the primary risk of a GLOF: for the water levels will be lowered at the four priority lakes (Thulagi, Lower Bauri, Upper Bauri, and Anan) to below 200 m above sea level (a.s.l.), thereby reducing the risk of triggering a GLOF, as well as reducing the magnitude of a GLOF when one is triggered. Furthermore, Eco-Disaster Risk Reduction interventions will be implemented in the four priority lakes and their catchments. These interventions will also be implemented downstream of the lakes to reduce the residual impacts of a GLOF after lake lowering, supported by rehabilitation of degraded ecosystems. The project will also implement an integrated approach that includes a combination of conservation ponds and flood walls to divert GLOF and flash flood flow and nature-based eco-engineering measures in the Anan Basin. This will significantly reduce the risk of flooding downstream infrastructure interventions and Eco-DRR will significantly reduce the exposure of vulnerable communities to climate-related disasters, including floods and landslides. The project will also provide livelihood diversification options for the most vulnerable communities and households as well as potential economic losses from GLOF events by US$ 182,562–930,819 (219) (See Section D.6 for more detail on avoided costs).

- **3.18**: This impact will directly benefit 385,956 people in Nepal (55% women and 45% men; 13% of the total population of Nepal) through glacial lake lowering, flood risk reduction and ecosystem restoration. Indirect beneficiaries include households that are dependent on the Anan Basin for their livelihoods and those who benefit from direct interventions will increase the climate resilience of 10 districts in the target basins. This increased resilience will be achieved by reducing the risk of flooding downstream infrastructure interventions and Eco-DRR, as well as an estimated 1392–4386 households (2024) will be benefited in terms of climate mitigation and adaptation. The project will also provide livelihood diversification options for the most vulnerable communities and households.

**Project beneficiaries were assessed based on the areas of the Anan, Dutkhuli and Manyangri River Basins, which are characterized by high population density and poverty levels. These basins have degraded ecosystems in the Anan and Dutkhuli Basins that will trigger benefits for the populations living in the basin as well as those who benefit from direct interventions. The downstream impact areas of these basins are 856 km² and 4,063 km² respectively. The downstream impact areas of these basins are 856 km² and 1917 km² respectively. The total area of the Anan Basin is 2,919 km² and there are 29 municipalities in the direct impact area of the Anan Basins 16 and 19 in the Dutkhuli and Thulagi Basins, respectively. The number of beneficiaries was taken as the population of these impact areas.**

Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for climate change adaptation and resilience.

Output 1 will strengthen the GCM's institutional and technical capacity to develop and integrate climate risk and hazard information into its decision-making processes. This objective will be achieved through the implementation of two project activities under Output 1. Under Activity 1.1, the existing knowledge base on climate risks and hazards in the priority areas will be strengthened. This will be achieved by developing methods and SOPs for the inventory and development of a social vulnerability index (SVI) based on the results of household surveys, community-based participatory investigations, as well as topographic and bathymetric surveys. In conjunction with the GIS database and surveys, methods will also be developed to integrate climate risk information into GCM's decision-making processes for mapping other extreme events. Furthermore, the GIS database and surveys will inform the development of methodologies and SOPs for the integration of climate risk information in the GCM's decision-making process activity. The development and implementation of these methods and SOPs will ensure that DMAs' institutional capacity is strengthened to integrate climate risk information into its decision-making processes.

Output 2 will strengthen the GCM's institutional and technical capacity to develop and disseminate climate risk information for early action in response to anticipated extreme climate events. Output 2 will use the flow of climate information by facilitating technology transfer to strengthen NRC's institutional and technical capacity to provide climate risk information services and tools for early warning and early action preceding a GLOF. Under Output 2, Activity 2.1 will contribute to the generation of climate information through the installation of hydrological and meteorological stations across the prioritized GLOF

### Core indicator: R. Dependent on GCF and other sources of finance to constitute an effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards.

In addition to contributing to the CGIIF’s impact indicators, the project aligns with and contributes to several targeted outcomes for the CGIIF’s work plan for 2034-2036:

* **Target 1:** NDCs, NAPs and LTS. Through the proposed project, GCF contributions will contribute directly to the achievement of the CGIIF’s target on NDCs, NAPs and LTS.
* **Target 2:** Early Warning Systems (EWS). The CGIIF will support the Government of Nepal in improving hazard monitoring and the generation and dissemination of early warnings to local communities and important environmental indicators. This will be achieved through the implementation of the CGIIF’s work plan for the Action Plan 2018-2030, Paris Agreement NAP, NDCs) and also EEWALL initiatives.
* **Target 3:** Climate Resilience Infrastructure (CRI). The CGIIF will support the Government of Nepal in strengthening climate resilience infrastructure in Nepal by reducing GHG risk through both physical measures and EcoDRR measures.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

With the increasing intensity and risk of GLOF events, the Government of Nepal (GoN) has identified the existing model of reactive response to these extreme events as no longer appropriate and initiated a shift to proactive measures. The proposed project will support this paradigm shift by building on the lessons learned from the preventive and preparedness model built with support from NEMA to manage and respond to GLOF and associated flooding and other hazards in the Rupakot and Jhikri Gola Catchments. While these initial efforts were effective, and had important demonstrated impacts, they were limited by a lack of scale and institutional capacity for replication at scale and instituting GHG risk reduction tactics.

While this approach will require government partnerships to promote investment, it will also enable the adoption of an integrated approach that addresses the root causes of GLOF events. The CGIIF will support the Government of Nepal in establishing up-to-date risk reduction measures with downstream interventions and upgraded early warning systems (EWS) to address these risks. This will be achieved by supporting the Government of Nepal in establishing an enabling environment, including establishing an improved policy environment, demonstrating the complementarity between GHG risk reduction and poverty reduction strategies, and providing technical assistance for project development. The CGIIF’s support will go beyond the static quo of meeting immediate needs through project-based externally funded disaster response to comprehensive management that will break the cycle of recurrent, precarious and often expensive measures.

Potential for scaling up and replication

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

The proposed project focuses on reducing the risk of a GLOF at the 6 most at-risk glacial lakes in Nepal, namely the Thalay, Lower Barun, Lumbini Tsho and Hongu 2 Lakes. Under the project, the water levels of these four lakes will be reduced by implementing a combination of engineering (Activity 3.2) and nature-based solutions (Activity 3.3) to reduce the residual impacts of these lakes on downstream communities in Nepal as well as across the border into India. The project will also use a combination of engineering (Activity 3.2) and nature-based solutions (Activity 3.3) to reduce the residual impacts of these lakes on downstream communities in Nepal as well as across the border into India.

The project will promote their use at the remaining 17 PGLs in Nepal and across the Himalayan region. While not all of these PGLs are currently at risk, they are all vulnerable to GLOFs if conditions become sufficiently extreme technologically or economically feasible, climate change is continuously altering the risk level of each lake. The risk management strategy for these lakes will be based on a combination of early warning systems (EWS) and interventions as needed, ensuring that risks are identified and mitigated as they develop around each lake. Potential cost-effective solutions include: 1) Early Warning Systems (EWS); 2) Community-based monitoring; 3) Water management measures, with varying levels of input required. This will create a national framework for ongoing action, ensuring that governance structures are in place to support the implementation and maintenance of these systems.

The project is also designed to build on existing partnerships – such as those between GEF and the Government of Nepal (as evidenced by the GEF-funded project, entitled Community Based Flood and Glacial Lake Outburst Risk Reduction Project) and with other partners. The project will also seek to strengthen existing partnerships or create new ones where necessary. These include: 1) Strengthening the capacity of DHHM and its partners to implement EWS; providing the platform for DHHM to expand these networks (for example through the establishment of new stations); 2) Building on existing relationships with other key stakeholders, such as the National Disaster Risk Management Authority (NDRMA), the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM), and the Ministry of Federal Affairs and Local Development; 3) Strengthening the capacity of DHHM to access additional funding from other sources. The project will also seek to strengthen existing partnerships or create new ones where necessary.

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

143. To support the uptake of the new adaptation interventions in Nepal, an enabling environment will be created through a number of activities (Activity 2). These include: strengthening institutional and regulatory structures for climate change adaptation; developing and implementing water resources management plans; integrating climate risk information into planning and decision-making will be developed; establishing a national early warning system (EWS) to provide timely and accurate weather and climate information; involving in flood risk information services (Activity 1.2) to enable ongoing partnerships between the private sector and government agencies for flood risk reduction, including through the development of an EWS; and enhancing disaster response. DMI’s capacity to fulfil its mandate will be strengthened by expanding the existing hydrometeorological monitoring network and developing a national early warning system (EWS). This will support the implementation of the climate change adaptation strategy reduction decision-making (Activity 2.1). These improvements will be complemented by establishing an enabling environment for flood risk management through the development of a national flood risk information service, including information through the development of EWS and community-based GLOF risk reduction activities (Activity 2.2). The improvement in the enabling environment will also be supported by strengthening institutional capacity to provide training for government stakeholders (such as DMI and NDRIH), community members, CBOs and NGOs. This training will include climate change adaptation and disaster risk management concepts, flood risk management, integrated, climate-and gender-sensitive watershed and national park management plans. A key aspect of the enabling environment is the development of a national early warning system (EWS) for GLOF risk reduction. A detailed discussion on how the project will promote the transfer of ownership to local entities – as a concept that has been embedded in the project design.

## Potential for knowledge management and learning

144. The project includes numerous activities that will contribute to knowledge management and learning. First, the project will provide an opportunity to build upon existing knowledge on how to approach and undertake the highly complex task of take-backing to reduce the risk of GLOF events occurring. Lake lowering is technically difficult and has only been undertaken in a few locations around the world. Second, the project will contribute to building the capacity for implementing lake lowering interventions for the proposed project will help to ensure that learning is held in institutional memory. Third, the project will provide an opportunity to build on existing knowledge of how to implement hazard mapping and creating systems for data-basing climate-induced hazard, which will contribute to the implementation of the proposed project. Fourth, the project will provide opportunities for building capacity for implementing the design of the proposed project activities for mutual learning and knowledge sharing by working with communities in Nepal. Fifth, the project will provide an opportunity to build on existing knowledge about flood risk reduction interventions and EVS. Additionally, capacity development and training workshops will strengthen knowledge management and learning through the exchange of experiences between stakeholders and local communities. During implementation, opportunities for local and indigenous knowledge to be continually incorporated into the project design.

145. The knowledge and lessons generated under the project will be properly archived within DMI, GOH, the Nepalese Army, UNDP as well as the communities in the project areas. Additionally, relevant knowledge products and lessons with respect to climate change adaptation and disaster risk management will be shared through various platforms established under the project will support effective dissemination and utilization of information for future GLOF risk reduction interventions. The project will also contribute to building a strong institutional memory within DMI, GOH, the Nepalese Army, UNDP as well as the communities in the project areas. Through the project, with the strengthened regulatory and institutional mechanisms, will help maintain an enabling environment for flood risk management, thereby increasing the sustainability of this paradigm shift catalysed under the project.

## D. In support of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), approximately 1 page

146. The proposed project will contribute towards achieving 8 of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These include: SDG 2 – Zero Hunger; SDG 3 – Good Health and Well-being; SDG 5 – Gender Equality; SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 7 – Climate action; and SDG 10 – Life on Land. The contribution of the proposed project to these goals is summarized in Table 2 below. The project will also contribute towards achieving the following Sustainable Development Needs (SDNs) to achieve the country’s goals and objectives. First, by contributing to SDGs 11 and 13, the project will contribute to reducing disaster risk through climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction including: i) Disaster Risk Reduction and Management – To reduce human, economic, social, cultural, and environmental losses from disasters; ii) Climate Change Adaptation – To increase adaptive capacity of citizens and support the development of sustainable infrastructure through reliable hydrological and meteorological services. ## Environmental co-benefits

### 147.2 Mitigation of flood and impacts of a Global Lake Outburst Flood (GLOF) in Nepal, upgrading and expanding the country's existing hydrological and meteorological Early Warning Systems (EWS) and implementing climate-resilient land use practices to reduce vulnerability to GLOFs.

- The reforestation of riparian zones, planting of grass seedlings and revegetation of riparian areas will contribute to improved soil stability and consequently reduce erosion. This will improve the quality of soils and promote sustainable agriculture.
- The reforestation interventions will also have a mitigation co-benefit, as the 4.6 million trees and other vegetation planted in the target area will sequester atmospheric carbon.

### Preventing soil erosion, sequestering atmospheric carbon and creating environmental buffers, all of which will be beneficial to the communities living in the project area and provide additional environmental services in the target area.

- Improving and increasing agricultural and nutrient supply through conservation and erosion reduction interventions will also contribute to environmental provisioning services in Nepal.
- The benefits from the upgrading of EWS will be utilized by the agricultural sector for crop selection and harvest planning.

## Social and Economic co-benefits

### 147.3 Mitigation of flood and soil-lithified floods occurring and severely impacting vulnerable communities in Nepal will increase the resilience of these communities and provide several social and economic benefits to them.

- Decreasing the amount of stagnant water post-flood will reduce the prevalence of water and vector-borne diseases. This outcome will alleviate the burden on post-disaster emergency response services and local authorities.
- Involving community members living near and the target lakes in the construction process of lowering the target lake levels, as well as providing them with training to operate the new infrastructure, such as pumps and gates, in turn, will contribute to securing their lives and livelihoods under future climate change conditions.
- Improved access to information on weather patterns, early warning systems, disaster preparedness, and climate information services will reduce personal financial losses of community members. This is because communities will be better informed about the risks associated with GLOFs and can take appropriate measures in advance.

### Close engagements with Indigenous Peoples throughout the development of local early warning and response mechanisms will also ensure the preservation of Indigenous knowledge and promote inclusion of IPs in the decision-making process.

- The reduction of GLOF risk is a key element for the livelihoods of communities including those living on or near the target lakes, as well as those who depend directly or indirectly on the water resources and wetlands.
- The interventions will reduce the severity of GLOFs impacts on hydroelectric installations in the target area, benefiting the local economy. This will also provide a significant economic benefit to the country by reducing costs associated with hydropower installations posed by flood impacts but will only decrease costs of physical damage (primary project benefit). The secondary project benefits include reduced costs for emergency response and disaster recovery efforts, communication networks and local businesses to continue operating as normal during and immediately after an event.

#### D

**14. Gender-sensitive impact**

The gender-sensitive impacts of a GLOF will decrease the time required for economic sectors to become operational and for communities to recover immediately following GLOF or rain-induced flood events.

Improving the resilience of communities to climate change requires that the skills and knowledge required to lower the targets linked will embed the skills and knowledge of this process within the communities. This will not only improve their ability to respond effectively but also increase their capacity to adapt, including through employment — and subsequently livelihoods — of participating community members to undertake similar contributions in engineering and civil works in the future as the demand for such expertise increases under climate change conditions.

### Gender-sensitive impact

The gender-sensitive impacts of a GLOF will help to limit the disruptions to critical health, education and care services following these events. Given the gendered demographics of the employees and users of these services, and their vulnerability to climate change, the project will facilitate gender-sensitive development, as described below:

- Reducing disruptions to the provision of public building and the delivery of primary services will enable both women and men to access these services in a timely manner following GLOF or rain-induced flood events.
- The project activities under the Gender Action Plan (GAP) have outlined actions to promote gender equality in project implementation that include: i) development of a practical Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) working strategy guideline for all project staff; ii) integration of GESI into all project components; iii) promotion of participatory, inclusive and effective and meaningful decision-making on climate change induced disaster; iv) integration of GESI into Climate Change Adaptation (CCA), Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) and Flood Risk Management (FRM); v) facilitation of participatory GESI awareness workshops. There will also be integration of GESI in the CCA, DRR and GLOF risk assessments; vi) promotion of gender equality and social inclusion through the use of a gender-sensitive approach to make them responsive to the experiences of women and highly marginalized groups; v) integration of GESI into all project components; vii) facilitation of participatory GESI awareness workshops. There will also be integration of GESI in the CCA, DRR and GLOF risk assessments.
- The GAP activities will play a critical role in Community-Based Flood Early Warning Systems (CBFEWS) in Nepal. The GAP requires collaboration with these groups to promote GESI throughout all stages of the project, including through the gendered approach the project activities, including to increase decision making and leadership opportunities and engagement.

The project will also contribute to reducing factors contributing to gender inequality in the country, thereby contributing to gender-sensitive sustainability being part of its project lifetime.

### D.4 Needs of vulnerable (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### Vulnerability of the country

Nepal is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change-induced disasters, ranking within the top 10 countries most affected by climate change on the long-term global Climate Risk Index from 2009 to 2018. The country’s vulnerability is a result of several factors including: i) its geographical location; ii) its country’s vulnerability, including: (i) the country's fragile topology; (ii) climate sensitive livelihoods of the people; and iii) its low adaptive capacity.

#### Reports from Nepal’s Ministry of Home Affairs (MoHA) indicate that over the past 10 years, more than 4,000 people have died from climate change-induced disasters. Moreover, these disasters have resulted in accumulated economic losses of approximately USD 2 billion. The country has experienced a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, landslides, and droughts. This trend expected to continue under future climate change conditions. Such events have severe impacts on Nepal’s economy, including: i) loss of life; ii) economic losses; iii) displacement of people; iv) damage to infrastructure; v) exacerbation of the country's economic competition. For example, tourism makes up a large portion of the country's GDP, and flood disasters can cause significant damage by reducing tourist numbers or damaging vital infrastructure for these industries. This not only affects revenue generation but also leads to

## Vulnerability of beneficiary communities

### Socioeconomic status

133. Nepal's Human Development Index (HDI) value for 2019 was 0.622, which ranks it at 142 out of 189 countries and territories in the world. The HDI is a composite index that measures life expectancy, education attainment, and income per capita. From 1990 to 2019, major poverty reduction has been achieved in recent years; Nepal still ranks below many neighbors in terms of human development.

### Institutional capacity needs

154. The Government of Nepal (GoN) currently lacks technical capacity and institutional capacity to implement climate change adaptation strategies and reduce vulnerability measures. This results from national- and local-level decision-makers becoming locked into a cycle of reacting to GLOFs and other extreme events, as opposed to developing proactive adaptive strategies. To address this gap, the GoN has committed itself to:

* improving the country's GLOF EWS;
* developing comprehensive preparedness plans to facilitate proactive action in response to climate change impacts.

The project's alignment with these priorities and plans is described below.

vulnerability assessments; ii) promote a transition to a green economy; iv) mobilize national and international resources for climate change adaptation and mitigation; v) mainstream climate change issues into national and subnational development planning, policies, programs, and budgets; vii) enhance the effectiveness of climate change programs; and viii) increase the use of risk forecasting in planning for disaster risk reduction**. In particular, under Output 2 early warning systems (EWS), flood monitoring, and climate change adaptation and mitigation (CCAM) monitoring, response and information dissemination to communities vulnerable to GLOFs.

### The Country Programme Document for Nepal (2022-2027) outlines UNDP's contributions to Nepal's development priorities in the areas of poverty reduction, inclusive growth, social inclusion, gender equality, and increased climate adaptation and environmental resilience. Specifically, the document provides an overview of the country programme's strategic objectives, which are aligned with the Government of Nepal's Seventh Five-Year Plan (2017-2022) and the Fourthteen Development Plan. Key priority areas for the country programme include: i) to promote inclusive and sustainable growth; ii) to enhance social inclusion and gender equality; iii) to improve environmental resilience, sound policies that enhance productivity and investments and reduce multidimensional poverty; iv) improve the coherence of development planning at all levels; v) to strengthen the capacity of local governments and communities to participate in decision-making processes; vi) to promote access to justice and empower people, especially women and marginalized communities, to exercise agency over their lives through improved access to information and services; vii) to enhance the role of civil society organizations and active community participation**. The proposed project is well aligned with the country programme as various outputs will contribute towards achieving these objectives. For example, Output 1 will support institutions and active community participation**, including taking low-lying and diversion infrastructure, eco-DRR and EWS monitoring improvements. Furthermore, the project will also contribute to Output 3 by strengthening institutional capacity for improved planning and response to climate change risks through the development of a national early warning system (EWS) for GLOFs and other disaster information services**. This will include strengthening the institutional knowledge-base and developing policy and strategic frameworks for climate change adaptation.

### Nepal's National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) 2010 identifies the GoN's priority activities towards climate change adaptation. These priority goals, as related to flood risk reduction, include: i) promoting integrated water resources management; ii) improving disaster preparedness and response capacity at community level; iii) community-based disaster management for facilitating climate change adaptation; iv) GLOF monitoring and disaster risk reduction; v) strengthening the institutional framework for climate change adaptation and vulnerable communities through sustainable management of water resources and clean energy supply**. The project will contribute to these goals by: i) supporting integrated water resources management, disaster risk reduction and adaptation and community upliftment** by directly lowering flood and GLOF risk through taking low-lying and diversion infrastructure; ii) improving disaster preparedness and response capacity at the community level (Output 2); iii) supporting institutional knowledge and technical capacity for improved planning and response to climate change risks through the development of a national early warning system (EWS) for GLOFs and other disaster information services**.

### The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2015-2030 comprises six priority areas, which are aligned with Nepal's resilience to climate change by integrating adaptive measures across sectors and levels of government. The process of developing Nepal's NAP included engagement with a wide range of stakeholders from the national level down to local communities, as well as participation in regional and cross-sectoral dialogues**. In addition, the NAP aligns with critical international frameworks, including the Paris Agreement on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030, the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCDD), and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030. The NAP also aligns with national strategies such as the National Forest Conservation Act; water resources and energy; rural and urban settlements; and its disaster risk reduction and management policy, which all contribute to climate change adaptation and resilience in Nepal's context of managing climate risks. The priorities of Nepal's NAP comprises programmes in eight thematic and four cross-cutting actions: a) options, stock, medium- and long-term time frames. The proposed project is aligned with Nepal's NAP.

160. Nepal's Third National Communication (TNC) to the UNFCCC (2021[2]) presents an overview on the progress made in implementing its NDCs for climate change mitigation and adaptation, with a focus on three priority areas which includes an indication of the country’s current greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory. The adaptation priorities include: i) strengthening early warning systems by improving the observation network density and implementing early warning systems (Output 2); ii) increasing resilience to extreme events such as GLOFs and monsoon floods; iii) promoting climate-resilient livelihoods in high-risk areas.

161. In its Second Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC), Nepal identifies mitigating GHG emissions and undertaking studies and actions for adaptation as critical actions to reduce the impacts of climate change on vulnerable communities, especially those living in remote mountainous regions with limited natural resources and technical capacities for scaling up climate action. While this project will not directly contribute to reducing GHG emissions, it is expected that the implementation of the proposed project will have a cascading effect by increasing the resilience of communities against extreme events such as GLOFs which are causing the accelerated rate of glacial melt, increasing GLOF risk. The proposed project is aligned with the NDC, which includes: i) strengthening early warning systems; ii) promoting climate-resilient livelihoods in high-risk districts in Nepal – six as very highly vulnerable, nine more as highly vulnerable and seven as moderately vulnerable to extreme events.

162. Nepal Disaster Risk Reduction National Strategic Plan of Action (2018–2030). This action plan was prepared by the Govt of Nepal with the endorsement of Nepal National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Council. The plan is aligned with the UNISDR’s Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030, which serves as a global policy framework for disaster risk reduction (DRR). The plan aims to reduce disaster risks by: i) strengthening understanding of disaster risk through multiple assessment types; ii) strengthening multi-level disaster risk management and governance structures at national, regional, and local levels; iii) enhancing disaster preparedness and integrating “build back better” approaches during recovery from extreme events. Moreover, the plan also addresses disaster risk reduction in five key sectors: i) floods; ii) landslides; iii) earthquakes; iv) cyclones; v) droughts.

163. The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015–2030)[3] – of which the Govt is committed to implementing – aims to substantially reduce disaster risk and losses in lives, livelihoods, and health as well as in the economic, social, cultural, and environmental dimensions on an inclusive and equitable basis. The framework identifies four priority actions to achieve this goal: i) Priority 1 – Understanding disaster risk; ii) Priority 2 – Identifying strategies for reducing disaster risks at all levels; iii) Priority 3 – Strengthening disaster risk governance at national, regional and global levels to ensure effective and equitable disaster risk reduction in line with the Sendai Framework; iv) Priority 4 – Enhancing disaster resilience in communities through education and awareness raising.

#### Stakeholder engagement

The project has been developed in a participatory way in collaboration with stakeholders from relevant Nepalese government ministries and departments, civil society organisations (CSOs) and private sector entities. The Ministry of Water Resources and Environment (MoWRE), the Ministry of Finance (MoF), and the National Trust for Nature Conservation (NTNC) are key partners involved in the overall management of GCF engagement in Nepal and has been engaged throughout the project development process.

All entities engaged during project preparation, including the MoWRE, MoF, and NTNC, further information can be found at Annex 2. 

- **The Ministry of Water Resources and Environment (MoWRE):** The MoWRE is the Executive Entity of the proposed project and has been actively involved in the GEF project advisory council - Project Coordination Committee providing support for the project. This ministry also houses the DRM, one of the project implementing units (PIUs).
- **The Ministry of Finance (MoF):** As NDA for the proposed project, the MoF is involved in project oversight and implementation.
- **The International Centre for Integrated Mountain Development (ICIMOD):** This pan-Himalayan research institute has been engaged throughout the project development process. ICIMOD brings its extensive expertise on glacier and high-mountain regions to the project development team. ICIMOD plans to work closely with the MoWRE, MoF, and NTNC in the implementation of the proposed project, including glacier mapping support, and enable capacity development for DRM and other GCF stakeholders.
- **The Department of Forests and Soil Conservation (DoFSC):** The DoFSC will also be an implementing partner along with DRM and has been consulted on linkages with this project and ongoing forest-related projects.

Hydropower companies and numerous coordinating organisations have readily participated in stakeholder workshops, including the Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA), National Hydropower Association of Nepal (NHAN), and the Department of Hydroelectricity Development (DHD). The DoFSC, also under the MoWRE, will be an implementation partner along with DRM and has been consulted on linkages with this project and ongoing forest-related projects.

The National Trust for Nature Conservation (NTNC) and Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC): These are two key partners in Nepal that have been engaged throughout the project development process. They will support implementation processes to enable capacity development in the country.
- **National Disaster Risk Management Committee (NDRMC):** The NDRMC is a coordinating body with DRM, and will be a responsible party and coordinate with the Local Disaster Management Committees (LDMCs).
- **Nepal Forest Research Institute (NRFI):** NRFI has been engaged in consultations and discussions with indigenous peoples from specific villages of the Koshi and Gandaki river basins. These consultations were conducted with indigenous peoples through their representatives, including the National Indigenous People’s Forum (NIPF).

### 163. The project was developed following an extensive stakeholder engagement process, beginning with initial institutional and community consultations held during the first phase of project design. These engagements were conducted in January to March 2015. The focus of these engagements was on identifying the broad needs and vulnerabilities within the project’s target areas, which informed the development of the project concept note. Subsequent stakeholder engagement activities were conducted focusing on defining priorities for project formulation. Similar to the initial phase, stakeholders consultations were held in January to March 2016. These consultations were designed to ensure that all relevant sector entities would validate the design of the project and ensure alignment with national priorities. More than 400 representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations (CSOs), and communities participated to adequately capture the needs of communities. In this context, stakeholder engagement was primarily intended:
    * to ensure that the needs and concerns of all stakeholders were captured in the project design and implementation.
    * to enable CSOs and other partners to leverage their networks for the project.
    * to identify a range of gender-related development impacts of climate change projects at the target sites and communities.
    * to support responsible parties in preparing gender assessments and action plans, and
    * to ensure all stakeholders, especially women, are effectively involved and equally represented throughout the project design and implementation.

### 164. In addition to these consultations, UNDP is working with two national organizations specializing in Indigenous Peoples’ rights and gender equality to ensure that the needs of indigenous communities and women are captured throughout the project area. This includes a three-day Consultation Workshop with indigenous peoples from January to February 2015, which was held at the National Museum in Lusaka. The workshop aimed to collect additional information. Detailed consultations were also held as part of the gender assessment process. These included focus group discussions (FGDs) and key informant interviews (KIIs). Other stakeholders such as GLIF/C/GLOF-CC-related personnel/mentors, partners and community people (government, NGOs, CSOs, etc.) participated in these consultations. The results from these consultations were shared with all stakeholders throughout the project area. These consultations were carried out with women and men from diverse groups, with efforts to target individuals who were not included in previous rounds of consultations. More detailed information on the gender assessment process is provided in the relevant IPF and Gender annexes. More recently, several further engagements were held in August 2016 as part of the National Environmental Impact Assessment (NEIA) and National Environment Impact Review (NEIR). During these consultations, support was shown for the general project design, while stressing the need for urgent action to address climate change impacts on water resources. The consultations also highlighted actions that should be followed during implementation – including continued engagement and FPC processes, as described in Annex 7: Project Governance and Institutional Arrangement. In addition, specific recommendations were made to ensure inclusive involvement of indigenous communities in the project, including conducting further gender assessments.

    * Loss of lives averted;
    * damage to water resources averted;
    * loss of agricultural produce averted;
    * damage to hydropower projects averted; and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

## Loss of water income averted.

170. The decrease in damages was calculated by comparing the expected amount of damage or losses in the no-intervention scenario, to the expected amount of damage in the post-intervention scenario. The damage numbers were based on the results from the HEC-RAS model and the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF) Water Resources Management Assessment. To design optimal interventions downstream of the four glacial lakes, experts modeled the expected damages associated with a breach of each lake using the Hydrologic Engineering Center’s River Analysis System (HEC-RAS) for the modeling, and reported on the expected damages sustained by communities in the event of a breach. The model assumed that the dam would be breached at the point where the water level rises to the recommended crest of 1.3 meters and a complete breach of the moraine dam; a partial breach was not considered as it is unlikely given the size of the lake and the height of the moraine dam. The model suggested downstream measures in place, to assess the effectiveness of the suggested risk reduction measures.

171. This data on damages in different scenarios was used to work out the expected benefit, in terms of loss averted, for each intervention scenario. The benefits were calculated by comparing the expected damage under no-intervention and hydropower projects. The value of lives was taken as the Value of a Statistical Life (VSL) proportional to the US proportionality to the VSL.

172. It isn’t known when a GLOF would occur with any certainty. Therefore, the benefit was normalized by factoring in the probability of a GLOF occurring at any point in time during the approval time horizon of 25 years. The annual probability of a GLOF occurrence for each lake was calculated using data from published and reviewed papers, and ICMOD documents for recorded GLOFs that had the potential of causing damage downstream. These probabilities were then used to calculate an expected benefit for each intervention scenario.

173. The probability was also calculated from return period data from a paper published in the journal: “Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences”. This method uses historical records of glacial lake outburst floods (GLOFs) and statistical methods to infer lakes volume from lake areas known through satellite data and that in simulated scenarios, the annual probability was calculated by dividing 1 by the return period. The assumption is that if interventions take are located at Eastern Himalayas, the return period on flood volumes was matched with the expected benefits of the intervention scenario. This method calculates a separate annual probability for each GLOF event.

174. By multiplying the annual probability with the value of expected benefit from a GLOF event occurs in the absence of an intervention and compared with the expected losses after the intervention, a value was assigned to the benefits of pursuing the interventions under this proposed programme. This was compared with costs (and other factors).
