## Application of best practices for the proposed project

The following lessons learned were used to inform the design of GLOF risk reduction interventions to be implemented under the proposed project. Lessons learned from numerous baseline assessments and vulnerability studies conducted in Nepal are summarized below.

### International best practices and lessons learned identified for the project include:

- drawing on the technical capacity of the Nepalese Army and DRM to undertake the planning and implementation of the project;
- constructing check and detention dams to manage high volumes of water in high-risk flood areas by detaining the water upstream, thereby reducing downstream flooding risks;
- installing protective transverse and longitudinal structures (including gabion spurds, flood walls, RCC revetments, etc.) along vulnerable riverbanks to protect infrastructure from GLOF impacts on low-risk communities, infrastructure and land;
- implementing vegetation management practices such as tree planting along vulnerable riverbanks, vegetative galion walls, vegetative gabion retention vegetation, vegetative check dams, vegetative stone rip-rap, bush levées, etc., in high-risk areas to reduce the risk of GLOF impacts on low-risk communities and infrastructure;
- ensuring effective operations and maintenance (O&M) by establishing SOPs for GLOF risk reducing systems and providing training to project staff and local communities on O&M procedures. This will also include community-based early warning systems, nature-based solutions and the database for climate-induced hazards;
- using remote sensing via satellite tracking to track interannual changes in glacial lakes.


---
**Page 63**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

- integrating meteorological stations and high-altitude meteorological stations and integrating existing hydrology stations into a real-time data acquisition system to expand the reach and usability of monitoring systems;
- developing an early warning system for climate-related hazards, including for hazard prioritization and mapping, and geophysical, topographic and bathymetric surveys;
- developing community-based disaster risk reduction plans and location-based response methods that are co-developed to increase the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities;
- establishing a national focal point for climate change adaptation in collaboration with key stakeholders by establishing multi-hazard early warning systems that include centralised and decentralised monitoring;
- supporting the development of institutional memory by establishing systems for recording and managing climate-related data;
- developing climate risk knowledge products from public and private sector staff to support disaster management and capacity development.

## Sustainability

Some investments have been made into GLOF risk reduction measures in Nepal but have generally lacked the long-term engagement and institutional arrangements needed to catalyze broader investment for sustainable lake management and flood mitigation. For example, there has been a lack of engagement with communities to mobilize further investment in risk reduction measures beyond the immediate vicinity of existing and future interventions—not only to increase their capacity for monitoring, early warning and downstream flood mitigation.

### Low

Regularly updated knowledge on lake-level dynamics and climate change adaptation measures are integrated into the work plans of national and sub-national institutions. Policies have been established to formalize and institutionalize the management and mobilization investment for risk reduction in Nepal to complement the existing institutional arrangements building Communities are actively involved in the planning, implementation, monitoring, early warning and downstream flood mitigation.

The involvement of government institutions and Nepalese army in GLOF risk reduction is recognized. However, there is a need that learning is held at institutional memory to benefit future GLOF risk reduction measures.

Beyond the selection of knowledge and experience, the project will actively invest in improved risk management systems related to GLOFs, including early warning systems. This will include institutionalizing spatial digital databases of glaciers and lakes, as well as improving the capacity of local communities to use the status of glacial lakes to ensure decisions are based on the latest data. The project will also support the development of a database that contributes to the long-term of technical flood risk reduction knowledge making.

Broadening private sector engagement will be facilitated by improving DMH's capacity to develop and disseminate climate risk information to private sector stakeholders, including through public-private partnerships. This will include making policy provisions underpinned by an enabling environment for private sector investment in GLOF risk reduction measures.

Institutionalize long-term agreements to support the sustainable uptake of GLOF risk reduction in Nepal.

Multidirectional knowledge sharing with communities in the implementation, operation and maintenance of lake lowering (deglaciation) projects will be supported. This approach will further enhance the role of knowledge in long-term sustainability.

**Scenario I: Annualized Probability of a GLOF at any one of the four lakes**

**Scenario II: Return Period based Probability calculated for GLOF at each lake using return period data from a published study (Hazard from Himalayan glaciers – see Annex 6b). This method used statistical modeling to estimate extremes from lake areas known through field surveys and subsequently simulated several thousand events in terms of the sizes and frequency of future GLOFs. The annual probability from this study was then applied to the four target lakes and was converted into an annual probability.**

The full details of the probabilities, scenarios, and calculations are presented in Annex 4: Technical and Economic Appraisal.

**Page 81**

Second, methods and tools will be introduced into DMF’s cooperation procedures for GLOF hazard reduction and response to other extreme events. This hazard prioritization will use the same criteria as ground-truthing and socio-economic vulnerability assessments.

Finally, methodologies and SDPs for implementing integrated GLOF risk management in Nepal will be developed and informed by the GIS database and hazard mapping that will also be undertaken under this project. The methods will include Remote Sensing and GIS and Snow Survey and Glacier Lake Department expertise to support DMF’s work on GLOF risk prioritization, as well as how to use the risk information generated for decision-making in terms of investment in high-risk areas. Given the methods and tools established will be robust and sustainable, DMF is committed to continuing to provide training to its partners and other stakeholders within the Glacier Lake Departments on the methods and tools established under this project. The training will include a mix of face-to-face and e-learning. Training will also include training on priority work in its annual programme. Overall, this project will contribute to the implementation of Nepal’s Disaster Risk Reduction National Strategy, 2014, as well as the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act, 2014 and Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Regulation, 2015, which are the main guiding policy documents for disaster response in the country.

This project activity will contribute to improving GLOF risk flood hazard management through developing climate-influenced hazard event recording and management database systems. The operational risk information products used by productive sectors (tourism, and hydropower) will be developed and disseminated. This will include public-private partnerships to generate long-term flood and risk reduction services.

Develop private-public partnerships for sustainable financing and incentives for investment in GLOF risk reduction.

has been identified. While GHG producer sector-specific user-friendly climate information in the aviation, agriculture and tourism sectors, there is a need to expand the provision of tailored climate information for the power generation sector. The expansion will be supported by training GHG staff on the generation, recording and maintenance of GHG data. This will ensure that the most updated climate risk information disseminated to all production units in the power sector. To support this, targeted productive users, user statistics will be registered on GHG website and will be used for updating the user databases. Other opportunities to ensure use of the information have been identified through the provision of socio-economic data to appropriate responsible agencies and developing customized climate risk information products.


To support the sustainability of GCF/UNFCCC reduction strategies in Report 02, a national greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reporting system should be established. Private sector actors in the hydropower, tourism, aviation, agriculture and other sectors will be identified as appropriate partners, and most of the private and government agencies will be involved in this process. The Ministry of Environment also the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation. The Department of Meteorology, National Climate Change Authority, Department of Water Resources and Irrigation and Private Sector Development Authority will be involved. Appropriate provisions will be made, and extensive stakeholder consultations will be held to ensure that the system is sustainable. This will allow sectors to formalise and institutionalise long-term agreements to support the development of a national GHG emissions reporting system. Risk information for pre-project adaptation planning and early warning systems can also be used by private sector actors as an incentive for investments in investment areas. While access to such information are a function of the level of risk, it is important that appropriate risk information for such investment partners can be incentives for co-investment. The Ministry of Environment will provide technical support for pre-project adaptation planning and knowledge dissemination workshops for stakeholders.

In response, the following will take place under the project activities:

A. A total of 24 automatic weather stations (AWS) will be installed at key locations in the Manang, Kaski, Lamjung, Gorkha, Jajarkot, Chitwan, Dhanterai and Arun Sub-basins. These AWSs will be installed at key locations in the Dhulikhel Sub-basin (2) and in the Arun Sub-basin.

B. 24 automatic weather stations (AWS) will be installed at key locations in the Manang, Kaski, Lamjung, Gorkha, Jajarkot, Chitwan, Dhanterai and Arun Sub-basins. These AWSs will be installed at key locations in the Dhulikhel Sub-basin (2) and in the Arun Sub-basin.

C. 9 Precipitation gauges (PCG) will be installed at key locations in the Manang, Kaski, Lamjung, Gorkha, Jajarkot, Chitwan, Dhanterai and Arun Sub-basins to measure precipitation data.

The real-time data acquired from this critical network will be used for T2 (hydrological modeling), T3 (flood forecasting), T4 (EWS), and (5) climate modeling. Additionally, the data will be used in the development of a hydro-meteorological database for existing and future projects.

### 3.1 Lowering levels of four of the highest risk glacial lakes

#### Detailed engineering designs for lowering water levels will be undertaken for the lowering of the Thulug, Lower Barun, Lunting Taro and Hongri-2 lakes informed by the following assessments and validated by expert opinion.

* **A topographical survey** of the site and the moraine dam is needed to determine the current volume of water in each lake.
* **Bathymetric surveys** to determine the most recent volume of water, the depth of the lake, its extension, and end moraines change.
* **Hydrological surveys** for maximum discharge using the most basic method, the floating method. In the Thulug Lake, the floating method is not used. Therefore, to design the more detailed structural works, a hydrograph survey using a GPR survey, and an electric resistivity tomography of the site are required.
* **Once designs have been validated**, construction will commence with pre-construction actions for lakes: Water levels of the Thulug, Lower Barun, Lunting Taro and Hongri-2 lakes lowered by 3 m.

## Under Activity 2.3: Eco-disaster Risk Reduction (Eco-DRR) solutions will be implemented at strategic locations in the Dudhikot Sub-basin to reduce the risk of flash floods and landslides, while simultaneously contributing to the restoration and rehabilitation of degraded ecosystems.

The interventions that will be implemented under this project activity include:

1. Planting 532 trees along vulnerable embankments, sub-banks, and check dams in the Dudhikot Sub-basin.
2. Planting 46 million trees across vulnerable embankments, sub-banks, and check dams in the Dudhikot Sub-basin.
3. Establishing 17,744 m² of vegetative gully walls in the Arun Sub-basin (20,894 m²) and Dudhikot Sub-basin (5,650 m²).
4. Establishing 2,420 m³ of vegetative ground cover in the Arun Sub-basin.
5. Establishing 13,724 m³ of vegetative gully traps in the Arun Sub-basin and Dudhikot Sub-basin (6,893 m³).
6. Establishing 10,000 live check dams in the Arun Sub-basin.
7. Establishing 5,647 live check dams in the Dudhikot Sub-basin.

The interventions will be implemented under this project activity include:

1. Planting a total of 298,162 m² of vegetative gully check dams in the Arun Sub-basin and Dudhikot Sub-basin (150,419 m²).
2. Planting 294,162 m³ of grass seedlings.
3. Establishing a total of 7,284 m³ of vegetative gully traps in the Arun Sub-basin and Dudhikot Sub-basin (3,500 m³).

The interventions will be implemented under this project activity include:

1. Planting a total of 29,655 m² of vegetative check dams in the Arun Sub-basin.
2. Establishing a total of 15,000 live check dams in the Dudhikot Sub-basin and a total of 8,638 m³ of grass seedlings.

179. Monitoring and evaluation of project activities will be undertaken on an annual basis. The type of monitoring activities to be conducted during the implementation phase, as well as the responsible parties for undertaking these activities, are detailed in Annex 11: Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan. Such activities include field observations, interviews, and document reviews. These activities will be used to assess project performance against its targets at the project’s mid-term and final evaluations. The results of such monitoring activities will inform the project evaluations as presented in Annex 4: Detailed budget.

180. A project inception workshop will be held after project approval and when the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) becomes effective and UNDP document has been signed by the Project Manager, supported by the Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, and Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist. The purpose of this workshop is to: i) present the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that may influence project implementation; ii) review the Logical Framework (LF), including assumptions, indicators, targets, outputs, activities, resources, risks, constraints, and mandatory requirements, and agree on the arrangements for the annual audit; and iii) plan and schedule Project Board meetings and finalise the first year annual work plan for the project. The minutes of this workshop will be prepared by the Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist, and Project Manager.

181. An Annual Performance Report (APR) will be prepared by the Project Manager, UNDP Country Office, and UNDP Regional Technical Advisor for submission to the GCF Project Board and relevant project stakeholders. The Project Manager, supported by the Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, will ensure that all the indicators included in the performance monitoring framework are tracked against the targets set out in Annex 10: Performance Monitoring Framework. These indicators will be presented in the GCF APR. Any challenges and delays experienced during the implementation phase will be monitored by the Project Manager and reported in the GCP APR.

28. During the implementation phase, specific means of verification will be used to assess that: i) progress of project interventions towards achieving GCF Fund-level impacts and outputs is being made; ii) the project is on track with respect to its targets; iii) the project is meeting all mandatory requirements as set out in Annex 10: Performance Monitoring Framework. The Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist will be employed to conduct and coordinate on-the-ground monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of the project’s performance against its targets. This will be achieved by: i) conducting regular field visits to monitor progress; ii) using a performance monitoring framework to track the project's progress towards achieving its targets. This will be achieved by: iii) measuring the performance against the project indicators set out in Annex 10: Performance Monitoring Framework, and iv) providing technical support to the Project Unit (PU). The Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist will also provide technical assistance to the Project Manager on the preparation of the GCF APR. Additionally, Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, and Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist will be responsible for: i) providing technical support to the Project Unit (PU); and ii) providing technical support to the Project Manager. Additionally, Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, and Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist will undertake evidence-gathering exercises to verify the project’s performance against its targets as set out in Annex 10: Performance Monitoring Framework. The results of such monitoring activities will inform the project evaluations as presented in Annex 4: Detailed budget. These reports will be submitted by the Monitoring, Evaluation and Training Specialist, Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist, supported by the Project Manager.

183. UNDP will be responsible for managing the independent Interim (mid-term) and Final Evaluations of the project, including the process for contracting these positions. For the Interim evaluation, an independent consultant will be contracted to provide an assessment of project performance against its targets at the project’s mid-term. This will be an outcomes-based assessment and will include analyzing whether the project is on track, what problems and challenges the project is encountering, and what corrective

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Turnover of key project personnel, as well as new staff at technical and local governments, could delay project implementation and negatively impact the retention and transfer of institutional knowledge gained under the project. Staff in key government institutions may lack the capacity to implement effectively and on time. Staffing challenges may be further compounded by limited opportunities for work at high altitudes.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Regular stakeholder consultations will be held with all project partners involved in project implementation to ensure the institutional capacity is retained in the event of turnover of key personnel. In addition, the PSC will be responsible for identifying and training a core group of staff who can provide technical support to the project. The PSC will identify personnel whose primary responsibilities will be to act as a focal point for facilitating stakeholder engagement with the project team. A capacity building plan will also be developed in consultation with the PSC to develop project staff with standardized training on project interventions. UNDP, as AE, will provide technical assistance and support to the PSC in developing this plan.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

The occurrence of a GLCP, other flooding event, earthquake or other natural disaster or climate change-related event could negatively impact the project's ability to implement interventions and negatively impact project areas and target communities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Project interventions will be continuously monitored to identify potential extreme events for appropriate measures to be enacted should such an event occur. Operational manuals will also be developed to identify the necessary mitigation measures that can be implemented in case of a GLCP or other natural disaster. A contingency plan as possible to project interventions, disaster mitigation and response activities will be prioritized at the target communities. Project staff will be trained on how to implement these plans. The project team and partners will consider historical trends regarding the frequency of extreme climate events and will plan around these periods accordingly. In addition, a risk reduction strategy will be developed in consultation with the PSC for implementation where possible. Downstream risk reduction interventions will be conducted after taking and EVA into consideration. A disaster response plan will also be created to address potential risks, sign off by all parties involved, and actions to take in the event of an emergency.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 90

## Technical and operational Late Medium

### Description
Delays during the procurement process could disrupt the efficient implementation of project interventions.

### Mitigation Measure (6)
UNDP will assist GMD with the procurement process to ensure that the processes proceed according to international standards, and to ensure that the procurement process is completed quickly and efficiently. Moreover, UNDP's experience in the adaptation field will enable them to directly approach potential partners should the initial rounds of the procurement process prove unsuccessful.

Additional mitigation actions intended to avoid procurement delay include: (i) adherence procurement planning and approval procedures; (ii) completion of annual work plan of the project; and (iii) direct working with the entities who have gone through competitive process and the government or UNDP for long-term agreements with the entities. The current GCF Board approved procurement process has been adapted in procurement as set by standard procurement rules of the government and UNDP. All procurements are subject to a pre-qualification stage, which is followed by an open tendering process based on pre-set criteria, which is part of procurement notice. All project transactions are mandatory audited by the government and UNDP as per set audit rates every year which also includes financial management.

Given the relevance of EWS, weather stations and measuring equipment and civil works related to the project's implementation, UNDP will work with GMD to ensure that the procurement process is expedited. UNDP Nepal and Bureau of Management Office of Procurement/Orbis Climate and Infrastructure Team soon after securing GCF Board approval for the project will start working on the procurement plan and tender documents in line with GCF rules. In addition, UNDP can also provide specialized support for infrastructural works and lessons learned through other GCF projects of similar nature.

### Selected Risk Factor 4
#### Category: Governance
##### Probability: Low
##### Impact: Medium

**Description**
The Government of Nepal's transition from a unitary government system to a federal democratic republic over the past decade could alter political priorities. This shift could lead to project stakeholders diverting their efforts away from project implementation. Similar risks may emerge following elections, when key government stakeholders might change.

### Mitigation Measure (7)
The Government of Nepal's transition from a unitary government system to a federal democratic republic means that the mandates, capacities and interagency relationships of governmental institutions and mechanisms are still being established. To ensure that the project is not adversely affected by this transition, detailed information on the roles, responsibilities and coordination mechanisms of government operations, the project will be developed in close collaboration with the NDA. The PSC will engage directly with the NDA to ensure close communication incorporates, but are not solely reliant on, government agencies. This approach aims to minimise the impact of any potential political instability or collapse. The PSC can engage directly with the NDA to ensure close communication with government officials and to identify and address potential delays or changes in project management or interventions.

### Selected Risk Factor 5
#### Category: Technical and operational
##### Probability: Medium
##### Impact: Medium

**Description**
Potential risks related to the implementation of project interventions in the remote project target areas include:
- Difficulty accessing project areas for installation and maintenance of equipment.
- ESS risks related to altering river flow.

## Selected Risk Factor 9
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | Low | High |

### Description
Risks may arise related to procurement and management of construction works linked to lake lowering and FWS projects. These include: (a) potential for cost overruns, quality issues, and price manipulation; (b) contractors lack capacity/experience to deliver on time and in budget; and (c) contracts not following the procedures laid out in the ESMP.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
UNDP, as the accredited entity, will ensure that fair and transparent procurement processes are followed, and that a robust quality assurance process for the procurement of technical suppliers will include inputs from a suitably qualified technical advisor. This will be supported by the TAG. The UNDP will also support the development of a quality assurance process for the procurement of technical suppliers will include inputs from a suitably qualified technical advisor, who will sit on the PMU, with support from members of the TAG. This includes screening processes and selection criteria to ensure that only firms meeting the required standards are selected.

## Selected Risk Factor 10
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description
The project may face challenges in maintaining financial sustainability and ensuring compliance with evolving environmental regulations, and limited infrastructure development has already had negative impacts on the Nepalese economy. The situation and the challenges faced related to adapting to changing political priorities during federal transition.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
(a) Nepal's priorities to build resilience against climate change (CC) risks prevails all other priorities, including economic development, which will continue despite changes in political landscape; as risk reduction remains at the top of the national agenda. The project will be aligned with the government’s CC priorities and will support near future empowering sub-national governments who are expected to play a more significant role in risk management.

(b) Nepal has been a signatory to several international agreements on climate change, including environment conservation regulations supported by protected areas law, which has remained stable in comparison to other environmental regulation-related decisions at national level monitoring of Co2/CH4 fluxes and sharing this information with concerned authorities has been embedded in the regular work stream of GMA funded from the government budget.

Risks related to money laundering (ML), terrorist financing (TF), and prohibited practices (PP) could undermine the integrity of project financing, leading to misallocation of funds, reputational damage, and potential legal repercussions. Weak financial oversight in remit or high-risk areas may also expose the project to illicit financial flows, impacting effective implementation and sustainability.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Environmental impacts

Environmental risks are primarily associated with the project linked to potential impacts associated with the installation of equipment, flood mitigation structures and construction surrounding take lowering measures. Further environmental risks include those associated with the use of chemicals and fertilizers for each catchment will also be prepared prior to commencement of construction.

To limit the potential damage caused by monsoon rains, all construction activities will take place during the dry season. The project team will monitor the progress of these activities and undertake any remedial action as needed to reduce the environmental impacts. To reduce the potential for a negative visual impact on the landscape, local materials will be used where possible in the construction of flood mitigation structures.

### Social Impacts

The main potential adverse social impacts associated with the project include the potential discrimination or exclusion of certain groups such as women and indigenous peoples from the benefits of the project. Indigenous Peoples of the mountain communities in the proposed project sites and the broader Himalayan region have deep cultural ties to their land, which is a critical component for sustainable livelihoods and resilience. They play a crucial role in shaping sustainable climate resilience strategies through local actions. Practices such as community-based forest management, water resource management, and traditional knowledge on sustainable resource use can provide valuable insights into Glacier Lake Outburst Flood (GLOF) risk reduction, nature-based solutions to climate change adaptation, and disaster risk reduction.

Community communication practices also enhance Early Warning Systems by complementing scientific methods with localized expertise. The project team will work closely with the communities in the proposed project sites to ensure that they are managed through implementation of the Indigenous Peoples Engagement Framework that has been prepared. In addition, the project team will engage with local communities and indigenous peoples throughout planning and implementation, to ensure they are not adversely impacted by proposed measures, such as like lowering measures or construction activities for flood mitigation structures in the proposed project sites' three catchments. Activities that could trigger the need for FPC have been identified in the IPPF. Organizations like the Nepal Federation of Indigenous Nationalities (NEFIN) and the National Indigenous Women's Federation


---
**Page 98**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95

**(NWF)** are actively involved in engaging Indigenous communities, ensuring their knowledge is systematically integrated and that they have a say in project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting to ensure appropriate, community-driven, and ecologically sustainable solutions for long-term climate resilience. It should be noted that for no activities will indigenous people be relocated from their land or have traditional access to land removed.

### Activities

133. There are activities within the project that could represent gender risks e.g., uneven representation of women, girls, and men in decision-making processes, lack of awareness about gender equality, and limited opportunities for empowerment and gender equity within the frameworks of the Project. The Plan includes detailed measures that target these gender risks and ensure that they are addressed through a gender-sensitive approach to reduce vulnerabilities. Further to this, participation from women, Indigenous groups, Data communities and other marginalized groups is ensured in all aspects of project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting.

134. In addition to the above, the GCF Action Plan includes a GCE/SEAH Action plan to address SEAH-related risks and the grievance redress mechanism includes specific measures for women and girls.

### Country Partnership

As mentioned above, country partnership is prioritized as critical to the sustainability of the project. Similarly, the importance of Indigenous traditional practices, knowledge and skills in relation to natural disasters — including extreme weather conditions such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, and storms — is recognized. Addressing water conditions downstream is also necessary, as water is traditionally considered sacred in Dolakha, Manang and other parts of Nepal.

### Project Implementation

135. The project will involve a wide range of stakeholders over large areas; therefore, multi-level systematic engagement will be required if all stakeholders are to be effectively communicated with and mis-information avoided. It is important that the project team engages with these stakeholders in an inclusive manner. Engaging these to all stakeholders in terms that are readily understood is critical to the success of this project. Further, the various stakeholder groups have different levels of understanding about climate change and its impacts.

136. A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) has been developed for this project and is described in it or elsewhere in the EPM. Through the GRM, stakeholders can lodge complaints with UNDP and GCF staff, allowing for a timely response to stakeholder complaints or grievances towards the project to communicate their concerns through an appropriate channel. The GRM also provides a platform for dialogue between the project team and affected communities and effective response to concerned stakeholders, in particular any vulnerable groups that may lack access to formal grievance mechanisms.

137. A Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Assessment plan (500 words, approximately 1 page) has been developed for the project to help guide the engagement process.

### Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Assessment

#### Country Context

**Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Assessment — in line with the objectives of the GCF’s Gender Policy and gender guidelines—has been developed by UNDP to inform the design of the proposed project (Annex B). The assessment is based on a review of existing literature, including the results from previous studies (e.g., discussions [FGCs] and two multi-stakeholder national level workshops) — as well as available data from studies conducted in Nepal. The assessment also draws upon lessons learned from other GCF projects and development banks.

#### Gender Assessment

138. The gender assessment indicates the extent to which gender inequality exists in Nepal. The country has a moderately high gender gap index (GGI), ranking 147 out of 159 countries in 2018. Gender inequalities and vulnerability are exacerbated by climate change in multiple, nuanced and overlapping ways in Nepal. Gender norms and practices that limit women’s access to resources and opportunities for economic empowerment, as well as assets for women in fewer livelihood options available to women, making them more vulnerable to climate change, conflict, political instability, natural disasters, and post-disaster relief.

#### Key Vulnerable Groups

139. Other key vulnerable groups identified in the GESI assessment include Dalits and Indigenous Dalits — distinguished from Dalits according to the Hindu social structure prevalent in the Terai and Pahadi regions—are considered to be the most excluded and vulnerable group in the target area. Indigenous groups, particularly those living in remote areas, are also considered as a highly vulnerable group due to their limited access to resources and services.

140. Within these different marginalized groups, women, women-headed households, the elderly, people with disabilities, and children are considered to be the most vulnerable sub-groups that will be disproportionately affected by climate change and extreme climate events. Other groups vulnerable to climate change and natural disasters are

These groups are often excluded from climate change adaptation efforts, both historical and recent inadequate consideration and integration of indigenous groups in resilience efforts.**

200. In line with the GCF’s gender priorities, and the recommendations put forward by the GESI experts following consultations with the project team, this proposal will ensure that women and marginalized communities are fully integrated into all aspects of the project design, including: (i) mainstreaming gender equality in the project design; (ii) ensuring equal participation of women and marginalized groups in the development of the project’s climate change-related committed planning structures; (iii) ensuring that the project promotes the empowerment and value of women (VVs) and their enterprises in the Khas Basin area for women and men from poor and marginalized groups; (iv) ensuring that women and marginalized communities are fully engaged with and represented through gender-based working groups about the project and local government resources; (v) ensuring that the Gender Monitoring Information System (MIS) of the project for feedback and improved upscaling purposes.

climate risk and DRR management strategies with systematic integration of GESI for the project cycle, and capacity building packages for project staff and key partners with a GESI focus.

201. These measures will ensure equal participation and maximise benefits from investment in this project. The key results areas (KRAs) are designed to be gender-sensitive and inclusive of marginalized women and girls to lead and manage at least 35% of the GLO/GR, Integrated Watershed Management and Eco-system based road network projects, 40% of the water supply and sanitation projects, 60% of the irrigation projects, 70% of training positions, 60% of women technical staff in GRM or regulatory/operational monitoring for risk prioritization, priority project areas, and 50% of the project’s financial resources. The project will ensure that women and marginalized communities are leading and implementing project activities (see Annex II for further details). The KRA targets will be monitored by the GESI focal points and the Gender Monitoring Information System will be achieved.

## G

### Capacities of the Implementing Partner by undertaking an evaluation of capacity following the Framework for Cash Transfer (FCT) and Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers – HACT

#### The UNDP Enterprise Risk Management (ERM) Policy identifies Capabilities of the Partners as a key Strategic Risk. The level of risk is assessed at the project level, using the PCAT tool, which provides a structured approach to assess the level of risk that is present when UNDP works with partners to implement programmes and projects. The level of risk is determined by assessing the partner’s capacity in relation to the programme/project. The level of risk assessed will be used as an input for the project-level risk assessment. By identifying areas for capacity improvement, the PCAT could also help to reduce future potential risks.

#### A Partner Capacity Assessment Tool (PCAT) is a structured approach that provides a framework for assessing the partner’s capacity in relation to the programme/project. The level of risk assessed will be used as an input for the project-level risk assessment. By identifying areas for capacity improvement, the PCAT could also help to reduce future potential risks.

#### A Partner Capacity Assessment Tool (PCAT) is conducted for all projects and covers executing entities, responsible partners, grant recipients and other parties involved in the implementation of a programme/project. The PCAT will assess the partner’s capacity against practices that are inconsistent with UNDP’s social and environmental standards and codes of ethics.

### Project Monitoring

#### The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and aligned to the audit framework for cash transfers (FCT) and harmonized approach to cash transfers (HACT). UNDP will be appointing the auditors. In UNDP, scheduled audits are performed during the project cycle as per the audit plan. The audit plan is developed in consultation with the implementing partner.

#### A scheduled audit is used to determine whether the funds transferred to the Implementing Partner were used for their intended purpose and whether the financial statements of the programme/project have been subject to a financial audit and/or an internal control audit.

### Avoidance of Money Laundering, Terrorist Financing, Sanctions Violations, or Prohibited Practices

#### UNDP will apply its Anti-Corruption (A/C) MGT policy which defines due diligence and partnership assessment criteria for all partners it engages. Each entity is screened against the criteria set out in this policy and other policies referenced herein.

#### UNDP applies a zero tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. The guiding principles of UNDP’s commitment to prevent, identify and address all acts of fraud and corruption have been laid out in the UNDP Policy Against Fraud and Corruption Practices (the “FCP Policy”), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP. UNDP will ensure that all partners are aware of their obligations under this policy and that they are familiar with and are integrated into UNDP’s policy frameworks in relation to procurement, financial management, internal control systems and anti-corruption.

### Under the terms of the FCP Policy

#### UNDP has a commitment when developing a new programme or project, to ensure that risks related to fraud and corrupt practices are fully identified and understood. The processes and procedures for UNDP’s partnership capacity assessment will be applied in relation to all partners with whom UNDP is considering entering into an agreement for the programme/project it intends to engage in, as well as in relation to the potential parties who will be involved in the implementation of a programme/project. This includes, but is not limited to: (i) a review of the partner’s history and screening of all entities being considered to be contracted by or partner with UNDP against the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List; (ii) an assessment of any history of fraud, corruption or other fraudulent practices in relation to the partner; (iii) an assessment of whether the partner has a robust internal control component into the design of the project and ensures that the financial management of the programme/project is designed with adequate controls to prevent fraud, corruption, bribery, embezzlement, or any combination or AML risk that may be identified.

### Reference

#### Reference is also made to the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) applied by UNDP which represents a structured approach for providing cash transfers to government and non-governmental Partners (both Implementing Partners and Responsible Parties). The common objective of the HACT framework is to ensure that UNDP’s partners have adequate management, financial and technical capacities for management and accountability. It serves as a set of procedures on requesting, disbursing, providing assurance and monitoring cash transfers in order to ensure that they are provided in an efficient manner with development in a coordinated manner. Cash transfers (above the threshold of $300,000) in a programme cycle, or otherwise where there is a high risk of corruption, bribery, embezzlement or other fraudulent practices and/or experience with the partners’ are presumed to be a material risk to UNDP and will require a HACT micro assessment. The level of risk assessed will be used as an input for the project-level risk assessment and auditing requirements are then determined and carried out at a frequency determined in line with the risk rating.

Secretariat's assessment of FP272

Proposal name: Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change-induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country: Nepal

Project size: Small

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| *The project, "Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change-induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal", presents innovative and long-term solutions for improving the resilience of communities in the glacial river basins of Nepal by addressing critical vulnerability issues associated with GLOFs through ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction, use of climate information and early warning systems, and improved production, dissemination and use of climate information and early warning systems to vulnerable communities in three target river basins. This will contribute towards ensuring that the incidence of GLOFs is significantly reduced and that vulnerable communities receive timely early warnings and develop the capacity for taking appropriate action to mitigate, prepare for and respond to GLOF hazards in a timely manner.* | *The project will be implemented under the Direct Implementation Modality in the UNDP operational modalities. Hence, the Secretariat recommends that UNDP, as the accredited entity (AE), ensure proactive and continuous engagement with national and local authorities, stakeholders, and ownership and involvement of all the main stakeholders, which is critical for the success of the project.* |


---
**Page 108**
---

will also aim to protect infrastructure and private assets, transport networks, agricultural production, power supplies from hydropower plants and tourism activities in the targeted river basin communities learned from earlier work carried out by the AE on GLOF risk reduction in Nepal. This will help the Government of Nepal to adopt and maintain and expand the coverage of GLOF risk reduction and end-to-end GLOF EWS that are tested and proven effective means in reducing the exposure of vulnerable communities to GLOF hazards and the associated risks.

The project also focuses on promoting a coordinated approach to addressing the barriers identified by AE for the adoption of integrated approaches to GLOF risk management. This will be done using a suite of solutions that blend institutional and technical capacities, physical protection entailing both structural and non-structural approaches, and hazard monitoring and EWS. In addition, the project's implementation through the existing national-level institutions is commendable because it will facilitate the improvement of the existing capacity for risk management and governance mechanisms across the different levels of governance in the country.

The Secretariat has to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed in the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the AE, and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/J.84/02.


## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

The project, "Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change-induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal," aims to safeguard the lives and livelihoods of communities in glacial river basins, as well as some major economic sectors. The project will be implemented through a coordinated approach that includes flood risk lowering, downstream eco-disaster risk reduction measures and EWS. The objective of the project is to reduce the exposure of vulnerable communities to GLOF hazards by strengthening community livelihoods in three major watersheds, namely Maryangdi, Dudhkoshi and Arun, located across two target river basins, namely the Gandaki Basin and Koshi Basin, by


---
**Page 109**
---

establishing the necessary policies and institutional frameworks, introducing and scaling up integrated water management practices and climate-smart agriculture, and strengthening hydrometeorological monitoring systems within the Sunkoshi basin and its neighbouring areas.

Within the Gandaki Basin, the project targets the Thulagi Glacial Lake which drains into Narayangadhi River. The project also targets two other glacial lakes in the upper part of the basin, namely - Luming Tsho and Hongz 2 drain into Dhauligiri river of Dudh Koshi Basin and are located within the Upper Terai Watershed. The project target glacial lakes and river basins and watersheds face increasing risks from disasters owing to the increasing prevalence and severity of GLOFs caused by the climate change. The project will address these challenges through a multi-pronged approach that includes: risk of GLOFs threaten lives, infrastructure and livelihoods in downstream communities as they cause landslides, debris flows, floods, and other hazards; loss of human life and private assets, disrupt transport networks, agricultural production, power supplies from hydropower plants and drinking water.

5. The project will overcome these challenges by implementing an integrated approach to GLOF risk management in the target glacial river basins and watersheds that includes building institutional capacity for climate change adaptation; developing and disseminating tailored physical protection measures, including both grey and green infrastructure approaches, and enhancing community resilience.

6. The project is requesting USD 31.6 million of GCF financing. The total cost of the project, including co-financing, is USD 49.94 million, with co-financing primarily in the form of high-level institutional capacity building from the Government of Nepal (PPAN) and UNDP. The project will also receive parallel financing of USD 60.61 million from the Government of Nepal, PPAN and 30 of the municipalities located in the target glacial river basins.

7. In terms of environmental and social safeguards (ESS), this proposal has been categorized as having a low ESS risk category B. The review by the Secretariat confirms the E&S risk category assigned by the AE.


2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Output 1: Institutional and technical capacity strengthened to develop and integrate climate risk and hazard information into planning and development (total cost: USD 43.52 million; GCF cost: USD 32.71 million)

Output 1 will enhance the technical and institutional capacity of the relevant government departments to develop and disseminate tailored climate risk information and will establish a national-level monitoring system for assessing, managing and mitigating risks in Nepal. The output will focus on building capacity for GLOF hazard mapping and monitoring, operationalizing maintenance of existing risk databases, and identifying key GLOF risk reduction strategies; leveraging the use of technology to disseminate tailored, user-friendly climate risk information to major economic sectors in the country, including hydropower generation, agriculture, tourism, and disaster management. The output will also support the development of proactive preventive measures for the identification and mitigation of potential GLOF-related risks.


---
**Page 110**
---

Output 2: Improved hazard monitoring and the generation of early warnings, including the dissemination of information to local communities and important economic sectors (Total cost: USD 10.39 million; GCF cost: USD 8.53 million)

- Output 2 will focus on strengthening Nepal's GLOF EWS and on enhancing the capacity of the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) to monitor glacial lakes, associated hazards with other extreme climate events and to disseminate early warning information to local communities and important economic sectors. The output will entail installing hydrological and meteorological stations across the project's target areas, including four major lakes in the Terai region. It also includes the acquisition of equipment for data collection, storage and management of climate data from the stations for the development of early warning information systems. The output will further include capacity building and awareness-raising interventions aimed at improving the vulnerability assessment process in the target large river basins and watersheds to respond and adapt to GLOF risks and impacts.

Output 2: Flood flash flood risk and impact reduction measures implemented in priority glacial lakes watersheds (Total cost: USD 10.74 million; GCF cost: USD 9.70 million)

- Output 3 will focus on the implementation of GLOF risk reduction measures in the target glacial lakes, which were selected based on their respective hazard levels that warrant the immediate implementation of potential measures for GLOF mitigation as they contain the glacial lakes that pose the highest risks to communities and infrastructure. The output will implement a multi-pronged approach combining physical lake lowering to lower the water levels of the four priority lakes, namely Thulagi, Lower Barun, Lunsing Tsbo and Hongze 2, with other complementary measures aimed at reducing the potential of triggering a GLOF or the magnitude of a GLOF if it occurs; and ensuring that these measures are sustainable. The complementary measures will be based on a hybrid approach of green and grey infrastructure to reduce the residual impacts of a GLOF after lake lowering.

Project management, coordination and monitoring and evaluation (Total cost: USD 10.59 million; GCF cost: USD 7.12 million)

- Project implementation and monitoring and evaluation costs have been adequately provided for in this project as they have been planned and integrated within the design of the project to ensure that project implementation and monitoring and evaluation processes will be effectively managed.

Gender and environmental and social management framework aspects

- An environmental and social management framework (ESMF) and a gender action plan have been developed for the project, outlining specific activities that will sufficiently ensure that ESS safeguards are put in place and observed throughout the project's lifespan, as described in sections 4.1 and 4.2 below.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential
Scale: N/A

- Climate change is causing accelerated glacial retreat in Nepal, thereby increasing the risk of GLOFs to communities living downstream from glacial lakes and other vulnerable communities. Four lakes, namely Thulagi, Lower Barun, Lunsing Tsbo and Hongze 2, are known to pose the


---
**Page 111**
---

# Para-Shift shift potential

The project has a high para-shift shift potential in that it seeks to transition GLOF risk reduction and adaptation processes in Nepal from reactive approaches focused on response to the impacts of GLOF hazards and GLOF-induced disasters towards an approach that focuses on preparedness. The project aims to reduce the incidence of GLOF hazards and their impacts by lowering water levels in glacial lakes. The approach also seeks to ensure that vulnerable communities and decision-makers in glacial river basins and watersheds have easy and wide access to, and can make use of, reliable science.


---
**Page 112**
---

based climate information and early warnings in order to protect themselves from the huge potential impacts of GLOFs going forward.

The project promotes an approach that fosters the development of an integrated and comprehensive blend of technical capacity, grey and green infrastructure, ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction (DRR) measures, and community resilience building as a multi-option strategy for enabling the sustainable reduction of GLOF risks and associated impacts. The project creates a replicable model for scaling up and replication in glacial lake and river watersheds and basins across Nepal and in globally similar settings. The innovative approach to promoting and increasing the participation of local and national governments, communities, and other stakeholders in the development and implementation of GLOF risk reduction strategies and actions can be practically implemented in any country facing GLOFs.

The project is designed to have a significant impact on three components of the project and is designed to be impactful over the long term. The project presents an innovative and strategic approach to promoting and increasing the participation of local and national governments, communities, and other stakeholders in the development and implementation of GLOF risk reduction strategies and actions.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: N/A

The project will significantly reduce the exposure of poor and vulnerable communities to GLOF hazards and will thereby be instrumental in helping these communities to safeguard their lives and livelihoods, as well as avoid human and economic losses and the development of local and national economies that are resilient to GLOFs. The project will also contribute towards achieving a number of environmental benefits for the vulnerable communities in the target glacial lake and river basins and watersheds, and for the sustainable development aspirations and goals that Nepal and the region seek to achieve.

The project contributes directly towards Nepal's efforts to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) as defined by the United Nations. Specifically, SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), SDG 13 (climate action) and SDG 15 (life on land).

The project has the potential to deliver environmental co-benefits through the restoration of 200 hectares of mountain ecosystems and sequestering 339,480 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent. The project will also have a number of social and economic co-benefits that the project offers would be achieved and sustained through strengthening the capacity of relevant government departments and communities to increase their ability to sustainably manage climate information and early warning systems. In addition, the project would foster and developing, operating, maintaining and sustaining managing climate information and early warning systems in a manner that is sustainable over time by building awareness of GLOF hazards and to building capacity to mitigate the risks that they pose and adequately respond to their impacts.

The sustainable development potential of this investment is therefore estimated to be high.


### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: N/A

The project targets glacial lake and river basin watersheds and basins that contain some of the communities most vulnerable to GLOFs in the world, communities that face water, food, health and livelihood insecurities as a result of GLOFs.


---
**Page 113**
---

## The target communities face climate change-induced GLOF hazards that threaten their livelihoods and access to reliable access to climate information and EWS. Currently, livelihoods, food supplies, water, health and transport systems, and infrastructure in the target areas are not safeguarded from the potential loss and damage posed by their exposure to GLOFs. Through the development of a comprehensive early warning system (EWS) and other risk reduction measures and EWS, the proposed project will reduce the exposure of communities and the economic systems to GLOF hazards and increase their resilience in the face of such risks. Key strategies for safeguarding and transforming their livelihoods, food supplies, water, health systems and ecosystems are outlined below.

Moreover, Nepal is heavily indebted and has insufficient resources to invest in proactive actions to safeguard its population from GLOF hazards and risks associated impacts and losses. The project will provide a cost-effective solution that can be scaled up nationally to reduce the likelihood of GLOFs occurring, through access to usable climate information and do not have to rely on expensive infrastructure solutions. This will help protect livelihoods, food supplies, water, health and socioeconomic systems, and infrastructure from GLOF hazards, without having to invest in costly infrastructure solutions. The project will also empower local communities and economies in the target glacial river basin will remain exposed to GLOF hazards and will be unable to protect their lives and livelihoods against such risks. The project will therefore provide a cost-effective solution that can be scaled up nationally to reduce Nepal's public and private sectors to foster longer-term solutions for enhanced GLOF risk reduction, while also providing immediate benefits in terms of increased access to climate information and EWS to such risks.

### 3.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: N/A

Country ownership is well embedded in the project, which is fully aligned with the climate change and broader development aspirations, strategies and policies of Nepal. The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach involving governmental and non-governmental actors in the target glacial river basin to identify and plan for GLOF risks and to disseminate and use climate information and EWS to guide their own responses and solutions. This will ensure that the project is owned by all relevant stakeholders, which places an important focus on ensuring better synergy with ongoing national strategies and actions being implemented in Nepal.

The project is aligned with the nationally determined contributions, national adaptation plan, national adaptation action plan of action and a wide range of other climate change and environmental policies. The project will also be consistent with the broader development priorities of Nepal. Furthermore, the project has been developed with the strong participation and commitment of the Government of Nepal at all levels, including the Ministry of Environment, Forests and Soil Conservation and local level stakeholders, involving all levels of stakeholder groups, including grass-roots women and youth.

The AID is the UNDP, which has a long-standing presence in the country and a wealth of experience working with stakeholders and dealing with the development and environmental challenges facing Nepal. The UNDP will provide technical assistance to the project as well as experience and partnerships that will facilitate the meaningful engagement and involvement of all relevant stakeholders in the successful design and scaling up of the project's activities and outputs.

The architecture of the project consists of a central hub and project management unit (PMU) that will be led by the relevant government ministries and departments will participate directly in the governance and management of the project and its activities. This will bolster the integration of local content and ownership in the project's activities and outputs.

**Page 114**

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A

This project will directly support poor and vulnerable communities in the target glacial river basins of Nepal by providing them with climate-smart agricultural practices to increase their capacity and livelihoods. It will target at least 385,000 direct beneficiaries and about 1.9 million indirect beneficiaries. The project will also provide a range of blended grey and green GLOF risk reduction measures and providing access to climate information services (CIS) for early warning systems so that communities can take preventive action to protect themselves from GLOF hazards and risks.

The economic analysis demonstrates that the proposed interventions, like lowering and damming water levels in rivers, are cost-effective with significant economic benefits, making the project both effective and efficient in addressing the risks posed by GLOFs. The net present value (NPV) of the investment is US$105,329,847 at a discount rate of 6% and the internal rate of return (IRR) of USD$86,919,219 and a cost-benefit ratio of 4.9; while the return period-based scenario yields an NPV of US$105,329,847 at a discount rate of 6% and IRR of USD$86,919,219. Therefore, the benefits of the intervention will far outweigh the costs, underlining the project's effectiveness.

The funding amount is well justified to provide the support needed to safeguard trees, livelihoods, and infrastructure in the target glacial river basins and across Nepal by transforming disaster risk reduction governance in the target glacial river basins and across Nepal at a cost-effective level. The project will also make significant contributions to our investments in GLOF risk and disaster risk reduction and in climate information and GIS. It will use the best available technology, including the latest scientific knowledge, to provide a comprehensive mix of solutions for addressing the technical and financial capacity and infrastructure barriers that are preventing effective implementation of an integrated approach to helping the country to develop long-term and sustainable solutions for GLOF risk reduction and management. The project's investment will further deliver environmental co-benefits through the restoration of degraded land, which is estimated at 105,329,847 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent through reforestation activities, which presents a significant non-financial benefit.

The project also has an innovative exit strategy that will enable scaling up its approach across Nepal and the broader Himalayan region and will build partnerships between the Government of Nepal (GoN) and other stakeholders to ensure sustainability. The project is able to tackle GLOF-related challenges with innovative and locally appropriate solutions supported by reliable technical assistance, including GIS services. The GoN will be responsible for operation and maintenance provided by agencies such as DHIW, the Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC), and the Department of Forests and Soil Conservation (DoFSC) would ensure that the project remains financially sustainable after the implementation phase, which contributes to the project's ongoing effectiveness.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Project overview:** The project aims to safeguard the lives and livelihoods of people and the environment by reducing the risk of GEF9 and related harms in three identified watershed areas in Nepal. The project will adopt an integrated approach through the following: lowering sediment loads, improving water quality, and increasing ecosystem resilience by 30% through tested engineering methods; implementing downstream interventions such as reference sites, monitoring stations, and community-based early warning systems; using remote sensing technologies, to complement the lake lowering interventions; strengthening technical and institutional capacity of communities and local authorities for sustainable land use management and EWS development; and increasing community awareness on GLOF and EWS. The E&S co-benefits would include, among others, improved soil fertility and quality from reference sites; increased water availability in downstream areas; improved livelihoods of communities; increased employment opportunities from project activities during construction and operation phases; reduced vulnerability to extreme climatic events; and an improvement in the levels of social inclusion.

**Environmental and social risk category and safeguard alignment:** The project has been classified as a Category 1 (low) environmental and social risk, which is deemed equivalent to a GCF category B classification and is aligned with the accreditation level of the AE. Potential E&S risks include: loss of biodiversity in the three identified watersheds; sedimentation in downstream waterways; installation of observation, monitoring and EWS equipment, and construction and installation of other project components; introduction of inappropriate species during the implementation of eco-based solutions; impacts on cultural heritage; and impacts on livelihoods. While a number of key risks have been identified, the AE notes that no significant adverse environmental or social risks are expected to be associated with the project.

**Compliance GCF E&S standards:** The following paragraphs describe how the project complies with the standards.

**ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts:** The ESMP describes the procedures for screening and assessing the potential E&S risks and impacts; identification of potential mitigating measures and associated plans; the triggered actions in case of adverse events; grievance redress mechanisms; the information disclosure requirements; and the stakeholder engagement process. A detailed description of the environmental and social safeguards, including the comprehensive stakeholder engagement process are detailed in annex 7 to the funding proposal package). Three subprojects are identified, covering three watershed areas, but the specific locations of all activities will be determined during project implementation. Environmental and social risk assessments are expected to be prepared and finalized during implementation, and environmental clearances shall be secured prior to commencement of any work, in line with

### ESS-2: Labour and working conditions

The project will promote respect and realize fundamental principles and rights at work and ensure that there will be no discrimination and no forced or compulsory labour. The project will also ensure equal remuneration for work of equal opportunity. Based on the information provided, the expected workforce per site would be approximately 10 workers per site, with a total of 25 workers across all sites. Workers will be local conditions. The expected work will primarily be local labour, with no blasting or use of heavy machinery. The ESMF provides an outline for the labour management procedures and the detailed labour management plan will be developed in consultation with the project team. The labour management plan will set out the working labour procedures for each subproject; identify the main labour risks associated with each subproject; provide a risk assessment to identify potential issues and risks; and set out an action plan. The project would prohibit the employment of workers under 18 years old, pregnant women or those who are breastfeeding. Concerns related to gender equality will be addressed through the project's equal grievance redress mechanism.

### With regard to occupational health and safety

UNDP commits to protecting and promoting the health and safety of all workers involved in the project. The following risks have been identified by UNDP: ensuring appropriate control of site access; adopting appropriate engineering controls, such as the use of barriers or other physical means to prevent falls from heights and use of safety barriers; using appropriate personal protective equipment; ensuring appropriate waste management practices; and ensuring adequate first aid facilities are available on-site. The project has developed specific occupational health and safety measures, such as requiring on-site medical facilities, limiting work during the dry season, limiting exposure to occupational health and safety hazards through the use of personal protective equipment, providing training, ensuring relevant skills and experience in a similar work environment, provision of insurance, etc. Health and safety plans will be prepared for each subproject and integrated into overall project plans as part of the environmental and social management plan (ESMP) prior to any works commencing.

### ESS-3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

Construction work related to lake lowering, installation of equipment and eco-disaster risk and nature-based interventions are anticipated. The project will ensure that all construction materials used in the project comply with the preparation of waste water management plans as part of the ESMP to be developed during project implementation. All construction materials for the project will be locally sourced where possible; materials will be acquired locally from legally permitted sources. The use of locally available materials is anticipated, and any imported materials will be subject to environmental impact assessment. Waste generated on site will be managed through sediment and erosion management plans as part of the ESMP. The project will only use locally adapted species when establishing small-scale community-based nurseries; in compliance with the National Forest Policy and the National Biodiversity Conservation Protection Act.

### III. Community health, safety and security

The project sites for lake lowering, installation of check dams and other infrastructure for residual GLOF and flash flood flow will be located far away from settlements. To address potential harm from a failure of structural elements, such as embankments, the project will adopt appropriate engineering controls and engineering measures; studies, such as hydrological modelling and dam break modelling, have been undertaken to identify the most likely scenarios for failure of structural elements, as appraised by DHM. An independent technical review and assessment will be undertaken prior to construction commencing on any site. The project will establish a comprehensive design and maintenance phase, institutional requirements for periodic safety inspections, emergency procedures and proper maintenance will be integrated within the existing mandates of DHM and DoFSC. To address potential risks related to labour influx, the workforce will generally be

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples

The project meets the requirements of the Indigenous Peoples Policy and includes an Indigenous Peoples policy framework that will guide engagement with Indigenous Peoples throughout the design of the project and three Indigenous Peoples policies, one for each catchment, will be developed to guide engagement with Indigenous Peoples. The project will also promote


### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The AF does not foresee any physical or economic displacement resulting from the project's activities. The majority of the construction work is expected to take place on public land, and free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) for land tenure will be confirmed at all project sites as part of the detailed site surveys. In the event that land acquisition is required, it will be done in accordance with national laws and regulations, including those related to public land. There may be risks associated with sites located in territories in which there are customary user rights but no legal ownership rights. In such circumstances, the local government or Indigenous Peoples may have the right to grant access for project activities only upon agreement of these communities. UNDP will work closely with Indigenous Peoples and other stakeholders to ensure that any land acquisition is done in a manner that respects their rights. If the project is unencumbered and/or not land on which local livelihoods depend, as a last resort, in the event that land acquisition is required, it may be acquired through an auction or by a willing buyer/willing seller basis. In such cases, the AF will ensure that the following conditions are met: (i) the transaction occurs only if there is no other suitable alternative; and (ii) where some exist, the transaction occurs with the seller's informed consent; and the seller is provided with fair compensation based on prevailing market values. A land acquisition plan will be prepared to describe how the project will ensure that Indigenous Peoples are consulted in advance of their use of the land. The application of the land acquisition plan and Indigenous Peoples plans will be reviewed by the AF, and any changes or modifications to these plans will only occur if the environment is not adversely impacted.


### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project has identified potential adverse impacts to habitats, ecosystems and ecosystem services, risks to endangered species, risks in relation to the introduction of invasive alien species, and risks associated with climate change adaptation and restoration. The AF has identified two national parks (Makalan Barun National Park and Sagarmatha National Park) that are located within or adjacent to the proposed project sites. The glacial lakes Hongren 2 and Lower Bumrun fall under the jurisdiction of Makalan Barun National Park, while the glacial lake Upper Bumrun falls in the buffer zone of Sagarmatha National Park. Before commencement of any work, the AF will ensure that all project activities are legally permitted and that the project meets the requirements of the respective national parks. The AF will also consult with local communities, affected communities, and Indigenous Peoples and other stakeholders as presented in annex 7 to the funding agreement. In addition, the AF will ensure that the proposed management plans for national parks consider climate-resilient measures. The low-lying areas are prone to flooding during monsoon season; therefore, further biodiversity assessments through an environmental and social impact assessment will be undertaken at these sites. The project will avoid all adverse impacts from project activities once identified. Critical habitats will be avoided in all the project's activities. The predicted actions for projects that may have a significant adverse effect on the conservation status of habitat or species are considered unavoidable impacts to critical habitats and food acquisition in endangered species. The project will not introduce invasive species in the covered sites. In the unlikely event that an unavoidable impact is identified, during design and implementation, appropriate experts will be engaged to prepare such a plan. An outline is provided in annex 8.


### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples

The project meets the requirements of the Indigenous Peoples Policy and includes an Indigenous Peoples policy framework that will guide engagement with Indigenous Peoples throughout the design of the project and three Indigenous Peoples policies, one for each catchment, will be developed to guide engagement with Indigenous Peoples.
