### 5.2 Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B13M-2018/11 requires safeguarding from SEAH as a key element of the project’s environmental and social safeguards in accordance with the terms of the funding proposal. The project includes activities that involve engaging with various actors, such as individuals, communities, local authorities, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), disaster response plans, municipal disaster preparedness and response plans, and local-level climate change adaptation plans. To address these risks, the project will implement a number of risk holding training workshops and establishing and mobilizing youth and volunteer groups. Risk factors include the high vulnerability of women living in the geographically remote and disaster-prone areas of the project area to sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH). These risks negatively affect women. Identified potential SEAH risks outlined in the ESME include a risk of illiteracy among women and girls, lack of awareness about their rights, and lack of knowledge to prevent SEAH incidents. To mitigate risks, the project will, among other things, provide SEAH and grievance redress mechanisms for women living in remote areas. The prevention of SEAH is included in the gender action plan; establish a code of conduct and require its signing by personnel involved with the project; provide training on preventing sexual harassment and raise awareness of SEAH in the community in which the project will operate. Additionally, the project will employ multiple grievance redress mechanisms to report incidents and provide support for victims. The institutional grievance redress mechanism of the AF and the Independent Redress Mechanism of the GCF will also be available to stakeholders. The effectiveness of these measures will be monitored for their effectiveness on an ongoing basis during implementation.


### 4.2 Gender policy

The AEPS has provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment outlines the national policies and strategies that are in place to address gender equality, including those providing enabling environment to address gender issues and women’s empowerment. National climate change adaptation plans (NCCAPs) also provide a framework for addressing gender equity and inclusion in disaster risk reduction. The gender impacts of climate change related disasters such as GLOFs are also discussed in the gender assessment. Women are identified as being most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change, particularly in relation to water scarcity; women are particularly exposed to risks and are likely to suffer higher rates of mortality, morbidity and disability. Women are also disproportionately affected by the impacts of providing increased care for children, the sick, disabled people and elderly people after climate change related disasters. The gender assessment also discusses the indirect effects of the impacts of disasters, including limited livelihood options, restricted access to education and health services, and loss of property. Gender inequalities in terms of social norms and interactional contexts, such as age, caste or those historically excluded, the status of Indigenous Peoples, literacy and disability, further exacerbate the vulnerabilities of women.

## Risks

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

The proposed adaptation project for Nepal will help to address climate change risk in the country by promoting GLOF risk mitigation measures and EWS in four target high-risk watersheds. The project also supports community protection and the restoration of 1,500 hectares of mangrove forest.

GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 293,158,230. Together with co-financing of USD 114,760,000 from the Government of Nepal (GoN), USD 100 million from UNDP, and USD 290 from IPPAN, the total project investment is USD 499,448,530. The co-financing ratio is 1:3.03 and accounts for 60% of the total project cost. The remaining 40% will be covered by GCF funding as expected is from the Government of Nepal (USD 464,184) municipalities in the three target watersheds (USD 50,180,000) and IPPAN (USD 962,470) for implementation and operation and maintenance.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the project (medium risk)

The AE is UNDP, which has an extensive track record and global experience in implementing climate adaptation and climate information projects and has technical expertise in developing and managing GLOF risk management projects with a focus on upstream ecosystem management. This funding proposal is expected to benefit from lessons learned in earlier GLOF risk management projects in Nepal supported by the Global Environment Facility.

The overall implementation of the project will be led by the Ministry of Finance and DHM under the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation. These ministries will work with various other government agencies including the National Disaster Risk Management Authority and the Ministry of Forests and Environment and its departments, including the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology to manage the project review, financial management and reporting. The Government's capacity and role for managing donor projects, and GLOF risk mitigation projects in particular, is high, and this poses a low implementation risk.


---
**Page 121**
---

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

The proposed project plans to deploy grey and green infrastructure consisting of physical lake lowering infrastructure and ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction measures in Nepal. The implementation at all sites is considered technically feasible; however, successful implementation has been identified as occupational and community health and safety risks during construction and operation of the infrastructure. These risks are associated with exposure to dust, which could result in loss of life/lives and may lead to reputational risks and implementation delays.

The deployment of grey infrastructure (e.g., dams, levees) is subject to a range of natural hazards such as GLCs, earthquakes and extreme weather events, which could result in higher operational costs and potential damage to the project. This risk will be mitigated by ensuring resilient design of the infrastructure, putting in place appropriate action plans to ensure proper siting and adequate monitoring of the infrastructure, and implementing the procedures of the AI at all times of project implementation. Furthermore, lake lowering and EWS challenges are associated with the lack of local knowledge and technical expertise, thereby reducing the effectiveness of the project's intervention. This risk will be mitigated by ensuring that the selection and design of lake lowering and EWS infrastructure is based on best practices adopted in the project case into account the potential increases in hazard frequency and severity.

### Project viability and concessionality

The proposed project plans to implement good practice to enhance GLOF risk reduction within three target watersheds in Nepal. The project also aims to improve GLOF early warning and preparedness systems, which are generally viable and have been implemented in other comparable locations. The proposed project is not expected to generate significant economic products per capita (USD 1,530). The country's ability to raise private capital is also limited, and the programme is expected to generate significant adaptation benefits for Nepal.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

UNDP’s due diligence process includes Know Your Customer (KYC) and Anti-Money Laundering/Counter-Terrorism Financing (AML/CFT) checks for all partners. Public entities involved in on-financing are evaluated based on their financial stability, project prioritisation, and long-term sustainability of the project. This reduces the risk that public entities will not be able to fulfill their commitments. For private entities like the Independent Power Producers Association (IPPPA), UNDP has a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) in place to strengthen the relationship between UNDP, the Government of Nepal (GoN), and IPPPA is considered stable, and IPPPA’s compliance with local laws is regularly monitored. This reduces the inherent risk of financial mismanagement and corruption, which could have an adverse impact on the private nature of IPPPA's finances.

### Implementation

Implementation will be in line with the Harmonised Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) ensuring that financial transfers are conducted in accordance with predefined risk management levels. Regular audits, financial oversight, and capacity assessments are integral components of HACT. These measures ensure that project funds are used in a manner consistent with the project's goals and legal requirements.

In the event of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of results areas, single proposal or AE concentration.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

Following a comprehensive review of the funding proposal for the Nepal GLOF risk reduction funding proposal, several key financial management aspects have been carefully examined and evaluated.

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 5 August 2016 and became effective on 23 November 2016, which was amended and restated pursuant to a first amendment to the accreditation agreement dated 18 March 2023, and which became effective on 3 May 2023 (the "AMA").

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has advised the GCF that there are no known impediments to the implementation of the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Nepal, a country in which GCF is not provided with any protection against litigation or expropriation. The GCF Secretariat is not protected against litigation or expropriation in the country, which needs to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in such countries may also need to be considered as it relates to the protection of deployed GCF personnel.

Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

The Board also recommends that it is recommended that any approval by the Board be subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 100 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP272

## Proposal name: Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change-induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

#### Country: Nepal
#### Project size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

The project entitled "Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change-induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal" is a seven-year initiative targeting high-altitude catchments across the Arun, DudhKoshi, Marshyangdi, Kali Gandaki, and Karnali river basins. The project aims to reduce human exposure and ecosystems from increasing GLOF risks caused by human-induced climate change.

As a public service, the project seeks to reduce human and economic losses by strengthening the anticipatory capacity of Nepal to manage GLOFs and related hydrometeorological hazards.

### 1.1 Objectives

The project has three main objectives:

- **(a)** An integrated approach to planning and development that takes climate risks and hazard information into account is adopted by local and national-level institutions.
- **(b)** Communities in high-risk areas are better prepared to respond to GLOFs and flash floods, and have increased preparedness to respond to other flood risk.

To achieve these outcomes, the project will deliver three outputs:

1. Enhanced institutional and technical capacities for climate risk-informed development planning and implementation.
2. Improved hazard monitoring systems and early warning coverage, including expanded hydrometeorological infrastructure in GLOF-prone basins; and
3. Implementation of a range of adaptation and mitigation interventions such as glacial lake lowering, vegetation galleries, slope stabilization, and rehabilitation of degraded riparian areas.

The project is categorized as environmental and social risk Category B reflecting moderate risks mitigated through targeted safeguards. It delivers environmental and social co-benefits including the creation of 10,524 jobs during implementation; the planting of over four million planted trees. Gender equity and social inclusion are integrated throughout the design, with specific provisions for the participation and benefit of women, Indigenous Peoples, and marginalized groups.

The total cost of the project is USD 93.94 million, of which USD 36.16 million is requested from GCF as a grant. The remaining USD 57.79 million will be co-financed by national governments.


---
**Page 126**
---

### Impact potential

#### Scale: Medium-High

The project is expected to directly benefit 285,956 people (55 percent women and 45 per cent men) in Nepal representing approximately 1.3 per cent of the population through glacial lake lowering, ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction (eco-DRR) measures, and improved early warning systems. Indirectly, 1,280,960 people (c.o.p.) (e.g., part of the population) is indirectly benefited by the project through increased access to water resources, improved agricultural services, and restored ecosystems. The direct beneficiaries are primarily concentrated in the downstream areas of the Himalayan mountain range, which are vulnerable to glacial lake lowering through dam breach modeling and population overlays. The project gives explicit attention to the most vulnerable groups such as women, children, indigenous people, and youth, with specific measures in place to ensure the inclusion of Indigenous Peoples, Dalits, and remote mountain communities.

To avoid maladaptive infrastructure, the project applies a hybrid approach combining grey infrastructure (e.g., conservation ponds, flood walls) with nature-based solutions (e.g., vegetation restoration). The project also promotes climate-resilient land use planning by engineering structures alone and are designed with climate projections (representative concentration pathways - RCPs 4.5 and 8.5) to ensure long-term sustainability of the interventions. These standards incorporate climate modeling and dam breach simulations, with operation and maintenance (O&M) protocols and continuous training for long-term sustainability.

The project is expected to reduce flood hazard exposure levels by (1) reducing GLAD hazard magnitude through glacial lake lowering; (2) expanding early warning coverage in downstream areas of the Himalayan mountain range. The indirect benefits include the reduction of economic losses by USD 45–87 million and an expected reduction in loss of 2,036 lives compared to a no-intervention scenario. Capacity-building for local actors is also provided through training on climate risk management and disaster preparedness, contributing to sustainable risk governance.

Improved climate information services are provided to DRRM enabling it to produce tailored GLOF forecasts and risk information products. Additionally, the project aligns with national disaster risk reduction (DRR) legislation and supports the mainstreaming of climate risk into sectoral planning and decision-making processes through inter-sectoral coordination among hydropower, tourism and agriculture actors through formalized risk adaptation strategies.

Climate information use is significantly expanded through new high-altitude hydromet stations, real-time monitoring networks, and standard operating procedures for community-based early warning systems.


---
**Page 127**
---

based dissemination. The project enhances early warning dissemination through both centralized and decentralized channels, including Community Disaster Management Committees and mobile alerts, ensuring timely communication to at-risk communities downstream.

13. While the project shows high potential for immediate adaptation benefits, three limitations should be noted:

* **Adverse impacts:** The number of indirect beneficiaries is substantial; the depth of benefits for these groups is more diffuse and may rely heavily on the sustainability of early warning and knowledge systems beyond project closure.
* **Access to information:** There are strong theoretically practical challenges in reaching highly mobile or isolated Indigenous groups, which could limit impact unless safeguards and monitoring are rigorously implemented.
* **Theoretical limitations:** Scale and glacial land models do not incorporate freeze-thaw cycles, a driver of lake instability acknowledged by the AE in response to a query from the independent Technical Advisory Panel. This may lead to an overestimation of the project's impacts on both the frequency and magnitude of GLAs and therefore the full scale of avoided damages.
* **The IPAF assesses the impact potential of the proposed project as medium to high.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-High

15. The GLOF project catalyzes a shift from a historically reactive, fragmented approach to GLA risk in Nepal towards a proactive, integrated model of anticipatory climate risk management. This is achieved through the development and implementation of new technologies, including high-altitude lake-lowering, eco-DRR infrastructure, and automated early warning systems.

16. Based on its theory of change, the project presents a replicable and cost-efficient model for reducing GLA risks. Initial interventions at four high-risk glacial lakes serve as demonstrations to inform policy and investment decisions in other high-risk areas in Nepal and within comparable high-altitude regions across South Asia. The integration of capacity-building and hazard mapping into existing disaster risk management systems will also increase the quality of data from ITAP regarding clarity and causal logic of the theory of change.

The AE has proposed the following changes to the project’s Theory of Change: (1) The initial interventions at Langtang and Hong 2 glacial lakes are reduced and nature-based flood mitigation measures and early warning systems are introduced. (2) The capacity-building component is expanded, and community preparedness and adaptive capacity of downstream communities will be enhanced. BECAUSE the likelihood that a catastrophic event will occur in the project area over the next five years will be reduced and communities will be better prepared to avoid and recover from residual flood risks. However, the absence of a fully elaborated and costed national replication plan, particularly for the nature-based flood mitigation measures, is a significant limitation on the scale of expansion post-project.

The project incorporates long-term sustainability measures by embedding climate risk governance into national systems and through public-private partnerships (PPPs) with the hydropower, tourism and insurance sectors. These partnership aims to generate market-based incentives for sustainable management of high-risk glacial lakes. However, while these PPPs remain at a preliminary stage, with few signed commitments or tested business models to ensure continuity beyond GCF funding. Moreover, while the monitoring and


---
**Page 128**
---

evaluation framework is robust, its current design is largely top-down, community-level knowledge feedback loops and co-design mechanisms, particularly with Indigenous groups, could be better articulated to strengthen local ownership of scaling pathways.

### Sustainable development potential Scale: High

1. The GLOF project in Nepal delivers significant environmental co-benefits through extensive ecosystem restoration and hydro-eco-DR interventions. Activities include planting over 450,000 trees, reforestation, soil conservation measures such as terraces and revetments that reduce erosion, increase soil infiltration and stabilize degraded floodplains. These initiatives contribute to the project's goal of reducing vulnerability while strengthening biodiversity conservation. The estimated carbon sequestration potential is 100,194 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2-e) over the project’s lifespan (20 years), calculated using the Eko Carbon Calculator. The project has a low environmental footprint aligned with SDG's: 13 climate action; 15 life on land; and e water and sanitation targets. This is due to the use of locally sourced materials, minimal reliance on foreign inputs, and the presence of local forest user groups and the sustainability of DoSC-supported community-based restoration.

2. The project generates substantial co-benefits by reducing water- and vector-borne disease prevalence through flood mitigation, preserving public services and decreasing recovery costs from natural disasters. This has a positive impact on the livelihoods of people living in off-grid and financial distress, particularly among poor and remote populations. Furthermore, the project empowers communities to adopt sustainable land management practices and agroforestry techniques, thus improving their employability in future risk-reduction initiatives. Inclusion of Indigenous groups ensures that local knowledge is respected and integrated into co-design mechanisms for EWS supports SDG's: 3 good health and well-being; and 11- sustainable cities and communities.


---
**Page 129**
---

23. Economic co-benefits are expected through job creation in engineering, construction and ecosystem restoration activities, as well as from the stimulation of tourism and improved energy sector stability. Tourism benefits from reduced flood risk in glacial basins, while development of hydropower projects will provide reliable electricity to remote sectors that are critical to the economy of Nepal. The anticipated increase in employment opportunities, especially for women and youth, is expected to contribute towards poverty reduction, enhanced energy supply and transport systems connect to SGGs 1: no poverty; 7: affordable clean energy; and 8: decent work and economic growth.

24. Gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) Action Plan integrated tailors activities such as women-led cooperatives, community-based flood early warning systems, and youth engagement in disaster risk reduction. Women’s groups are key implementers in community-based flood EWS. The project aims to address systemic barriers to women’s participation and leadership while also supporting Indicators of GESI: gender equality; SDG 5: gender equality; and SDG 10: reduced inequalities. However, sustaining institutions and cultural change beyond project timelines will require strong commitment from the government and other stakeholders.

25. While the co-benefits are broad and well-structured, risks remain. The longevity of environmental and social gains hinge on maintaining cross-sector collaboration, private sector engagement, and institutional mainstreaming. These challenges are compounded by the Independent Power Producers’ Association of Nepal and Tourism Board is limited, and the project will need to engage with other stakeholders such as local government bodies and groups. Without stronger commitments to financial continuity and institutional mainstreaming, there is a risk that these gains may be short-lived or unsustainable in the long term.

26. Long-term ecosystem service management mechanisms and develop a road map to integrate co-benefit tracking into national planning frameworks.

27. The GLOF rates are high and sustainable development potential of the proposed project at high.

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

28. Nepal is among the most climate-vulnerable countries globally, ranked as the most economically vulnerable to social flood risks in the Himalaya-alongside Bhutan. Since the 1970s, 26 GLOF events have occurred, severely damaging lives, livelihoods and infrastructure.

29. Future projections indicate that climate change will exacerbate these risks through increasing temperatures and monsoon variability, which will intensify glacial melt and increase GLOF frequency. The project area is also vulnerable to a range of other natural hazards such as heavy precipitation and avalanches. fragile infrastructure, exacerbating these risks. The four glacial lakes identified in the project, Thulagi, Lower Bhorang, Lumbini Tsho and Hongz 2 have been identified as the most dangerous due to their high water levels and proximity to human settlements.

30. The project targets over 2.5 million people, including 385,956 direct beneficiaries living in the GLOF flood zone and an additional 1.9 million in surrounding municipalities. These populations are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts on agriculture, water supply and tourism. The project design prioritizes vulnerable and historically excluded groups, including women, youth, indigenous communities, people with disabilities, and those identified by the GES assessment; these groups face systemic barriers to resilience, such as discrimination, mobility restrictions, lack of access to resources, and limited decision-making power.

31. Despite modest improvements in the human development index (current value:602), Nepal remains one of the poorest countries in South Asia, with 30 per cent of the population living below the poverty line. The project area is located within the Himalayan region’s mountainous target areas; income levels are lower, service access is weaker, and vulnerability to disaster shocks is higher. These areas have limited basic infrastructure, frequently experience


---
**Page 130**
---

service disruptions after floods or landslides, and remain underpinned by public investment and climate finance.

Financial barriers are acute. The ability to finance GLOF risk reduction in Nepal is constrained by increasing public debt (41 per cent of gross domestic product in 2022), limited fiscal space for new investments, and the high cost of adaptation measures. While GLOF risk reduction is nearly absent due to high capital costs and long-time frames. The project addresses these gaps through a multi-stakeholder approach that seeks to leverage public resources of $17 million to co-finance. However, private sector engagement, particularly from the hydropower and tourism sectors, remains limited in Nepal and will be explored beyond the proposal stage.

The project strengthens institutional capacity at national and subnational levels, especially for forest and forest authorities. It supports regulatory reforms, technical training, and integrated watershed management plans. However, implementation lags persist. Institutional arrangements are complex with multiple actors involved, roles and responsibilities under-defined. Coordination between ministries and local governments is still limited, with overlapping mandates and unclear lines of accountability.

While the project identifies highly vulnerable groups and seeks to integrate them, meaningful participation, particularly of Indigenous Peoples and linguistically isolated communities, remains a challenge. The project has not yet demonstrated its readiness. The Indigenous Peoples Engagement Framework and GES Action Plan provide important safeguards for Indigenous Peoples but require solving, sustained implementation and monitoring to ensure equity in benefit sharing.

The ITAP assesses the needs of the recipients of the proposed project as high.


# Country ownership Scale: High

The GLOF project in Nepal is strongly aligned with the country's national climate change priorities and frameworks, including the second nationally determined contribution (NDC), the national adaptation plan (NAP), the National Adaptation Plan of Action (NAPA), the Third National Communication, and the Climate Change Policy (2019). Each of these identifies adaptation to climate-induced risks, including GLOFs, monsoon floods, and landslides, as a national priority. The project is also aligned with the NDC's targets for flood risk reduction and EWS in high mountain areas and supports NDC targets around DRR as an adaptation measure.

The project builds upon existing institutional and legal frameworks in Nepal, including the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act (2017), the DRR Strategic Plan of Action (2018-2030), the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2019, the Ministry of Finance (the national designated authority), and multiple key institutions—such as DHM, DaFG, and NDRRMA—as well as local governments. The project also builds on existing or coordinating partners. The project integrates its interventions into national watershed and park management plans, which are in turn aligned with the broader DRR strategy and responsibilities of domestic institutions. However, challenges remain in ensuring that policy-level commitments translate to effective implementation at subnational levels. Budgetary absorption and institutional readiness vary widely across provinces.

The AIF has a proven track record in GLOF and DDR projects in Nepal, including the Inja Lake lowering project, and in similar high-mountain contexts, such as Bhutan. The Government of Nepal is committed to supporting the project through its own NDRRMA initiatives. The proposal builds on past lessons, including workforce mobilization in remote areas and gender-sensitive approaches. While national capacity is being strengthened.


---
**Page 131**
---

especially within DRM, the long-term risk remains that technical operations such as monitoring and maintenance of EWS may continue to rely on UNDP oversight unless further integrated into national budgeting and civil service structures.

The proposal was developed through a comprehensive and inclusive stakeholder engagement process involving representatives from government, civil society, Indigenous Peoples' organizations, academia, and the private sector. Stakeholder consultations were held at all stages of project development, including workshops in line with the principles of free, prior, and informed consent. An Indigenous Peoples' Working Group was established to ensure that their perspectives are fully considered.

The project includes a grievance redress mechanism aligned with national law and GCF policies. The project is designed to be self-sustaining financially and operationally; it is not fully debt-free; stakeholder platform for ongoing co-management could be institutionalized further.

Nepal has long-standing and globally recognized experience in community management of state-owned forest land. This experience should be explicitly considered both in the co-design of land-use activities and in the promotion of community-based monitoring systems within the project.

Furthermore, Nepal has been actively engaged in climate risk assessment and adaptation planning at national level. The project will build on this work. Activities that are part of the inception phase include a comprehensive stocktaking, not only for existing knowledge but also of potential opportunities for leveraging existing initiatives or complementing them with complementary initiatives. These can be leveraged, adapted, or scaled up through the project to maximize impact.

The IFAP assesses the country ownership of the proposed project as high.


### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium

- The project requests a total of USD 264.5 million in grant financing, complemented by USD 308.9 million in co-financing from both grant and grant-based borrowing, bringing the total request to USD 50 million.
Given the status of Nepal as a least developed country, the public good nature of GLOF risk reduction, and the lack of a viable commercial return, the full grant structure is appropriate, capable of up-front investments and ongoing operations that private markets will not underwrite.

- The project's total cost is USD 573.4 million at base-case annual GLOF probability scenario (Scenario I) and USD 451.5 million under a more conservative return period probability scenario (Scenario II). These costs are based on the most recent technical review, which reviews the "annual decay in benefits" parameter from annex III (section 3.45). Decay is beneficial to the project because it reflects that the risks of GLOF events and associated damages remain realistic and trigger any necessary adjustments in maintenance budgets or reinvestment schedules.

- The co-financing ratio is relatively modest at 1:0.38, with most co-financing from domestic public institutions in the form of in-kind contributions or parallel O&M commitments. Although there are no formal agreements for co-financing, there have been informal discussions post-project GLOF, and there are few mechanisms to ensure durability of these commitments beyond the GCF project implementation period. The project's leverage of private capital is minimal and money limited to letters of support from tourism and hydropower stakeholders, without funding co-financing agreements.


---
**Page 132**
---

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The project builds on best practices from previous GLOF mitigation efforts in Nepal and Bhutan, including the 2016 Inga Lake project. It integrates hydrological modeling, climate-informed design standards, and stakeholder-specific disaster planning. The application of intelligent decision-making tools, such as machine learning for real-time monitoring and response protocols, is noted. However, there is limited evidence of dynamic updating of practices based on real-time data. The proposal suggests that the project will develop a phased approach to plans to integrate artificial intelligence/machine learning tools for improved forecasting; the proposal does not provide details about how this will be done or what specific resources are required in terms of budgetary or timeline allocation for these digital enhancements. Likewise, although local and Indigenous knowledge is highlighted as a cornerstone of the approach, the proposal does not detail the extent to which it will be integrated into project design and implementation phases.

The project proposes engaging private sector actors through private partners, through PPPs for EWS operations, fee-based risk-information services and/or co-financing with ODR investments; yet only offers high-level indications of private-sector involvement without detailing specific investment requirements. The proposal also does not provide sufficient detail on the roles of monitoring, data services, EWS maintenance, downstream protection structures or eco-ODR measures in terms of financing, implementing, incentivizing and holding private actors accountable remain insufficiently articulated.

The TAP considers the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project to be medium.

Based on the analyses presented above, the ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

## Proposal name:
Protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) and climate change induced flooding in glacial lakes of Nepal

United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Impact potential
The Medium-High rating is noted.

Sustainability of benefits for indirect beneficiaries will be supported through long-term policy and finance for the maintenance of EWS embedded in DHM and NDRRMA operations, with added focus on capacity building. The project will also include a range of technical and institutional safeguards to deploy to reach remote indigenous communities, with adaptive monitoring systems that are robust enough to withstand GLOFs. These systems will be anchored in field-based observations and real-time data from monitoring systems, supporting updated GLOF risk assessments. While such processes may impact the magnitude of risk, this will likely only increase the likelihood of more effective lowering interventions.

### Paradigm shift potential
The Medium-High rating is noted.
While ITAP notes the absence of a fully costed national replication plan, the project establishes foundational capacities—technical, institutional, and financial—to enable scaling. It embeds GLOF risk management into DHM and NDRRMA operations, with an emphasis on enhancing sustainability. ITAP’s concerns on the top-down nature of the monitoring system are well noted; emulating a bottom-up approach will be key to ensuring long-term sustainability of the project through adaptive management. Such feedback systems will be a key component in the M&E framework that will be developed at project inception.

The potential for paradigm shift is Medium-High.
While future planning for downstream infrastructure is not a primary output of the project, several of its outputs are expected to contribute significantly to improved GLOF risk information planning. In particular, the preparation and regular updating of risk maps and risk information products will provide tools needed for informed decision-making.

### Sustainable development potential
Regarding the sustainability of the project’s outputs—particularly the automated EWS and maintenance-intensive lake infrastructure these factors are well acknowledged, and budget allocations have been made accordingly. The project has also been formally committed through co-finance letters and will be integrated into the annual budgets of the relevant agencies (as outlined in the O&M plan). The development of PPPs will further seek to establish a sustainable financing mechanism for the maintenance of EWS and GLOF risk information products.

The rating of High is noted.

Concerns for the longevity of environmental and social gains are well noted. While it is true that these concerns are relevant on maintaining the collaboration and engagement of key stakeholders in the long term, extensive efforts have been put in place to ensure that these factors are fully integrated into the project design, minimising the risk of benefits not being realised.

Initial commitments from IPPAN and NTR, although limited, represent the intention of these entities to support the project. However, it is noted that some of the private sector partners are challenging to secure upfront; but extensive engagements throughout the process have indicated a willingness on their part that long-term partnerships are feasible. IPPAN is also in the process of finalising its financing agreement with the Government of India for the use of funds from hydropower companies to be used for development activities. Such arrangements will be explored further and developed through the PTP-linked activity.

Needs of the recipient

The High rating is noted.

Regarding the limited discussion in the O&M plan on vertical coordination between national entities and their decentralised sub-national entities: Although decentralisation is relatively new in Nepal, it has been a key component of the country’s development strategy. The decentralised governance structure that ensures efficient decision-making and accountability. As part of the mainstreaming of disaster risk management (DRM) into the National Water Resources Strategy (NWRS), Basin Management Centers (BMGC) are direct and reliable, minimising bureaucratic delays and enabling timely implementation of programs and activities. This integrated system enhances coordination between national and sub-national entities. BMGCs are a part of the established institutional setup. BMGCs benefit from clear mandates, uniform operational guidelines, and a well-defined organisational structure. The NWRS has been designed to implement integrated watershed and basin-level conservation and management initiatives effectively at all levels. The NWRS is supported by the National DRM Strategy (NDRMS) and policy-aligned mechanism that ensures smooth fund flow and governance. Guided by the BMGC Act, 2017, the NWRS provides a framework for coordination between national entities with municipalities, leveraging existing local disaster management committees and institutional structures.

The need for strict implementation of the safeguard plans and deep engagement with stakeholders is well acknowledged. The commitment to these systems is restored by the industry’s support to the safeguard officer on the PMU, who will be responsible for ensuring their implementation.


Country ownership

The High rating is well noted, as is the need for comprehensive stocktaking of climate risk assessments and adaptation efforts at project inception. This will be a key first step in the activities of the project. The PMU has already started this process by developing a methodology and SOPs for implementing integrated GLOF risk reduction in vulnerable communities.

IPAP’s concerns on varying budgetary absorption and institutional readiness of provinces is well noted. ## ITAP's concerns on project's ability to leverage private capital are well-noted. As noted in our response under the section on "Sustainable Development Potential," there is expressed interest of private sector entities in participating in the project, including through the establishment of these entities throughout implementation and explore opportunities for greater private sector participation in the project. The project will also provide a framework for public-private partnerships (PPPs) under sub-activity 1.2.3.

Regarding the point on indigenous knowledge, while there is no specific activity dedicated to systematizing, validating, and operationalizing this knowledge, the close engagement with indigenous communities and the organizations that represent them will create a platform for project activities to build upon. The project will also include an assessment of the potential use of a multi-hazard-based risk assessment that will inform local disaster risk management and will be instrumental in building on indigenous knowledge.

On AUM/IL, while budget was not specifically set aside for this component in particular, the budget for the IWS activities will be directed towards the most cost-efficient options. During implementation, the project team will continue to monitor costs and will include consideration of AUM/IL where it is deemed a feasible option. Budget under Activity 1.2 includes $70,000 for the development of an early warning system (EWS) and $35,000 for CTA to support the development of EWS and related data systems, totaling over $760,000.

One caveat is that data scarcity in Nepal will have impact the extent of potential investments in AUM/IL. The project team will continue to monitor this issue closely with an improved observation and data management creating a platform for all approaches over time.


# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

ITAP's recommendation of the project is greatly appreciated. The comments presented are all well noted and will be accounted for by the AE and IL during implementation.

## Key points

1. Private Sector engagement is a key element of the project's scaling and sustainably strategy. To this end, the need to identify, define and budget specific roles, criteria and targets for private sector entities has been included in sub-activity 1.2 specifically designed to fulfill this role. In particular, sub-activity 1.2.3 will formalise and institutionalize private sector engagement through a multi-stakeholder platform for risk reduction in Nepal — with such agreements including details on the roles and financial commitments of all stakeholders involved in the project's strategies, including for the maintenance and scaling of EWS and risk reduction measures.

2. As noted in the response above (Country Ownership), a comprehensive stakeholder engagement process will be undertaken to ensure buy-in from all relevant actors at each stage of the project design and risk assessment process. This approach includes the integration of community knowledge and capacity building into the project's planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation as well as through annual training and outreach programs. Moreover, the activity includes establishment and mobilizing of youth groups as well as volunteer's corps, increasing community participation in disaster risk reduction activities.

# Project's expected output 2: Improved hazard monitoring and the generation and dissemination of early warning to local communities and important economic sectors.

## Project activities

### Activity 2.1
**Improve observation networks to strengthen the technical capacity of DHI for the monitoring of climate hazard and risk.**

#### GEDSI activities
- Improve EWS to strengthen adaptive capacity, response to CLOF and other climate hazards.
- Limited awareness among women marginalized groups, including women with disabilities regarding EWS and their role in responding to climate hazards.

#### Baseline
- Limited representation of these groups from vulnerable areas lead and participate in community-based EWS committees, serve as Local Resource

#### Target
- 60% of women, marginalized groups, and PWIDs in priority areas demonstrate an understanding of climate hazards.
- At least 40% representation of these groups from vulnerable areas lead and participate in community-based EWS committees.

#### GEDSI outcome indicators
- % of women, marginalized groups, Indigenous Peoples (IPs), and other vulnerable areas take on leadership roles in community-based EWS committees, are LRP, and have access to CDD/R resources.

#### Timeline
- Year 1 - 2

#### Responsible
- Executing entity with support from GESI Expert and Mentored Ministries/Local Government, Local CSOs/CBOs, LRP partners.

#### Estimated budget exclusively for GEDSI (USD)
- $50,000 +30,000

# Annex 1: Examples of organisations with GEDSI sensitive climate resilient best practices

| Name of organisation | Expertise and best practices |
|---|---|
| AEPIC (https://www.aepic.gov.np) | Alternative energy technologies – e.g. tested and highly subsidized alternative energy technologies such as improved cook stoves, biogas, solar etc. |
| HELVETAS Nepal (https://www.helvetas.org/en/switzerland/what-do-where-we-work/partner-countries/nepal) & ICIMOD (https://www.icimod.org/) | Alternative Technologies – water harvesting; climate smart agriculture etc. |
| IFAD (https://ifad.org/) | Alternative Technologies – water harvesting, climate smart agriculture etc. |
| HIFRAPID- USAID (https://www.hifrapid.org/documents/654016/656716c-003a-42b8-b3f7-c51375b953) | FINANCIAL Literacy (package) for highly marginalized groups; Microfinance, Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs); Good Vegetable Farming; Cash Crops etc.; Seed funds for the very poor. AIMA-FAO Nepal (https://www.fao.org/3/i9052en/i9052en.pdf) | Alternative Technologies – water harvesting, climate smart agriculture etc. |
| SEEDS Nepal (https://seedsnepal.org/) | Home Gardening; Climate Smart Agriculture |
| MEDEPMEFDA (http://medep.moeica.gov.np/) | Trainings with skilled human resources/networks at local level – on small and micro-enterprises |
| NTNC (https://ntnc.org.np/), Hanyabon WWF-USDA (https://www.hifrapid.org/hanyabonprogram) | Water harvesting, bio-fuel and alternative energy and agriculture technologies and practices. |
| Pakisthan Agriculture Center, Dhankuta | For seeds in horticultural, horticulture and livestock technologies, approaches and seeds. |
| PANI Project/USAID (https://www.usaid.gov/pakistan-stories/pani-project) | Water Conservation technologies, practices |

# Executive Summary

On the request of the Government of Nepal (GN), a five-year project proposal on 'Protecting Livelihoods and Assets at Risk from Climate Change Induced Flooding in Glacial River Basins of Nepal' is being formulated by UNDP Nepal in collaboration with Ministry of Finance with support from several other partners such as MoH, MoWRD, MoEF/DefSC, and DNPWC for submission to the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

In this regard, a GCF proposal on glacial lake outburst flood (GLOF) and flood risk management on protecting livelihoods and assets at risk from climate change induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal is being prepared by bringing on board a technical team, organizing consultations with key stakeholders including government agencies, development partners, indigenous peoples (IPI), local communities and networks, women groups, youth groups, people with disabilities, marginalized groups especially those affected by disaster related events, flooding and climate change disasters in the selected basins. An Inventory of Glacial Lakes in the Koshi, Gandaki and Karnali River basins of Nepal has been prepared with technical support from GIZ.

In preparation for this proposal preparation, two consultation reports have been prepared: 'Indigenous Peoples' consultation to understand potential glacial lake outburst floods risks for safeguarding lives and livelihoods in the three river basins of Nepal (March 2016)' and 'Consultation report to understand potential glacial lake outburst floods risks for safeguarding lives and livelihoods in the three river basins of Nepal' (March 2016).

The detailed scope of the project being formulated will vary in each watershed or district or municipality or wards in the three basins (Koshi, Gandaki and Karnali). Tentative interventions envisioned are: Lake Lowering (selected lakes), Monitoring Stations for Hydro/meteorite data/information; Early Warning System; and Community Based Climate Risk Management Initiatives. The project will be implemented with a gender equality perspective.

In the context of the GCF, a Gender Equality, Disability and Social Inclusion (GESI) Assessment and Action Plan were to be prepared to have a well-articulated gender and inclusion components that build the gender-responsive and inclusive resilience building interventions. In this context of changing climate into a gender action plan, outlines a detailed gender-responsiveness-based management approach for GESI in the project. The Gender Equality, Disability and Social Inclusion (GESI) assessments were carried out on: overall differential impact of climate change and disaster risks and existing coping mechanisms and adaptive capacity on men and women in general; overall differential impact of climate change and disaster risks and existing coping mechanisms and adaptive capacity on people with disabilities; overall differential impact of the climate resilient development interventions and community-based adaptation measures on the lives and livelihood of men and women, with particular emphasis on most vulnerable groups such as indigenous peoples, youth, women, children, elderly, etc. The findings are being translated into gender and inclusion responsive planning and building resilience at the community level post-disaster. The project will be implemented in a manner that ensures mainstreaming gender and inclusion into climate and disaster risk management interventions; and the traditional/indigenous knowledge, if any, that makes them resilient to the climate change.

The analytical framework for the assessment took into consideration the critical GESI related barriers and opportunities in climate change induced disaster risks such as GLOF identified from the Terms of Reference.


Page 4 of 51


---
**Page 158**
---

Literature reviews of various studies in Nepal and elsewhere such as in Pakistan2 and Bhutan.3 A two-pronged approach was adopted to collect and analyze the data at the: (i) institutional level (organisational structure, policies, strategies, etc.) and (ii) individual level dimensions: a) Historical discrimination/exclusion and b) Situational vulnerability dimensions.

This framework guided the data collection, analysis, report writing, and development of the GEDI Action Plan.

The Kosi River Basin was selected for the field level primary data collection. In total 30 key informant interviews (KIIs) and 8 focus group discussions (FGDs) participated by 148 people were conducted in Nepal. The data collected from these sources were triangulated with sharing the findings of the literature review and the field work was organized solely considering on the GESI assessment report and generate inputs for the action plan; and b) validation workshop to finalize the GESI assessment report and the action plan.

Key findings

The findings from desk reviews reveal that though there has been a trend observed in managing GEDI data post the 2015 Earthquake in Nepal, there is a lack of GEDI disaggregated data not only in terms of GLOF but in general climate change induced disasters.

Most studies indicate that occur every year high number of human lives and properties in Nepal after the Annual Monsoon. The findings from the field studies also indicate that women related to GLOF indicate that after fire, floods and landslides cause the highest loss of lives in Nepal. The findings further indicate that women are among the most vulnerable affected by Nepal's earthquake, lack of gender sensitivity in post-disaster relief, improved understanding of the existing flood early warning system, and the need for a more participatory approach to disaster preparedness and response to the needs of vulnerable groups, and poor & highly marginalized women in particular. In the case of different impacts of floods or landslides socially, though there is a lack of documentation on this issue, the Nepal National Federation of Disabled - Nepal (NFD) also claims that people with disabilities are the most vulnerable in disasters including CC induced disaster like GLOF.

Findings from field studies

Community level findings

1. Who are the most vulnerable to Climate Change Induced Disasters? Findings from the field studies in the Kosi basin areas which can be generalised as applicable to other basins of the project indicated that poor Tarai and Hill districts who are both historically excluded and situationally vulnerable are considered to be the most excluded and vulnerable people following the earthquake, highly marginalized indigenous people (Adhikari Janaajati).

1. 10 July - June 2017 Gender Analysis FPGD Scaling up of Glacial Lake Outburst Flood (GLOF) risk reduction in Northern Pakistan: LKNP ID 34407
2. Chabahar, Tashkent, 28 September 2017: Gender Assessment, FPUSD, Bhutan for life, Bhutan, WFP, 8/18/04, Green Climate Fund
3. Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of Nepal, June 2019. Nepal Disaster Report 2019


---
**Page 159**
---

particularly living along the river beds such as Maphi, Kumal, Botes, etc. The poor from the indigenous peoples: such as the Rais, Bhotes living in drought and disaster prone and remote areas are more vulnerable to violence and further marginalization. They lack access to disaster and development resources and services. Within these different marginalized groups, their women, single women, elderly, persons with disabilities (PWD), pregnant and lactating mothers, children, and people living with HIV/AIDS are the most vulnerable in both man-made and natural disasters. The other climate and natural disaster related vulnerable groups are the poor women, elderly people, people living with disabilities across all caste and ethnic groups, Dalits, indigenous peoples, persons with disabilities, and sexual minorities.

2. Women's "Time Poverty" has been since long identified, as an issue but is still not recognized as a critical area for intervention in the context of development and climate change. The changing climate and increases in the magnitude and frequency of natural disasters have led to increased migration from rural areas to urban areas, particularly among women and increased male outmigration. This has substantially increased poor and marginalized women's already high household workload and drudgery, food insecurity and loss of income generating opportunities. Women's time poverty is also reflected in their lack of bargaining and social positions or status (leadership) and decision-making in society also seem to be declining, as women do not have the time to invest in their personal growth and development. Due to this shortage of labour force, situation of single women is even more critical.

3. Poor and historically excluded women and men particularly living in geographically remote and disaster prone areas, mainly between 25 to 50 meters from the glacial lakes have limited access to disaster risk reduction (DRR) services and resources and opportunities. The poor outreach mechanisms, exclusive and centralized planning processes, lack of gender equality and empowerment of women and marginalized groups, together with the domination by local politics and power have made accessing and benefiting from these resources challenging. For example even if the local groups are strong, ultimately the politics and power decides and authorises on who should be selected or represented or whose skills are recognized etc.

4. The participation of women and men in local development planning, disaster risk reduction (DRR) processes and climate change related activities and decision-making is highly exclusive and gendered. Generally women from urban areas with economic literacy, literacy and ability plays a major role on who participates, where or which level they participate and how they participate.

5. Though the local planing process is required to include participation of and consultation with all marginalized groups such as the women, Dalit and indigenous peoples , knowledge, experiences and roles of poor and marginalized women, particularly indigenous and Dalit women are not recognized and taken into account by the local political representatives and decision-making decision-making authorities. Their roles and contributions thus remain largely invisible.

6. Disaster response mechanisms often fail to address the specific needs of women, children, marginalized caste groups, indigenous peoples, persons with disabilities, and sexual minorities, leaving them more vulnerable to violence and further marginalization. The

Page 6 of 51


---
**Page 160**
---

capacity of disaster-related government and frontline workers to effectively incorporate gender equality, disability and social inclusion (GESI) concerns into their recovery, response and rehabilitation efforts is hampered by or exacerbated by displacement/disruption of family structures, and the breakdown of community protections create conditions where these vulnerable groups are at an increased risk of sexual violence, exploitation and abuse. The lack of access to basic services and livelihood security in disaster settings further expose these groups to abuse, while social stigma and discrimination hinder survivors, particularly poor women and highly marginalized groups, from accessing support.

### Institutional level findings

7. The planning and decision-making processes seem to be largely controlled by the local politics and power, side lining the participatory and inclusive processes and structures established at higher levels of government.

8. The impact of changing climate together with the transition from the past governance system to the new federal system has brought both opportunities and challenges in women's leadership and empowerment processes. A large number of women have come into positions of authority and responsibility, but their ability to influence disaster decisions has been affected due to reasons such as decrease in dedicated human resources and sector/units, one-door policy for development interventions, and near absence of active representation platforms in the provincial and national level for women.

9. There seem to be a lack of awareness at national and local level institutions about the need to address GESI. However, knowledge and skills on social methods and approaches, dedicated resources and investments on how to integrate GESI in design and planning cycle is limited.

10. The stakeholders and beneficiaries, particularly in disaster risk related planning, committees and networks are largely considered as a monolithic group, with the notion that disasters and hazards do not discriminate people. The historical exclusion and situational vulnerabilities of the poor women and marginalized groups are not specifically considered, which has made it difficult for them to compete for resources with the advantaged groups.

### Recommendations

1. Establish a working definition of the primary stakeholders (women, marginalized and highly groups, and vulnerable groups) for the project area, by contextualizing and categorizing according to their different and overlapping exclusion, vulnerabilities, roles/contributions and intersectionality. For example: Historically Excluded - Situationally Vulnerable

Climate Vulnerable = Most/Highly Vulnerable Group.

2. Identify and promote tested women-friendly and climate smart technologies and practices both at household and farm levels, by developing partnership with specialized government non-governmental institutions/local resource persons, cooperatives, preferably women-led cooperatives, and other stakeholders in a participatory and time-constrained manner.
3. Strengthen or form new women and marginalized people’s groups (e.g., women, IPs and highly marginalized IPs, Dalits, PWDs), and networks at local/community levels, and organize them to participate, practice leadership, market forest/agricultural/eco-tourism products, and access climate-smart technologies and services for their project and local development resources. Ensure systematic and formal linkages of these groups with the local wards and municipalities and DRR/CC related committees’ planning structures.
4. Increase poor and marginalized women and men’s income and employability by tapping and linking with those institutions with experience, and with Value Chains (VCs), local cooperatives, and other stakeholders in a participatory manner. Use existing models that are already tested, established and successful in the Koshi Basin areas (e.g., link with MEDPA* and Agriculture Research Centre (ARC)-Pahriban’s and their groups and networks.
5. Ensure timely, transparent/complete, and easy to understand communication and information sharing among all stakeholders and key players at the national and local government’s financial and technical services related to development, disasters and climate change.
6. Support the Monitoring Information System (MIS) of the project to capture gender and inclusion data and pilot the same in selected villages to upscale it. GEIS-related challenges and learning should feed back into the project’s annual planning and budgeting process, and disseminated through publications and other knowledge-sharing events at international, national, provincial or community levels.
7. Develop a simple working GEDSI (Gender Equality Strategy or a guideline for the project to ensure that all aspects of GEIS are covered in the project cycle and pilot the same in selected ward(s) to upscale it. The working strategy could be based on the findings and recommendations from the baseline study conducted by UNDP for the preparation of the proposal, such as the GEASI assessment (2019), Indigenous peoples’ baseline study (2020) and the GoN’s Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GEIS) Strategy for different sectors (Forestry, Agriculture, Ministry of Local Development).

*Poor and Marginalized people / groups should particularly include: historically marginalized groups e.g., Kumals, Mahis, Bote, hill Dalits etc.), situationally vulnerable such as PWDs, elders, pregnant women, economically poor families.  
**MEDPA (Micro-enterprise Development Programme for Poverty Alleviation). MEDPA is the government programme that provides micro-credit and other services to the poor in Nepal through a network of 1000+ service providers in Nepal through the micro-enterprise development and employment generation. MEDPA mobilizes Micro Enterprise Development Service Providers (MEDSP) who provide micro-enterprises, training, credit, and technical assistance to the beneficiaries. These service development facilitators (EDF) are the pillar of this programme who provide MED services in the beneficiary’s level.

1. **Background**

A five-year project proposal on "Protecting Livelihoods and Assets at Risk from Climate Change Induced Flooding in Glacial River Basins of Nepal" is being formulated by UNDP Nepal in collaboration with Ministry of Forests – the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Green Climate Fund (GCF). The project is supported by the Department of Water Supply, Irrigation Resources, Energy and Irrigation. UNDP as an Accredited Entity (AE) and the NDA was officially requested by the Government of Nepal to assist in preparing the project proposal. Department of Forests and Soil Conservation (DFSC), Department of National Park and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC) and other relevant ministries and departments are to also provide support in preparing and implementing the project.

In this regard, a project proposal has been submitted to GCF on glacial lake outburst flood (GLOF) risk reduction in three river basins of Nepal. The project is aimed at addressing climate change induced flooding in glacial river basins of Nepal. A technical team is working under the guidance of UNDP and GCF Project Formulation Guiding Committee, led by Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM), DFSC, DNPWC, Ministry of Water Supply, Irrigation, Energy and Environment (MoWSEE), MoF, CDMO, NGOs, Private Sectors, local government, indigenous peoples (IPOs), local communities, women groups, youth groups, persons with disabilities and other groups directly affected by glacial/ice lake outburst floods in the three river basins of Nepal.

The detailed dam breach modelling of 47 glacial lakes (from Nepal and neighbouring countries) have been also carried out. In addition, two consultations have been conducted on "Indigenous Peoples Consultation" (February 2018) and "Community Consultation" for safeguarding lives and livelihoods (February 2018)" and NGOs, CDMO, Private Sectors and Government consultation to understand potential glacial lake outburst floods risks for safeguarding lives and livelihoods. (March 2018).

Similarly, Inventory of Glacial Lakes in the Koshi, Gandaki and Terai River Basins of Nepal has been prepared by UNDP with technical support from CDMO. The report outlines, out of 47 potentially dangerous lakes (PDGL), 42 lakes are in the Koshi basin, 3 are in the Gandaki basin and 2 are in the Karnali basin. In addition, UNDP has undertaken dam breach modelling of the selected PDGLs to assess the potential flood risk from glacial lake outburst flooding among the likely impacted areas. The project formulation has also developed a risk model, which calculates the socio-economic impact of the glacial flooding for all selected PDGLs.

The detailed scope of the project will vary in each watershed/district/municipality/ward in three basins of Koshi, Gandaki and Karnali. Tentative interventions envisaged are: GLOF Risk Reduction such as Lake Lowering (selected lakes); Monitoring Stations for Hydro-met/Climate data/information; Early Warning System; and Community Based Climate Risk Management Initiatives including food mitigation/risk reduction and climate resilient livelihood promotion activities.

The GCF recognizes the importance of gender considerations in terms of project impacts and access to climate funding, taking a gender-sensitive approach and encouraging investment of relevant stakeholders in addressing gender inequalities and addressing gender gaps. In this regard, further to the process formulation UNDP sought inputs of a GESI Expert to ensure that the project has well-articulated gender and inclusion components which build the

| Page 10 of 53 |


---
**Page 164**
---

gender-responsive adaptation measures, outlines a detailed gender-responsive results-based management and plan to communicate the gender goals and results adequately.

In order to input to the preparation of the gender and inclusion components, assessments were carried out of:

- Overall differential impact of climate change and disaster risk and existing coping mechanisms and adaptive capacity on men and women in general; children, elderly, persons with disabilities, the vulnerable and marginalized groups;
- Impact of the climate resilient development interventions and community-based adaptation measures on the lives and livelihoods of men and women and with particular emphasis of most vulnerable and marginalized groups;
- Role of women and marginalized groups in contributing towards gender and inclusion responsive planning and building resilience at the community level; d) policy gaps that need to be addressed for ensuring gender equality, mainstreaming climate and inclusion into climate and disaster risk management interventions; and
- Of the traditional/indigenous knowledge if any that makes them resilient to the climate change.

### 2. Approaches and methodology

#### 2.1 Analytical framework

The analytical framework for the assessment took into consideration some of the critical GESI related barriers and opportunities in GLoF and Climate Change identified from literature review of existing frameworks such as “Chhara” and Bhutan”. Within this context, the following parameters of the analytical frameworks guided the data collection, analysis, report writing, and development of the GESI Action Plan.

A two-pronged approach was adopted to collect and analyze the data.

**A. Institutional level (supply side):** At the institutional level, the focus was on understanding the capacities (GESI-DGLOF/CDC induced disaster related knowledge, skills), commitments and accountability for gender equality and inclusion in the context of climate change and various CC induced disaster and GLOF risk reduction and management - related institutions viz. government and non-government institutions at federal, district and local levels and local private service providers and local committees.

1. **Chhara**, Tshering, September 2017. Gender Assessment, EPFOB: Bhutan for My, Bhutan, WWF, 8.18/04. Green Climate Fund.


---
**Page 165**
---

B. Community level (demand side): At the community level, the focus was on assessing the engagement/participation, access to, and control over resources and decision-making of women in relation to climate change issues. These were assessed using two dimensions: (i) engagement) and access were assessed in five livelihoods dimensions viz. social capital, financial capital, human capital, physical capital, and natural capital. The analysis also included an assessment of the material conditions that influence the roles women play in society. Examples include the material conditions related to the roles women out men play in society; increasing income from forest products; improving technical knowledge and skills in forest management; etc. Material conditions do not address the subordinate position of women in the society.

Social Problems and Inequalities: Social problems and inequalities are the issues that women face in society, it is a response to the inequalities in decision-making position and long-term benefits (Moser, C., 1989). Activities that address social problems and inequalities include increasing women’s access to resources; providing training and leadership roles by women and vulnerable groups; ensuring equal opportunities in forest employment, gaining rights over natural resources etc.

Historical exclusion - based on gender, caste, ethnicity, LGBT language or location that is transmitted from generation to generation. Women who are historically excluded may be vulnerable but not necessarily excluded – based on age (elderly), PWV, HIV, Children, earthquake affected etc.

# Data collection and analysis methods

## Consultative and participatory approaches were used to engage a wide and inclusive range of cross-sector stakeholders in the design and implementation of the assessments.

### TECHNICAL DIMENSION

- **Policy and institutional framework**
  - The guiding policy/ Acts and its operationalization in action plans, strategies, approaches, and M&E systems for gender and climate change induced disaster risk reduction and management.
  - The number of staff and the requirements and resources needed to work, such as job descriptions, training procedures, information and coordinating systems for gender and climate change induced disaster risk reduction and management
- **Organizational framework**
  - The people were positioned and the way they are allocated and related to each other in terms of procedures, information and coordinating systems for gender and climate change induced disaster risk reduction and management

### Expertise

- The number of staff and the requirements and resources needed to work, such as job descriptions, training procedures, information and coordinating systems for gender and climate change induced disaster risk reduction and management

| POLITICAL DIMENSION | Policy Influence | Decision Making | Room for Innovation |
|---|---|---|---|
| The way and extent management, people from within the organization and people outside of the organization influence policy-making within the organization | The patterns of formal and informal decision making processes. The way that conflicts are dealt with | The space and incentives available to staff to give input on work, such as rewards, career possibilities, variety in working styles |
| CULTURAL DIMENSION | Norms/Values | Cooperation | Attitudes |
| The symbols, rituals and traditions. The norms and values that govern the running of the organization and the behavior of the staff. The social and economic standards set | The way the work relations between staff with each other and with others outside of the organization such as working in teams, networking. The norms and values around these arrangements | The way staff feels and thinks about their work, their environment and other (external) employees. The extent to which staff stereotype themselves or others according to which staff identifies him/herself with the culture of the organization |

The field site for data collection was in the Koishi river basin area. Some reasons for selecting and proposing the Koishi river basin were because as per the CMAD, out of 44 PDGLs, 42 lakes were in the Koishi glacial lakes; the project’s all three key interventions (Livelihoods, Early Warning System and Monitoring Stations) were concentrated in the Koishi basin. The other two reasons for selecting this site was because it is the largest river basin in the Gundaisho river basin and UNDP project can avoid duplication. The specific sites for data collection within the Koishi basin were selected in consultation with UNDP and the government of Ethiopia. The selection criteria used to select these sites within the Koishi basin were: 1) Up to 100 kilometres downstream from the critical glacial lakes and other critical areas downstream, where it is possible to capture information from diverse groups of people that are critically affected.

### Desk reviews

Desk reviews of various documents were carried out, which broadly included the following.
- Concept Note (submitted to GCF)
- GES strategies of various ministries
- Government’s National Climate Change Policy 2019
- Approved GCF-Gender Assessment and Action Plans of other countries such as India, Bhutan, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Tajikistan, Cambodia, Kenya, Guatemala and Zimbabwe.
- Updated Gender Strategy (2020 - 2033)
- Gender Policy for Green Climate Fund (Annex XIII);
- GIZ/UN Women’s practical manual to support the integration of gender equality in climate change interventions and climate finance.

## 2.4 Field Data Collection

Following steps were followed to conduct the field data collection.

* **Preparatory/consultative meetings:** were held with UNDP Co-Nepal, their government partners, UNDP Asia Regional Office and with the proposal development team members (through Skype and face-to-face meeting). This resulted in an analysis on the analytical framework for the study, key informant interviews, focus group discussions, fieldwork sites and stakeholders for federal, district and local level institutions and community level interviews.

* **Data collection:** was done through key informant interviews and Focus Group Discussions (FGDs) with GESI-related personnel/members, partners and community people (government, NGOs, federations/networks/groups, diverse community people at federal, district and local community levels. See Annex 1 for the checklist used.
A total of 204 respondents were interviewed in the fieldwork sites. The gender-wise distribution of the KIIDs/IDs was: 19% (male); 11% (female); 11% (Brahman/ Chettri); 3 (5%) Dalits, 18 (50%) Janajatis and 13 (4%) Others. And in the FGDs and sharing workshops the gender-wise distribution of participants was: 27% (male); 69% (female) and 4% (Others). The inclusion wise - Brahman/Chettri were 44 (21%), Dalit 31 (15%), Janajati 100 (48%) and Others 28 (13%). The consultant developed the final GESI assessment report based on the analysis of the information and knowledge stemming from the desk reviews, field data collections, workshops and other consultations. The finalization of the report was based on the following deliverables:

1) **First Draft Report**: A detailed draft report was drafted for reviews by the consultant.
