3. Findings from desk reviews

### 3.1. Status of women in Nepal

With the Human Development Index (HDI) ranking of 147, Nepal falls in middle human development group, with the Gender Development Index value of 0.897. The HDI of women is 0.524. Under-five mortality rate at birth is 71.9 years. Estimated gross national income per capita of women is US$ 213.15

According to the Central Bureau of Statistics, Government of Nepal[6], of the total national population of 26,494,504 woman's population was 51.5% in the national census 2011.

Socio-economic wise, the female literacy rate in 2011 was 57.4 % and it increased to 62% in 2014. Of the economically active population 45.5% were women in 2011. In total in formal and informal employment, 38.9% of women worked in agriculture, forestry and fishing sectors respectively; 48.3% and 15.7% women were employed. Health wise, according to the demographic health survey 2016[7], percentage of women who were pregnant when they were 18 years or younger was 9.5%. The proportion of women who had their first child before age 18 years was 34.5%. Percentage of women who had at least one antenatal care visit in the last pregnancy were respectively 52.6% and 23.7% respectively. Access to antenatal care (more than four visits) increased from 50.9% in 2012 to 84.5% in 2016. The proportion of women who received institutional delivery from 55.2% in 2014 to 57.4 in 2016. Gender violence cases reported in 15-sample service centres indicated increase from 1308 in 2012 to 6,230 in 2015. Similarly, of the 280 cases of reported human trafficking 97.1% were women.

As far as social and economic empowerment of women is concerned women headed 27.4% households in Nepal 2011. Households, owners with ownership on fixed assets such as house and land was only 10.8%. In other words, percentage of women without ownership of house and land was 93.5%. Women's participation in the National Assembly was 17.4%; in the Constituent Assembly it was 12.5%; in Public Service Commission was 14.3%; in National Election Commission was 33.3%; and in the Commission for Investigation of Abuse of Authority it was 14.3%. Overall women participation in civil service is 11% and in police force is 8%.

According to UN Women[8] of Nepal, 2015, a total of 72.7% of parliament seats are held by women in Nepal; work still needs to be done in Nepal to achieve gender equality. The proportion of women aged 20-24 years old who were married or in union before age 18 was 36%. The proportion of women aged 15–19 years who had first sexual relation as of 2015, up from 71 per 1000 population in 2013. In 2016, 11% of women aged 14-19 years reported that they had been subject to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months. Moreover, women of

reproductive age (aged 15–49 years) often face barriers with respect to their sexual and reproductive health and rights: despite progress, the proportion of women who have their first child before they are 20 years old is still high at 36%. In Nepal, only 31.5% of Indicators needed to monitor the SDGs from a gender perspective are available, with gaps in key areas such as Unpaid Care and Domestic Work, Lack of Access to Information and Communication Technology (ICT). In addition, many areas such as gender and poverty, women’s access to assets including land, physical and sexual harassment, and general lack of awareness about their rights remain unaddressed. The need for comprehensive and periodic monitoring: Addressing these gender data gaps is a prerequisite for achieving the SDGs related to women and girls in Nepal and for achieving the gender-related SDG commitments.

### 3.2 Gender and inclusion disaggregated data on climate change induced disaster

There is a dearth of quantitative data on vulnerabilities to climate change and its impacts on climate change induced disasters. However, qualitative studies like the National Climate Change Impact Survey 2016[1] have reported that households are observing different climate induced disasters including flooding, landslides, droughts, hailstorms, and disease/insects in last 25 years. The most common disaster types were floods (47.9%), followed by disease/insects (39.7%), landslide (36.1%), and hailstorm (30%). The least common disaster types of disasters in last 25 years. The results depicted that 38.3% of households were impacted by wind wave induced by landslides (35.2%), fire settlements) (31.8%), and flood (31.7%).

To understand and assess the differential gender and social group impacts of the Climate Change Induced Disasters such as GLOF, with the dearth of direct data on the impact of climate change induced disasters, it can be best discussed in terms of the data available from other disaster types like floods and landslides. The vulnerability to climate change in Nepal or vulnerabilities to climate change can be most coherently discussed in terms of the data available on disasters like floods and landslides that occur every year cause highest loss of human lives and properties in Nepal after earthquakes. It is however to noted that climate change is not always the cause of GLOF. In a recent paper published by Akbar Khan et al.,[2] it was reported that 10% of GLOFs were induced by a rockfall. For details see link https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10346-018-1079-6

It is only after the Nepal Earthquake 2015 times in disaggregating disaster loss data gender and inclusion wise is observed. Post Earthquake the Government of Nepal reported that of the 8,891 people who died 55% were women (0.031%, national population). UN Women estimated that 47% of those affected by the earthquake were women. The total number of displaced persons 327,000 female-headed households [26% of households], 40,000 women with disabilities, 185,000 over the age of 65 years (9% of population), 764,000 girls aged 14 and above (28% of females in this age group) and 300,000 young girls under 14 years old (29% of female population). The Post Disaster Needs Assessment (PDNA) also recognized that women are among the most vulnerable of those affected by Nepal's earthquake.

Besides the post-earthquake GLOF disaster-affected people data from Community Flood Project and UN Women, there is no other NFDA and UN Women, there has been no longitudinal or quantitative study that can provide data or evidences with regard to GLOF and its differential impacts on gender and social group wise in Nepal.

One of the three studies in preparation of the GCF project commissioned to ICIMOD by UNDP has produced an inventory of the PDGL with estimates based on the population census of 2011 which estimates that the number of people likely to be affected by a potential GLOF varies between 10,000 in Sun Kosh and 19,000 in Dudh Kosh. The remaining population could suffer a loss of 45% of its assets. The total number of households directly affected would be indirectly affected through infrastructural damage and loss of goods and services ranges from 51,200 for Tama Koshi to 63,500 for Sun Kosh. ### 3.3 Disaster, gender and inclusion

According to a joint study by London School of Economics and Political Science, University of Essex and Mac-Plean Institute of Economics on disaster impacts, gender, disasters lower women’s life expectancy more than men’s. The study found that the gap between male and female life expectancy in Nepal increased from 14 years in 1981 to 2000. Women, boys and girls are 14 times more likely than men to die during a disaster.* It is to be noted in the context of Nepal also, during the 2008 Kosh Floods in Eastern Nepal, women were found to be more vulnerable as compared to men. Discussions found that children, women, elderly and the ultra poor were most affected by the flood and in immediate need of assistance.

Following a disaster, it is more likely that women will be victims of domestic and sexual violence, especially if they are displaced for fear for being sexually assaulted. There is enough evidence that in any disasters, disproportionately large numbers of women particularly from poor and socially excluded groups are affected more severely relative to men. Worldwide, it has generally been found that women are more vulnerable than men to the impacts of disasters, especially natural disasters. Women are also more likely to be displaced by disasters, especially in both development and disaster contexts; they perpetuate and in many cases augment existing inequalities between men and women. In addition, women are disproportionately affected by disasters because they are likely to suffer higher rates of mortality, morbidity and post-disaster ruin to their livelihoods. Several underlying factors exacerbate women’s vulnerability to the impacts of disasters, including poverty, lack of access to resources, gender inequality in society, and often sexist and discriminatory social, cultural and legal practices. However, women are also key agents of change. Their unique knowledge is essential to ensure the effectiveness and sustainability of disaster adaptation. Hence, their full and effective participation is essential.*

Besides women/girls, persons with disabilities (PWDs) are also more vulnerable in CC Induced *ICIMOD Report submitted to UNDP, May 2018. **Global Gender Gap Report 2017, World Economic Forum, University of Essex and Mac-Plean Institute of Economics - The Gendered Nature of Natural Disasters: The Impact of Catastrophic Events on the Gender Gap in Life Expectancy, June 2017 (http://epubs.lse.ac.uk/36581/3/Gender_nature_of_natural_disasters_SESR0929.pdf) ***The Multi-Agency Flood Impact Assessment Koshi River Flood – Nepal 2008. disasters (such as GLOF) due to their impairment, existing barriers and their socio-economic situation. On the other hand, disaster creates impairment/disability. PDW tends to be invisible in disasters, especially for people with pre-existing impairments. Not including PWDs in DDR means putting new barriers for them and not letting them participate in development processes. Disaster risk reduction needs to consider the intersectionality of disability and gender. Women are disproportionately affected by disasters, such as economic poverty, social, cultural and political exclusion (intersectionality). Women must be empowered to meaningfully participate in decision-making processes related to disaster risk reduction.

An ICIMOD Working Paper[1] published in 2014 identifies there is a lack of gender sensitivity in post-disaster relief. In the study, Koshi Vishwa Society, Saptari district, Koshi basin area in Nepal, an organization working on disaster relief and rehabilitation, had drawn attention to the post-disaster vulnerability of women. Women are disproportionately affected by disasters, such as economic poverty, social, cultural and political exclusion (intersectionality). Women in post-disaster scenarios are vulnerable to rape and other forms of violence and can even be forced into prostitution. The study also highlighted that women's needs were not met. In post-disaster relief work, the practical and medical needs of women including pregnant and lactating women, such as for safe toilets, a clean environment, access to health services, and accessible food distribution points are often ignored.

In another Flood Early Warning Systems in Nepal study from gendered perspective by ICIMOD[2], it was found that it had improved understanding of the existing flood early warning systems in Nepal. It suggested ways to make early warning systems more effective and responsive to local needs. The study highlighted that women's voices are often absent on a wide variety of development issues and livelihood challenges that communities face on a day-to-day basis, the study suggested it is important to tune early warning systems according to the local context. The study also highlighted that women's voices should be included in disaster risk reduction with the active involvement of local men and women as part of the development process. Early warning systems should be seen as a social and development activity rather than an exclusive domain of engineers and scientists.

This simplification and democratization of early warning systems requires bridging the gap between technical departments and communities, building local capacity, recognizing the stake of the local community in contributing to and benefiting from early warning systems processes, and most importantly, involving women as active agents in disaster risk reduction efforts for a 'one time disaster'. The early warning systems should be used with advanced applications to disseminate messages that will also be useful for local livelihood needs, such as daily weather trends to support crop-related decisions, market related decisions, and storage and transport related decisions.

Further, it is important to recognize that women play an active role in family livelihood security and efforts must be made to involve women and men equally in creating and receiving early

### 3.4 Importance of gender and inclusion in disaster

The importance of integrating GESI in climate change induced disaster is recognized nationally and internationally.

International agreements/treaty commitments, such as Sendai Framework for DRR 2015 - 2030 (SFDRR), Paris Agreement and Agenda 2030 Sustainable Development Goals all uphold the importance of gender equality and inclusion. The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030, specifically in its Strategic Priority 4 on Enhancing disaster preparedness for effective response and to “Build Back Better” in recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction, specifically mentions "Empowering women and persons with disabilities to participate effectively at all levels of decision-making" as a key strategy. The Framework also highlights the importance of ensuring that disaster risk reduction measures are gender-sensitive and culturally accessible so that recovery, rehabilitation and reconstruction approaches are key.52

Aligned to the international context, nationally in Nepal importance of GESI is mentioned in disaster, climate change related national plans and policies in Nepal such as National Climate Change Policy 2019 and Local Government Operational Act 2017. Most of the directly disaster related plans and polices in Nepal such as the National Strategy for Disaster Risk Management (NSDRM) 2015, District Disaster Risk Management Guidelines 2012, District Disaster Management Planning Guidelines 2012 and National Disaster Response Framework 2013 (updated 2019) also mention the importance of integration of GESI in disaster risk management. However, a list of samples of international and national examples from GESI perspectives related to climate change and disasters.

However, there is limited understanding and analysis of what the linkages are between GESI and climate change induced disaster and how to integrate GESI in disaster and climate risk management (DRM).


---
**Page 177**
---

3.5 National gender and inclusion policies/plans/DRM reports

Regarding national gender and inclusion policies and practices in general for Nepal, the Constitution of Nepal, 2015, guarantees a rights-based approach regarding gender equality and inclusion. The constitution stipulates a non-discrimination clause that reinforces the equality of all citizens before law and ensures equal opportunities for women and men to participate equally in the advancement of economically poor, socially or culturally backward social groups. Women are guaranteed the right to participate in all state mechanisms. Dalits have specific rights for participation in all state mechanisms as well.

In ensuring the constitutional rights, the Civil Service Act 2007 has legalized the principle of positive discrimination by ensuring reservation for certain sections of the community in the civil service. In the 14th Three Year Plan (2016/2018/2019), the Govt emphasized the meaningful participation of all citizens for Nepal’s inclusive development and aims to improve the human development indicators, reduce poverty and inequality, and ensure that no one is left behind. The Budget for 2018 incorporates issues regarding gender, poverty and marginalization under objectives and priorities. The federal government had allocated Rs. 135.1 billion in the budget of FY 2074/2075 to improve human development indicators at all levels to spend on human development indicators. Provisions for capital, skill and entrepreneurship training, and financial inclusion are made for women’s empowerment and increasing the ownership/proportionality of women. The 13th Three Year Plan (2019/2022/2024), recently released, acknowledges that though progress has been made since the last plan, inequality and exclusion have not been eliminated in Nepal. It recognizes the need to address gender equality issues through women and women managed cooperatives. The strategy for long-term vision mentions that criteria for socially progressive economic arrangements will be made to ensure women, Dalits, and other marginalized groups are included.

Numerous GoN policies and guidelines recognize the need to address GESI issues programmatically and institutionally in order to achieve sector objectives. All these sectoral GESI strategies and guidelines emphasize participation of women, Dalits/Advanjatias, Madhesis, and other marginalized groups in all sectors. They also mention the importance of monitoring and evaluation of sectoral policies, plans and programs. They recognize the need to identify the specific barriers faced by women, the poor, the vulnerable and the excluded in the sector concerned.

Regarding legislations or guidelines to promote GESI in local governance bodies the Ministry of Federal Affairs and General Administration (MoFAGA) governs local bodies, including the municipalities and has developed a GESI policy in 2017 that provides directives for incorporating gender equality into all activities. Similarly, the community development sections of the municipalities are responsible for facilitating GESI mainstreaming in planning, budgeting, and monitoring. Similarly, the Disadvantaged Group (DG) mapping at the ward level is done by the municipality and verified annually by the set committee.

The legal framework for local governance elaborated in the Local Governance Operational Act 2017, requires local bodies to prepare pre-panic plans – prioritizing women, marginalized groups, poor and children through participatory planning processes – and describe mandatory provisions for the participation of women, children and disadvantaged groups in the planning


---
**Page 178**
---

# and budgeting process. The local government regulation ensures 20% women and at least one Dalit member representation in all level elected bodies. The Minimum Conditions and Performance Standards (MCPS) 2009 require that all disaster risk reduction (DRR) plans be gender-sensitive and inclusive planning including pro-poor budgets to ensure the flow of central grant. Gender Responsive Budgeting (GRB) [since 2007/08] requires all line ministries to follow the guideline and provide a gender budget report. The percentage of total government budget that was gender responsive was 21% in FY 2015/16.

Recently promulgated Government of Nepal's National Policy For Disaster Risk Reduction 2018[37] regarding gender equality and inclusion ensures access, representation and meaningful participation of women and Dalit communities in all levels of disaster risk reduction. It also economically and socially marginalized communities in all steps and structures of disaster risk reduction based on inclusive disaster management concept. Further, as per the concept of disaster risk reduction, it provides that women and Dalits will be given priority for schools, health institutions, community buildings and shelters etc.) will be made senior citizen, gender persons with disability and children friendly. It further provisions an inclusive mechanism to establish a monitoring and evaluation framework effective monitoring and evaluation of the activities related to disaster risk reduction and management.

## 3.6 GCF gender frameworks and policies

The GCF has a standard framework for developing a gender equality and inclusion component of a country program and its associated project proposals. The GCF also has a set of Fund's gender frameworks, policies and action plans. In this regard the GCF's Gender Policy and Action Plan (March 2015) states that:

- Women, as well as men significantly contribute to combating climate change. Shifting the paradigm from adaptation vision and climate-resilient development pathways, which is the Fund’s mandate, requires a large number of individual and collective decisions by women and men. A gender-sensitive approach is therefore part of a paradigm shift;
- Climate change impacts women and men differently, to the detriment of women, and existing gender inequalities are likely to be exacerbated by climate change; and
- Gender inequality, exacerbated by climate change, is linked as other development areas to poverty, conflict, and environmental degradation. The GCF has identified key items from gender norms and discrimination that result in the imbalanced division of labour, lower incomes, and lesser livelihood opportunities, less access and control over land and natural resources, fewer legal rights, lesser mobility and lesser political and professional representation.

The GCF's commitment to gender equality centres on gender-responsive country programs and initiatives that benefit everyone, women and men. This requires gender analysis to understand the social, economic, cultural and environmental factors that contribute to gender inequalities, and the potential contributions of women and men to mitigating and adapting to climate change, and building climate resilience. It further entails adopting methods and tools to promote gender equality and reduce gender disparities in climate funding by mainstreaming

### 3.7 Reports on consultations for project formulation

In line with the GCF environmental and social policy that elaborates the commitment of GCF to integrate environmental and social issues into its decision-making and outcomes, and as part of engagement of various stakeholders during project formulation, Nepal Federation of Indigenous Nationalities (NEFIN) undertook a detailed Indigenous Peoples’ engagement study. The study was conducted in 2018 with the objective of understanding the climate change concerns of indigenous peoples (IPs). From this sector includes impacts of climate change on agriculture and livestock, water resources, forests, and biodiversity. Many IPs still practice subsistence farming. Agriculture is the life-line for their livelihoods. Unfortunately, indigenous peoples are encountering challenges in continuing their traditional livelihoods and practices as a result of changing and cutting is facing problems posed by the growing changes in climatic conditions.

In forest and biodiversity, indigenous peoples are nature worshippers. Indigenous peoples’ livelihood and their cosmo vision are intrinsically embedded with forests, flora and fauna, and with the natural resources that they provide. The relationship between IPs and the land goes beyond sustenance of their lives and livelihood to a more emotional, cultural and essential unified accord with nature. The intensity of changing environment, including climate change and droughts are threatening indigenous peoples’ lands and forests among other natural resources.

Regarding impact of climate change on water resources, for indigenous people water is sacred. It is vital to life, essential to agriculture and a valuable energy source. Indigenous peoples value water in terms of its spiritual significance as well as its practical use. Water has both its consumptive value and its use in general, but it means more than a natural resource. Indigenous people’s relationship with water and its resources are crucial to cultural vitality and resilience. The impacts of climate change on water resources are significant and GGRD are related with the Goal/Ancestors. Over the years, with changes in climatic conditions indigenous peoples are facing water scarcity in their villages; apart from their traditional sources of water, they have also been dependent on bore wells and dug wells for meeting up drying it. It is important to note that indigenous peoples have their own ways and practices of identifying natural hazards. It is important to respect and utilize the knowledge of indigenous peoples when developing climate change adaptation strategies.

Consultation with the NGO/CSOs, Private Sectors and Government informed that the stakeholders are aware of the issues on climate change induced disasters such as and GLOF, but have uneven potential risks from the GLOF; stakeholders have willingness to contribute, ensuring that they are involved in the project formulation. The need for capacity development program; and stakeholders need scientific knowledge, information management, and monitoring.

# Findings from fieldwork

## Community level findings

### Who are the most vulnerable to Climate Change Induced Disasters and hazards?

Poor Tarai and Hill Dalits who are both historically excluded and situationally vulnerable are the most affected by climate change-induced disasters and hazards and considered to be in the most disadvantaged position. They belong to the lowest strata of society, being marginalized indigenous groups, particularly living along the river banks and drought prone areas (Advisa/Janajatis) such as Majhis, Kumals, Botes etc.

- **Tarai and Hill Dalits:** Majority of Hill and Tarai Dalits residing in remote areas and degraded lands are among the most vulnerable. They have limited access to resources such as education, employment and other socio-economic opportunities and services, making them socially, economically, politically and culturally the most discriminated. As compared to the other indigenous groups, they are considered to be the least empowered (Advisa/Janajatis) are considered to be the most discriminated and vulnerable. These multiple layers of discrimination have affected both their material conditions and social status in society leading them to be among the most marginalized and excluded.

- **Poor and highly marginalized Advisa Janajatis:** Similar to the Hill and Tarai Dalits, poor and highly marginalized Advisa Janajatis such as Majhis, Kumals, Botes etc. living near and along the river banks, in landslide, flood and drought prone areas, seem to be among the most vulnerable. These groups are also considered to be the least empowered. They were found to be living in clusters usually in the outskirts or far from the main villages making it difficult for them to participate and easily access disaster and development resources and opportunities.

- **Water availability both for drinking and potting:** Water is our biggest problem and it has become very severe lately. We have no water source other than the river. The only water source from the neighboring village, but we could not get this water as we live far from the main village and it requires extra costs and labor.” - A Kumal man, Hattawgad 6, Bhigpur

- **Poor Advisa Janajatis:** Indigenous People from the hill and mountain areas, particularly living in disaster prone and remote areas such as the Ratos, Botes etc. due to the geographical location of their settlements are among the most marginalized and excluded. They have limited access to resources such as education, employment and other socio-economic opportunities and services making them socially, economically, politically and culturally the most discriminated. As compared to the other indigenous groups they are considered to be the least empowered. These multiple layers of discrimination have affected both their material conditions and social status in society leading them to be among the most marginalized and excluded.

Most of the interventions such as trainings, community initiatives and networking. Due to their limited physical and social assets climate change and disaster risks have further exacerbated their conditions thus increasing their vulnerability.


"When the Koshi barrage was flooded help came from the government and several organizations. Truckloads of soaps, mattresses and food were provided. The agencies handed over these supplies to the village leaders for distribution but these leaders distributed these supplies among themselves. They did not distribute them equally. Only 20 people got a mattress but was given only 2. Similarly they budgeted and requested for 75 feet deep tube well for a building which is only a 25 feet deep well.” - A single woman, Sardar community, Kuchha, Ward No.4, Bhigpur


---
**Page 182**
---

information, infrastructure development etc., were centered on the ward headquarters and they could not participate nor access it easily.

* **Women:** As compared to men, women across all social and economic groups seem to be more excluded from accessing climate change and development resources and opportunities. Women are particularly vulnerable in rural areas, especially among the poor single women. Tarai Dalit women (as described above) living in remote and disaster prone areas followed by Hill Dalit women (e.g., Gaddarwa), poor and marginalized indigenous communities such as Tharu, Khasi, and other small ethnic minority groups (Chetri, Brahmins).

* **Single women, elderly persons with disability and pregnant and lactating women:** These groups, particularly women have problems related to getting timely and complete information on climate change. The lack of awareness among these groups to understand and discuss their special needs and contributions is almost non-existent. Hence, they usually tend to get excluded from participating in meetings, consultations, trainings, and group activities such as the ward level planning and budgeting meetings, trainings or exposure visits etc.

2. **Women’s “Time Poverty” is still not recognized as a critical issue and addressed in the context of development and climate change.**

* The changing climate and increases in the magnitude and frequency of natural disasters has caused problems in water availability, agriculture, forest resources and increased male outmigration from rural areas. This has led to an increase in women’s work load, high household workload, food security and loss of income. The progress made by women in terms of their material conditions (well-being) and social positions or status (leadership, decision-making power etc.) is being reversed due to climate change. Due to the shortage of labour force, situation of single women is even more critical.

* **Water scarcity has hit poor and marginalized women the hardest:** The increasingly unpredictable rainfall patterns, which are more intense and shorter in duration, and the long periods of drought have led to a decrease in water availability for small farmers and herders. This has increased women’s problems. This has increased women’s need for managing water for household use, irrigation and conserving water sources.

* Since the past 11-12 years, availability of water has been one of the most pressing issues for us women. The spring water in our ward has dried and the “bulldoor road” and earthquake has made it even worse. We have to buy water from the nearby village. We have to collect rupes (approximately USD 1700) and gave 100 days of labour from each household to buy and bring the piped water from the neighbouring village. We did not get any support from the ward office. The women’s group has been trying to solve this problem by collecting funds from Kumal (marginalized) and some Dalit households living in the periphery of the main village could not be included in this scheme as it was taken additional resources to join for the piped water. Besides they cannot afford to pay for the water – Chundarbari Rani, Hothnegpal & Bhupur

* **Hardening of soil in farms has doubled women’s farm work:** In agriculture too the soil quality is becoming degraded and hardened, making it difficult to break and fertilize. The


---
**Page 183**
---

hardened soils have doubled the time for ploughing, requiring more labour, water and manure, making women work for longer hours in the hot sun.

*The long spells of hot weather make the soil very hard and requires additional breaking even after ploughing to make it suitable for sowing. In our village we have a system of compensatory labour where men are paid by women to help them with their tasks, thereby reducing the number of days of labour to the plougher, which is usually done by women. This means our workload is further increased. These soils also need more water, manure and long hours of physical exertion to make them suitable for sowing.*

Maya Rai, Hattawagad 6, Pipal Dada, Bhujpur

* Increase and emergence of aggressive pests and further increasing women’s time poverty and food insecurity: Farming of vegetables, crops and horticulture has become very difficult due to the increase in number of pests and diseases as well as weeds and pests. It has affected farms and storage, increasing women's workload, as they are the ones responsible for these tasks. Since these new and aggressive varieties of pests and weeds have no natural predators, the use of chemical pesticides is not an option. In realms of traditional knowledge, their local methods and approaches are not effective in addressing the problem. Women found to be using their hands to sprinkle on strong-smelling herbicides did not notice that they were inhaling the dangerous dust.

* The unpredictable weather patterns have caused huge loss los in the village. In Hattawagad this year 85% of their maize got destroyed with untimely rainfall followed by drought after the plantations. This resulted in having maize with no seeds and increase in sucking insects like aphids.

* Decrease in forest products due to droughts, forest fires causing increased workloads of women: Long spells of dry weather and increase in invasive species have degraded the quality of forests causing frequent forest fires, and reduction in the yield of fodder, fuel wood and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs). This has also contributed in increasing women's time in collection of the forest products, as these are almost exclusively women’s traditional work.

* Male outmigration: The trend of male outmigration seemed to have increased due to the decrease in agricultural production and food insecurity, also increasing women’s workload. In our village, many young men left for bigger cities where they could earn more money leaving young wives to leave the village to the nearby cities to educate their children living behind elderly parents and further increasing the workloads for the women who are left behind.

* Decrease in women's mobility and engagement in income generating community activities and decision-making processes: Due to increased workload, women’s mobility, participation in community initiatives, committees, decision-making processes, and social gatherings have decreased. Women are not able to participate as their communities are in their doorsteps; they do not have the time and interests to grab these opportunities. As traditionally, the caring or household work has been exclusively women's responsibility, women are required to finish their household duties and only then can they participate.

3. Poor and highly marginalized women and men particularly living in geographically remote and disaster prone areas**,** mainly between 25 to 50 kilometres from the glacial lakes have limited access, and benefits the least from development and climate change related resources and opportunities.

- **Near absence of platforms and invited spaces** for reaching, mobilizing and engaging women and marginalized groups: The poor outreach mechanisms, exclusive and competitive selection processes, and lack of representation of women and men living in remote areas make it difficult for these groups to organize and empower poor women and marginalized groups, together with the domination by local politics and power have made accessing and benefiting from these resources and opportunities very challenging. These are some of the reasons why women and men living in remote areas are not able to participate in local politics and power decisions and authorizations on who should be selected or represented or whose skills are recognized etc. For example women’s groups, farmers groups, ward citizens groups, NRC groups, and other groups that are formed (far away from the glacial lakes) has made it difficult for these groups to organize and collectively voice their opinions, advices, and make demands. The absence of these dedicated gender sensitive spaces and platforms such as Women Development Office, wards social mobilizers are felt by the women elected members and staffs of the wards and rural municipalities.

*Our women used to get a lot of support from the Women Development Office and social mobilizers in terms of organizing themselves and accessing resources. However, women have not been able to organize themselves and hence we miss out on mobilizing resources and funds. In the first year no women did not submit proposals to spend the small budget (NRS 800,000) allocated for women’s groups. This is because of the lack of information and budget heads. Similarly, last year too we did not receive any proposal. I feel presence of a separate local level institution that can provide support in terms of mobilizing resources and social issues is very important.* Women ward member, Num, Ward S, Mikalo Rural Municipality.

- The poor roads, scattered nature of the settlements, and limited human resources and budgets have forced these local level development organizations to limit its outreach around the ward or municipality headquarters. This automatically excludes the poor and the marginalized groups from accessing information, participating in meetings and training opportunities, representing in local level decision making committees etc. Even if they participate, they are unable to fully reap the benefits.

For example, computer training for youths (2 months) and tailoring training (3 months) for Dalit women were organised by the ward office in Num, in its headquarters of Ward S, Mikalo Rural Municipality. The training opportunities were for all the women, however due to the long duration of the training and lack of transport facilities (especially for the poor and women workers) could not participate. They could not afford the time and costs of leaving their village to stay in Num bazar. Further, most of the women and girls from the remote and distant wards had not


---
**Page 185**
---

received information about these trainings and neither were they consulted during the planning of the activities for them.

* Majority of the saving and credit groups, cooperatives and micro-finance institutions seem to be regularly meeting but these meetings are strictly limited to transacting money and paying interest. The majority of the women in these groups are active. Active cooperatives were found to be contributing to the local livelihoods and income-generating activities. Further, the unclear steps and mechanisms of the local government to engage with the communities and involve them in planning for their communities and address their needs, interests and priorities in designing and planning climate change and disaster risk reduction related plans and programs, have made inclusion even more challenging.

### 4. The participation of women and men in local development planning, disaster risk management and climate change-related activities and decision-making are highly exclusive and gendered.

* The gender, political affiliation, geographic location, caste, ethnicity, age, economic status, literacy and ability of the local women and men play a major role on who participates where or which level they participate and how they participate. The types and quality of participation can be categorized as follows (also, see Table 3):

| | |
|---|---|
| **Majority of poor and highly marginalized women**, particularly from remote and disaster-prone locations are not members of local interest groups or decision-making bodies or committees at the village level. They have been excluded due to their low economic status and other historical discrimination that makes them invisible when it comes to resource distribution and benefit sharing. They also do have no confidence and networks to mobilize themselves for participation in such activities. Similarly, women from Dalit and marginalized men (elderly, Dalits, marginalized indigenous men — Kumars, Boteh, Mails etc.) are engaged in community activities that entails voluntary participation or consultations or labor contribution.
| |

* Women in the leadership positions are members of key committees, but they are usually engaged and responsible for women-centred programmes, and committees e.g. health, agriculture (seeds distribution), conflict resolution — violence against women etc. They do not have any say on other development activities that are not women-centric. Women-centred activities do not require large financial transactions. Further, in most cases strong women leaders were found to be excluded from the key political and local developmental committees involved in planning and decision-making processes as they were perceived as a threat to the local power centres. A trend of selecting or nominating women with limited exposure and experience in leadership was observed, making their participation more token with no influence in the planning processes and decisions.

* **Mostly Advantaged Men** are engaged in the activities and committees related to large and small infrastructure development and technical activities. Only very few women

Page 32 of 51


---
**Page 186**
---

leaders were found to be engaged in these domains. During the entire fieldwork, only one woman ward vice-chair from Num, Makalu was actively engaged in the ward’s planning processes.

*Women like us, who are poor, make demands and question the forums are not the preferred list by the local administrators and their technical committees (such as road, irrigation, electricity). We are side-lined and obviously our issues and proposals receive least or no priority. If you assess, it's the same all over the region except in some exceptional cases.” – a women leader, Khadbari, Southwestern Nepal*

• Decision-making still remains men’s domain. Advantaged Men occupy almost all key leadership positions, and likewise decisions related to local level planning and disaster risk reduction are also made by them.

*It is really sad to say that most of the current elected or nominated women members44 and women leaders at the local levels are mere rubber stamps to the decisions made and the need to turn this problem into an opportunity is crucial.” – Member, HMAWANT*, Central Committee*

### 5. Knowledge, experiences and roles of poor and highly marginalized women, particularly indigenous and Tarai Dalit Women are not recognized and their contributions remain largely invisible.

- Poor and highly marginalized women, particularly those indigenous women from the mountainous high hills such as the Rais, Bhotes, Botes, Majhi's, Kumaun and Dalit women from the Koshi Tappu areas such as the Sardars, Bantars, Khatives, Musharas are highly dependent on the natural resources and have good knowledge and experience in climate change adaptation. Their knowledge and experiences and their roles in climate change and natural disaster management are not recognized and their contributions largely remain invisible in local policy making.


---
**Page 188**
---

planning and financing. Their experiences are seldom used to draw knowledge of local situation and ecology. Hence, they are unable to fully engage in, benefit from, and contribute in more meaningful, recognized and paid activities and opportunities.

*During the devastating Kashi flood in 2008 we had to move our cattle and grains to a safer place. Our entire group was affected by this disaster. We lost all of our livestock, water pots, drums tied under the beds. This saved many lives and assets of our people. It took 2-3 days for the external agencies to get the boats from India and the cities and we were the last ones to receive it. The women in our group used their knowledge of traditional medicine, herbal remedies, for diarrhea, worming off stomachs, headaches etc which are very useful during such times. We also had a lot of news that they asked us for our support. They don’t trust us and use other members of the community when it comes to providing information for distribution of goods and services.* — Sardar Women (Tarai Dalit) from Kashi Tappu

### 4.2 Institutional level findings

6. The planning and decision-making processes seem to be largely controlled by the local political powers, side lining the participatory and inclusive processes and structures established and promoted by the government and development agencies.

* Local politics and power structures[1] play a major role in deciding which groups or institutions, leaders or individuals should be given authority and recognition to decide on who or where to invest in, what information, govern on how and who to select for representation. This is especially true when it comes to the interests of women and men who are not part of these networks and their needs, interests and priorities are often ignored. Women and Dalits are also underrepresented particularly during disasters where they tend to get excluded. In the majority of the cases in the Koshi basin areas, ward chairman and secretary largely make the prioritization of issues, allocation of funds and other resources. The women’s group has only 3 key members in the ward committee; the other two members who are elected and nominated from the women and Dalits quota do not have much say or power to influence decisions. The fact that the women’s group is a voluntary group provides spaces for the local people to participate, is also not systematically and effectively followed. See table 4 for the trends in ward level budget allocation.

*We do inform the whole village to gather for the tale (cluster) planning meetings but only few people come. We don’t have enough resources to convince the poor women and Dalits to participate. They do not submit their demands and we end up planning for them. And later, they also cannot develop proposals for implementing their approved plans. Last year your budget got frozen because you did not use this budget for Teej celebration.* — Tole committee member, Kumal Gom, Tulsinghar

[1] Musahars, Sardar, Banters, Khawas: (Tarai Dalit) community organizes themselves in groups to collect funds from the village (usually from rich people) to use it for praying and during disasters. These groups seem to get together with other groups of similar communities to form a larger group.

*Power Structures: power structures in the local context are mainly – politics and political networks (ruling party), caste/ethnicity (dominant caste/ethnic group), gender (men and women from which groups) wealth, networks, education etc.

Although we knew the households which need land for rehabilitation, in the last assessment, we could not get the names of those households. Those who registered their names for resettling to school dana does not have Numb since they have political affiliations and connections, we cannot easily remove them.

Village social worker, Almora, Bhojpur, Makalu

7. The impact of changing climate together with the transition from the past governance system to the new federal system has brought both opportunities and challenges in women's leadership and empowerment processes. A large number of women have come


---
**Page 190**
---

into leadership positions. But due to reasons such as decrease in dedicated human resources and sectors, one-door policy for development interventions, and near absence of a strong women's network, women have been marginalized and are not able to influence decisions.
• In the previous governance system, at the village development committee levels (VDCs), there were about 45 members to run a VDC. However, in the new federal system, a ward is almost of the same size as VDC, which has only 5 key members, of which 2 are from the women's group. This means that women have less representation and influence in the planning and decision processes. Here, the chairperson and his two-team members make all decisions on behalf of the entire community. The need for external interventions and interactions by the communities to the outside agencies or sectors has to be made through the ward members. This one-door policy has largely affected women's access to resources and services. The merging of district level line agencies with the local government has also largely affected women leaders and groups from directly contacting and interacting with the line agencies.
• Nowadays it is difficult to get a packet of seeds and information on new pests directly from agriculture office. We have to go through ward members to get their support. Our ward members are usually busy handling large projects and do not find it important to deal with our small problems. The only way for us to get help in the absence of any other mechanism (Village Development Committees) is it was easy for us. Our farmers group could directly visit these offices (referring to DAD) to get services.” Women: farmer, Farmers Group, Janayatpur, Bhilour
B. There seem to be awareness among the national and local level institutions about the need to address gender equity and inclusion of GESI. However, knowledge and skills or capacity building for this is very limited.
• Knowledge and skills on participatory methods and approaches, gender and social analysis, planning and gender integration of the national and provincial institutions such as the climate change and disaster risk reduction related sectoral ministries, provincial and local line agencies, and other development partners (NGOs, INGOs, CBOs, CSOs) and local CSOs are very limited. Investments in the form of dedicated human resources; budget and GESI capacity building seem to be a non-priority. This gap has contributed in promoting and capturing of the development and disaster related resources and opportunities.
• For example, the agricultural research centers in the field with heavy focus on extension services, do not dedicate staffs with expertise on gender and social analysis to ensure that the new technologies and innovations are GESI sensitive. In PARC, the criteria for selection of demonstration sites and caretaker-farmers to test and promote improved technologies, varietal seeds, fruit trees, livestock etc., are based on interest in technology, land availability, farmer's experience, etc. However, women's progress to participate in trainings and meetings; having land to test etc. Poor women and


---
**Page 191**
---

marginalized groups do not fall in these criteria and hence majority of the caretaker- farmers are advantaged men.

• The poor investments on developing appropriate and GESI responsive extension materials and approaches have also largely affected in making these services ineffective, particularly for women and marginalized groups. The use of technical and non-local language in delivering the extension services seem to be an issue for them.


**"Three years ago there was a meeting on forest management and the participants were mostly women as most of the men in our village are abroad. Most of us could not understand what sr (resource person) was trying to tell us. The poor people like us, we mentioned that the meeting on forest management - but women (P), Besi, Pachauri, Moktan**


• Besides the issues related to capacities, the local level institutions do not have structures and mechanisms for integrating GESI in their planning cycle, making it difficult for them to find spaces to plan and implement. Before federal local level administration was introduced, there were women development organizations (WDO) with dedicated staffs and planning system and budget at the local district level. The WDO is now merged with the municipality with only one staff and the separated planning system and budget are not available.


**9\. The stakeholders and beneficiaries, particularly in remote /disaster risk reduction/management related planning, committees and activities are largely considered as a monolithic group. The historical exclusion of the poor women and highly marginalized groups from these spaces has made it difficult for them to compete for resources with the advantaged groups.**

• The local level disaster risk management committees and district disaster management committees largely do not categorize its stakeholders from gender, age, ethnicity, socio-economic status or geographic location during disasters. This has contributed in excluding the poor, socially and geographically marginalized women and men from disaster response, and rehabilitation support.


*Disaster does not discriminate between the rich and the poor and hence we do not have special provisions for the poor and marginalized during landslide rescue and response. The door is always closed to us - Member, District Disaster Management Committee, Khadubri Sankhwaasabi*

# 5. Key Recommendations

1. **Establish a working definition of the primary stakeholders (women, marginalized, highly marginalized and vulnerable groups) for the project area**, by contextualizing and categorising according to their different and overlapping exclusions, vulnerabilities, roles and contributions and intersectionality from the following perspectives:
   - Historical/Structural Exclusion – against certain groups based on gender, caste, ethnicity, origin, religion, etc. (e.g., Dalits, Adivasis such as Sardars, Doms, Mushahars etc.; marginalized ethnic/indigenous peoples such as the Botees, Mahis, Kumaonis living along the river beds; Rais/Bhotes from remote and disaster and drought prone locations etc.)
   - Situational Vulnerability – due to climate change and disaster risks – particularly women and children in disaster-prone, remote and climate affected locations and who are disabled, elderly, children and single women.
   - Economically poor households (ranging from food security for 3 months to 6 months/year).

2. **Women and men (Contributors) who are directly engaged in the day-to-day management of farms, and natural resources**, but whose contributions and knowledge largely remain invisible.

3. **Identify and promote tested women-friendly and climate smart technologies and practices both at household and farm levels by developing partnership with specialized government departments/organizations/local resource persons to reduce poor and marginalized women’s time poverty**.
   - The focus of the technologies and practices could be on water recharge and management, for e.g., recharge spring water, micro-irrigation, multiple-use water system, solar pumps etc. promoting women-friendly farming technologies (e.g., drip irrigation, precision agriculture), cooking energy (e.g., GEF/SGP, CRT, Practical Action, PEEDA); women-friendly farming technologies; fodder improvement; pest and weeds/invasive species management, storage of agricultural produce, use of improved seeds and varieties for income generation; development of commercial pockets etc. (e.g., Rural Municipalities/Wards, ARC/Fabrikum/NACC, URBID, CEARFAD, NRI, IDE etc.).

4. **Strengthen existing or form new women and marginalized groups’ people’s (e.g., Women, IPs, Dalits, Adivasis) organizations** to ensure their participation in the project through leadership, market forces/ agricultural/ tourism products, influence planning and decision-making processes, mobilize both project and local development resources. Ensure that women and marginalized groups are represented on all local wards and municipalities and DRR/CC related committees’ planning structures.
   - Ensure that poor and marginalized women and men who are not included nor part of any committees are the main members of these groups. Identify local women leaders and champions, particularly from among the marginalized groups, and provide them with leadership and advocacy training and regular mentorship/coaching.


---
**Page 193**
---

1. Ensure that the GESI related groups and networks allow both women and men to learn about GESI and the importance of women and marginalized group's engagement in DRR and climate change risk-based decision-making and access to financial and technical resources to improve their livelihoods.
2. Develop technical knowledge and skills of women and marginalized groups to ensure their meaningful engagement and to be able to implement community based Glover, EWS etc.
3. Ensure that local government’s budgets are also allocated for GESI related activities including GESI responsive infrastructure such as safe houses for shelter during disasters; meeting halls etc.
4. Increase poor and marginalized women and men's income and employability by tapping and working with those institutions with experience, and with Value Chains (VCs) and Micro-enterprises (MEIs) that are already tested, established and successful in the Koshi Basin areas.
5. For example, poultry, livestock, horticulture, vegetable seeds, crops etc. promoted by ARIC- Pahribhakas could be supported by the project through: the provision of seed money or facilitation for access to credit/procurement support; link them with organic friendly homestay; link them with micro-finance organisations and cooperatives, learn from MEDEPA, USAID’s KSBAN-II, Hanzy Ban Project etc.
6. Ensure timely, transparent/complete, and easy to understand communication and information sharing between the project and affiliated groups on the project's local government's financial and technical resources.
7. For example, information related to opportunities such as training, planning meeting; technical information (subsidies, loans, business/entrepreneurial skills, CC induced DRR, EWS, agriculture related etc.).
8. Support the Monitoring Information System (MIS) of the project to capture gendered outcomes and lessons learned in selected wards/municipality to upscale it. Gender-related challenges and learning should feed back into the project's annual planning process, and disseminated through publications and other knowledge-sharing events at national and community levels.
9. For example, by developing a knowledge management strategy plan on GESI and Climate Change Risk Based Decision Making, and which can be tied with the Project’s Knowledge Management/communication efforts as well as to monitoring and evaluation processes.
10. Develop a simple working GIS in Climate Change induced disaster risk reduction and management strategy or a guideline for the project to ensure systematic integration of GESI in the project cycle and pilot the same in selected wards/municipality to upscale it.
11. The working strategy could be based on the findings and recommendations of the various studies, including the UNDP’s Gender Equality Strategy (2019), Indigenous People's consultation report, baseline study (2020) and the GoN’s GESI Strategy for different sectors (Forestry, Agriculture, Ministry of Local Development).


---
**Page 194**
---

Working GESI strategy/guideline could include the following:

- Develop a working definition of the local community and primary stakeholders in the context of disasters and climate change (as explained in Recommendation 1 above). This definition should be used to identify and include representatives of the primary stakeholders for participation in all phases of project design, implementation, monitoring/reporting and benefit sharing for all project related programs and activities. Select interested/potential wards or municipality to pilot it.

- Designate mandatory quota for women and the marginalized groups. For example, at least 30% of the members of the Community Development Committee (CDDs) or Village Dalits) for their inclusion across all project activities, for example in key decision-making, membership, consultation and awareness raising, technicians, local resource persons, recipients of training, beneficiaries of infrastructure development related equipment, training, materials, funds etc.

- Systematically integrate GESI in operations and project cycle. The Working GESI strategy/guideline should provide a clear direction and resources to systematically integrate GESI into all phases of the project cycle management (design, planning, implementation and M&E).

- The projects and its partners' senior management and project supervisors should be held accountable. Terms of Reference (ToR) and appraisal systems should incorporate responsibilities for social and gender results in planning and delivery. Designation of GESI focal person at all levels.

- Provision to ensure Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) in decision-making. The FPIC is to establish bottom-up participation and consultation of indigenous population prior to the beginning of development on ancestral land or using resources within the indigenous community's territory. Indigenous communities have a special connection with their land and resources, and they inhabit 20% of the earth’s surface. These areas are environmentally rich in both renewable and non-renewable resources. The collective ownership of these lands is often not recognized by governments, which has impacted its continuous need for resources and land. To protect indigenous peoples rights, international human right laws have created processes and standards to safeguard their way of life and ensure participation in the decision-making process.

- Use of local languages in extension, communication, and information and respect of customary laws and practices.


8. Develop a capacity building package with adequate resources, for the project's staffs and its key and direct partners to enhance GESI integration.
* Strengthen knowledge and skills of local level resource persons, technicians and managers of both government and non-governmental institutions (e.g. Municipalities, Wards, civil society organizations, local resource persons, media) to develop, implement, and monitor GESI responsive local plans.


Page 41 of 51


---
**Page 195**
---

- The focus of the capacity development package should be on strengthening their:
a) participatory skills and methods (GESI analysis, vulnerability assessment from GESI perspective, etc.), including M&E; b) understanding the linkages between GESI and DRR/Climate Change; c) leadership, Organizational Assessment (including accountability, creating enabling environment and cooperation for GESI issues); etc.

- Develop and implement gender and social inclusion responsive materials, approaches and methods.

# Appendices

## Checklist for data collection

### Guiding Questions for Institutional Level Data Collection (for federal, district and local levels)

**GESI related structures and analysis capacities:**

- What are the existing gender capacities of the institution? (GESI/GLOF/EC analysis skills; knowledge of GESI/GLOF/EC issues; policies and approaches etc.)
- Is there a policy/strategy/guideline on GESI? If not, is there any interest/attempt to develop a GESI policy/strategy/guideline?
- What are the gender-related roles and responsibilities?
- Are there gender expertise and capacity building budgeted for?

### Commitment

- Are any changes made to make the M&E system more gender-friendly in terms of gender differentiation and in terms of operation? Do indicators exist to assess gender inequality and impact?
- Has gender been made a critical factor in the selection of activities and consultation processes? Are some of the marginalized groups consulted, and/or are their needs known and taken into account?
- What evidence is there of a commitment to the gender policy or gender mainstreaming program within the organization (management, different levels, board)? What kind of changes can be observed?
- Do you feel that the organization cooperates in promoting gender within the organization? Is so how?

### Guiding Questions for Community Level Data Collection

#### Material Condition Related Questions - household and community level

**Definition of marginalized groups:**

- Who are considered the marginalized groups from the GESI/GLOF perspective?
- Why?

#### Engagement and participation:

- What kind of GESI/GLOF related knowledge, skills and capacities do different groups have in the community? (Who does what? How? Where?) Who are the group-members? Who are not included in the groups? Why?
- What are the GESI/GLOF related needs and priorities of the different groups of women and men in the community?

#### Access:

- What are the GESI/GLOF related resources/information in the community? What are the external GESI/GLOF related interventions/resources that the community has access to these resources? Who? How does who? Why?
- Benefits (from what?)

- Who benefits the most from the forest-related resources and interventions and how? Who benefits the least and why?

#### Social Position Related Questions - household and community level

**Involvement in decision-making: representation**

- What is included in the decision-making processes and mechanisms at different levels of governance planning; LGMMP, LAPA, CAPA etc.?
- Who controls decisions? How? Where? Why?
- Who influences decisions? How? Where? Why?

#### Social Position Related Questions - household and community level

**Involvement in decision-making: representation**

- What is included in the decision-making processes and mechanisms at different levels of governance planning; LGMMP, LAPA, CAPA etc.?
- Who controls decisions? How? Where? Why?
- Who influences decisions? How? Where? Why?

#### Access to social position related resources/information:

- Who knows what? Who knows why (tells), and why?

# Annex 3: Sample of International and National Policies from GESI Perspectives

## International Policies

### The Universal Declaration of Human Rights
- Accords protection to the rights of women prohibits discrimination and accords equality before the law.

### The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
- Prohibits discrimination, and calls for equality of women and men's rights and equality before the law.

### The International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights
- Prohibits discrimination, and calls for equality of women and men regarding the rights recognized by the Covenant.

### Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW)
- Adopted in 1979 by the UN General Assembly, it is often described as an international bill of rights for women.
- Consisting of a preamble and 30 articles, it defines what constitutes discrimination against women and sets up an agenda for national action to end such discrimination.

### Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action and Beijing +10
- Protects the human rights of women, as defined by international human rights instruments, will only be realized when they are fully protected, guaranteed, applied, and effectively enforced at national legal and practical levels, in family, civil, criminal, labor and commercial law, and in administrative regulations (Paragraph 218).

### Gender and the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples
- The human rights of indigenous women are protected by all of the provisions of this UN Declaration, which also recognizes their specific needs and their rights and special needs, and calls upon States to take measures to ensure that indigenous women enjoy full protection against discrimination, violence and discrimination.

### The Hyogo Framework for Action (HFA)
- It is the first plan to explain, describe and detail the work that is required from different sectors and actors in order to reduce loss. It was developed and agreed on with the many partners needed to reduce disaster risk - governments, international agencies, disaster-prone countries, civil society organizations.
- The HFA provides a common system of coordination. The HFA outlines five priorities for action: reducing disaster risks through integrated policies; building resilience of nations and communities to disasters. This means reducing loss of lives and social


---
**Page 205**
---

| UN Convention on Rights of People with Disabilities (UNCRPD) | economic, and environmental assets when hazards strike |
|---|---|
| Adopted in 2007, aims to promote, protect and ensure the full and equal enjoyment of all human rights and fundamental freedoms by all persons with disabilities, and to promote respect for their inherent dignity. |

### National Policies and Strategies
#### Disaster Risk Management Act 2014 (2017)
- Have provisions that women at risk of disaster, children, senior citizens, Dalits, marginalized groups and communities are included in the planning and implementation of disaster risk reduction.
- Access to resources for the rehabilitation and reintegration of women, children, senior citizen, people with disabilities and the people from economically and socially marginalized groups is ensured. This includes all steps and structures of disaster risk reduction based on inclusive disaster management concept.

#### Disaster Risk Reduction National Policy 2018
- Advocates, recognizes and promotes the participation of women, children, senior citizen, people with disabilities and the people from economically and socially marginalized groups in all stages of disaster risk reduction.
- Implements a multi-sectoral approach to disaster risk reduction based on inclusive disaster management concept.

### Guidelines: Disaster Preparedness and Response Plan (DPRP) 2011
- Recognizes the different needs, capacities and contributions of women, girls, Dalits, marginalized groups and communities in preparedness and response planning.
- The assistance provided in emergencies is planned and implemented with the participation of people with disabilities (PWD), women, and other excluded groups; active participation in planning, implementation, monitoring and reporting.

### Guidelines: District Disaster Management Plan (DDMP) 2012
- Ensures participation of women socially excluded groups.
- The plan includes a gender-disaggregated analysis for disability and ethnicity/caste data.
- GISI analyses for District Profile.

### Guidelines: Local Disaster Risk Management Planning (LDRMP) 2012
- Implementation of LDRMP has been made mandatory for all local government bodies and aims to institutionalize disaster risk management work and mainstream gender equality at the district and local level. It ensures participation women, socially excluded groups, persons with disabilities at every stage of the development of Local Disaster Risk Management Plan.
- Mapping and documentation of VDC profile by age, disability and ethnicity/caste data.

### National Climate Change Policy 2019
- The policy has recognized the need to integrate GESI throughout all stages of climate change adaptation and mitigation programs", and as inter-thematic area with GESI related 6 key strategies and


---
**Page 206**
---

working policies. However, the policy is not informed by specific problems, challenges and opportunities faced by women and men in their everyday lives in the context of climate change. The differential impacts of climate change (CC) on different groups of women and men are not fully considered in the policy although the GESI policy (inter-thematic area), mentions about different vulnerable groups15 to be addressed, the section does not provide any specific measures for communities/citizens as a monolithic group and does not take into consideration gender, age, disability, situational vulnerabilities, and hence the differential impacts of CC on these groups. There is also no clear methodology or tool to measure the achievements in the end of the policy.

| National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to Climate Change 2010 | • Climate-induced disasters are one of the themes; GESI considerations are not fully considered in the analysis of climate change impacts on NAPA themes. However, the GESI analysis is not fully used to inform the plan and its activities. The gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) policy is not yet operationalized. |
| --- | --- |
| National Disaster Response Framework (NDRF) 2013 | • Disaster response activities (65 operational activities in total) are gender-neutral, except for the provision of arrangement at the camps and distribution sites for the protection of women, children, elderly, disable (47 activities). The NDRF does not provide any specific support for survivors particularly the vulnerable groups, and control gender base violence (54), DWDG; social support to unaccompanied minors (28); provision of shelter to those who have lost their supporting family members; and reunion of their families (30). The NDRF also does not mention about Emergency Response Preparedness; protection plan of the vulnerable groups and gender based violence (28), DWDG. |
| Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy of Ministry of Local Development 2010 | • Have provisions for GESI being mainstreamed in municipalities by trans-formatory social mobilization by creating a conducive environment for the establishment of Inclusive Ward Citizen Forums and Integrated Planning Committees supported by Social Mobilization Guidelines (SMG) and Community Based Organizations of MoSfAD; Social Units in Municipalities facilitate GESI mainstreaming through the implementation of the guidelines. Socially Excluded Groups: Women, Dalits, indigenous nationalities, Madhesis, Muslims, Children below the age of 16 years, senior citizens above the age of 60 |


---
**Page 207**
---

years, persons with disability and people living in geographically remote areas

Forest Sector GESI Strategy-2007
- Vision: Forests for Women and Soil Conservation is a gender and social equity sensitive and socially inclusive organization practicing good forest governance to ensure equitable access, utilization and decision making power over forest resources of all stakeholders.” Its Outcomes states: Favorable policy environment; institutional arrangements for conservation; Resource, services and benefits made available to the women, poor and excluded in a transparent manner; A gender responsive program budget and monitoring system instituted; Rights to equitable access and utilization ensured

Forest Sector Strategy (2016-2025)
- Gender equality is mainstreamed across forest sector organisations and institutions and focuses on increasing participation, competency and leadership of women, indigenous people, dalits, adivasis and other excluded groups and individuals. It promotes gender equity, inclusion and empowerment of the poor, women, dalits, janajatis, adibasi and other marginalised groups of people.

Agriculture Development Strategy (ADS) 2015 to 2035.
- The strategy has institutional mechanisms that have designed inclusion as one of the main component and has provisions for integrating GESI. Although it is not a gender specific or intentional mode of agriculture development, its institutional mechanisms, objectives and actions does provide for GESI mainstreaming. It ensures farmer’s participation in planning, decision making and implementation of agricultural extension of farmers (commercial, subsistence and landless), where the subsistence and landless farmers will be directly benefited from the strategy. The best effect is very strong in the case of the ADS measures related to gender, social, and geographical inclusion; land for landless people; promoting cooperative farming; reversing the trend towards degraded land.

Fifteen Plan (2076/77-2080/81) Approach Paper.
- The strategy states: Gender equity and social inclusion mainstreaming in forests, biodiversity and watershed management. Its Outcomes states: Favorable policy environment; implementation of GESI strategy (revision) in community based forest management modalities.
